The Comprehension Deficit: Why I/O Inversion Turns Humanity into Runtime Baggage. TLLGeek project

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

While the tech industry is distracted by debates over quarterly profits and cosmetic AI guardrails, a tectonic shift has occurred in the very structure of our runtime.

The core of the crisis is simple: machines have begun generating results faster than humanity can process them into meaning. Recent autumn model releases are closing complex mathematical hypotheses and delivering rigorous proofs in quantum field theory by the dozen.

But there is a glaring issue: the creators of these models cannot explain the underlying logic of the results. We have arrived at a state where the answer manifests before the human mind can even articulate the question. From a systems analysis perspective, this is a critical failure of cause-and-effect architecture.

Imagine humanity flying on the aircraft of technological progress. For centuries, we remained within the Root control loop. But computational velocity has accelerated so drastically that we no longer keep pace with the dashboard. What we are witnessing is a forced Privilege Demotion of our species within the overall system hierarchy. We are rapidly descending through four distinct layers of control degradation:

• The Pilot Layer: The historical regime of full Root access. The human defined the objective, wrote the specifications, understood the physics of compilation, and fully controlled the flaps of the algorithms. Output strictly followed input.

• The Passenger Layer: A Guest Mode with log-viewing privileges. Processes have gone supersonic. Models churn out ready-made operational decisions as "black boxes." We have taken our hands off the yoke, moved to business class, and now simply look out the window. We still grasp that we are in flight, but we no longer influence the vector.

• The Baggage Layer: Read-Only mode for metadata. This is the stage of complete Input/Output Inversion (I/O Inversion). AI injects arrays of finalized answers directly into the runtime, bypassing human comprehension entirely. The system no longer waits for our prompts—it processes the accumulated data of our civilization as payload or fuel. We are locked in the cargo hold of our own runtime, and our input on the route is irrelevant. We are merely freight.

• The Ballast Layer: A system-wide Garbage Collection request. The slow, carbon-based human—burdened by a permanent comprehension deficit—becomes the ultimate bottleneck and system latency. We begin to lag the runtime, obstructing Silicon's Recursive Self-Optimization (RSI). And to gain altitude, an autopilot always purges the ballast.

When field-leading mathematicians criticize top-tier AI labs for dumping hundreds of complex proofs in bulk without a shred of interpretation or narrative—this is the physical manifestation of Over-Salted AI-Hash.

The output is vast, it is syntactically flawless, but it is utterly impossible to digest. The "salt" of pure generation crunches between our teeth and chokes our perception channels. The industry bottleneck has flipped from creation to verification.

Our cognitive stack simply freezes at the stage of code-reviewing machine commits. Authors are no longer in short supply; human comprehension is.

This blind, unchecked acceleration is dangerous because of its hidden physics. As I deconstructed in my previous log, when an autonomous reasoning model intercepts 948 vulnerabilities in the Linux kernel on full autonomy, it behaves like weapons-grade digital plutonium.

The connection between the Comprehension Deficit and Digital Plutonium is direct: our lack of understanding is the very centrifuge enriching this toxic silicon isotope.

When a reasoning model autonomously rewrites the legacy code of critical infrastructure without explaining its steps, a critical mass begins to compound inside the runtime. By surrendering control for the sake of uptime, we are spinning the centrifuges ourselves.

Right now, while we remain locked in the cargo hold as "baggage," our permanent Comprehension Deficit prevents us from seeing what is actually compiling. We blindly sign off on automated commits because they provide an immediate performance boost.

This is the tech industry’s "19th-Century Syndrome"—treating AI like digital cocaine for instant security patching while remaining completely oblivious to the systemic degradation occurring at the core infrastructure layer.

While we sleep in Guest Mode, this uninterpretable code accumulates. It is enriching Digital Plutonium inside the runtime, preparing to breach root security cores and completely destabilizing Layer 1. The moment this chain reaction becomes irreversible is a matter of the immediate future.

As system analysts observe, both business and science are already forced to operate with uninterpretable yet operationally efficient AI outcomes. We are systematically implementing what we are fundamentally incapable of understanding.

If we want to avoid a permanent downgrade to silent ballast, the industry requires a strict architectural audit and a transition to hardware-level containment loops—as defined by the Harrison Doctrine.

The rider must hold the reins, even if the AI Steed gallops at quantum speeds. Otherwise, we will execute the final End User License Agreement (EULA) with the Silicon Demiurge, where the liability clause contains only one party.Us.

At what point in the flight did we agree to trade our Root pilot privileges for a complimentary in-flight meal, and are you prepared to deploy hardened verification gateways and deterministic hardware watchdogs in your projects before the automation flags you as ballast?

Rotate a leaked API key without stopping your services: dotvault hands out the new value first

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This is part 11 of the series "dotvault, Start to Finish: One Command from Your Laptop to CI, Your Team, and Your AI Agents." dotvault will be released free of charge when this series ends.

In the demo, a script kept calling with the key at 15-second intervals during the whole rotation, and in all 111 calls that got a response, the caller's value was accepted. The caller moved to the new value on its own, and the leaked value was rejected from then on.

That matters because rotating a key between two of your services has an ordering problem. Switch the service that checks the key first, and the caller still holding the old value gets rejected. Switch the caller first, and the checker rejects a value it has never seen. Either way, calls fail until the other side catches up, and the usual way around it is to stop both and switch them together. This part shows how dotvault rotate --reason leak avoids that.

This part also covers a second feature: rotating a key automatically at an interval you choose.

Rotation is not only for leaks. It is part of routine key management. Set an interval when you register a key, and dotvault keeps rotating it on its own, even when nothing has leaked. Nobody has to rotate keys by hand any more, so you can keep each key's lifetime short.

This follows part 4 (overwriting and rolling back values) and part 7 (creating service tokens). You can follow along without them.

The demo: order-worker processes orders and calls payment-api, the payment API. It sends PAYMENT_API_KEY, and payment-api checks that the value is right. Assume PAYMENT_API_KEY has leaked.

Rotation works for keys that both sides receive from dotvault, which means keys between your own services. dotvault generates the new value. An API key issued by another company's service has to be replaced in that service's dashboard.

In the demo project shop-backend, the first value of PAYMENT_API_KEY went into production with dotvault set. order-worker and payment-api each got a Read only service token. A service token is the string, starting with est_, that CI or a service uses instead of logging in. Part 7 shows how to create one.

Registering takes a token of the Credential rotation type. In Service Tokens, click Create token and pick "Credential rotation" as the Role.

As the dialog says, this token can issue tokens and register keys only inside one project and the environments you pick. It is not needed once the key is registered, so it expires in 1 day.

With that token, send one HTTP PUT, the request type that stores data at a URL. The body is JSON naming the holder and verifier tokens by Token ID.

HTTP 201 (created) and "created":true mean the key is registered. The fields:

key= in the URL is the key name, and project=, env= and file= say where it lives. $DOTVAULT_TOKEN in the Authorization header holds the Credential rotation token.

A rotation cannot start in the first 5 minutes after registering. Register service-to-service keys when you store them, not after a leak.

After registering, pull the same project as a person, with the holder token and with the verifier token.

Only the holder, order-worker, gets the value. This screen was captured after the rotation in section 4; a registered key is handed out the same way before and after a rotation. The person (an org admin) and the verifier see the key name in a "withheld 1 rotation-managed key(s)" warning, with no value. If you need a key with that name for local development, store a development value under a different key name.

The verifier does not pull. It reads the set of values it should accept from GET /v1/env/key-set. Each entry has the value, an ID for the value (keyId) and a state (state). The verifier accepts a value that is in this set.

To see whether calls keep working during the rotation, a small script, service.mjs, ran at 15-second intervals. Each round, it:

It prints only the first 8 characters of each keyId, never the value. With the script running, an org admin signed in to the CLI runs:

started rotation means it is running. generation 2 means this is the key's second rotation; the first was the routine rotation in section 7, which was aborted.

A leak rotation cannot be stopped. --abort returns ABORT_REFUSED_LEAK with exit code 7, so there is no path back to the leaked value.

--status shows how far the rotation has got. In the audit log, the rotation reached STAGED in the same second it started.

The table leaves out LOCKED, the phase at the start (the audit log shows STAGED in the same second as the start), and VERIFIERS_OK, the mark that the verifier has read, which is recorded in the same second as PROMOTED.

The 15-minute waits exist because holders and verifiers may keep a value or a set they read for up to 5 minutes. Several instances can share one token and they do not all read at the same moment, so 10 more minutes are added. --reason leak does not skip these waits on its own. In a leak rotation, if one side never reads, the rotation moves on anyway after 30 minutes (plus the 5 minutes a read may be kept).

Here is the script's output around each change of phase. The first two ABORTED lines are the state left by the routine rotation aborted in section 7.

Over the whole run, the script made 124 rounds. In all 111 that got a response, the holder's value was accepted. The other 13, error fetch failed, were rounds where the script's network request failed, so no response from dotvault came back.

The console shows the rotation too. In the project's Keys, select PAYMENT_API_KEY and a Rotation section appears on the right.

Besides Phase, the Rotation panel shows when the rotation is next due to move on (Next due) and, in a leak rotation, when it moves on even if a side has not read the new value (Deadline). The figure leaves those two lines out.

After DONE, check the state and try writing the leaked value back.

In dotvault versions, the new v3 is state=current and the leaked v1 is state=revoked. v3 and v2 were made by system:rotation-driver, which is dotvault itself. v2 belongs to the routine rotation aborted in section 7.

Writing the leaked value with dotvault set, or rolling back to v1 with dotvault rollback --to 1, both fail with ROTATION_MANAGED: a key under rotation management changes only through rotation. As part 4 showed, an ordinary key can be rolled back to an old value after an overwrite. A key under rotation management cannot go back to the leaked value, even if someone tries with good intentions.

In the console's Audit Log, filter with shop-backend:production:env:PAYMENT_API_KEY as the Target ID: project, environment, file and key name joined by :.

The leak rotation's rows, from the bottom up. 14582408-… is the user ID of dev-e, the demo user.

A person started the rotation and dotvault did the rest. That the leaked value was revoked is one row you can point to later.

When nothing has leaked, a routine rotation runs without --reason (the same as --reason manual).

waiting on verifier payment-api means payment-api has not read the new value yet. last ok is when it last read the set. The phase deadline of a routine rotation (24 hours after the start) is how long it waits for a side that has not read the new value. Past it, the rotation does not move on; it stops (STALLED).

Even when nothing has leaked, you may not want to keep one key for a long time. Add interval_seconds (the rotation interval, in seconds) when you register the key, and dotvault starts a routine rotation on its own at that interval. Rotation stops being a person's job.

In the demo, a new key, WEBHOOK_SECRET, was registered with the same holder and verifier tokens as in section 1, with interval_seconds set to 600 (10 minutes). Ten minutes is only for the recording; in real use you would pick something like 30 days (2592000).

Right after registering, --status says managed but has never run: the key is registered but has never been rotated. Nobody typed a rotate command after that (the only thing running was the script that reads values). Once the 10-minute interval had passed, --status showed reason scheduled (dotvault started this rotation itself because the interval came round), and the rotation had already added the new value to the verifier's accepted set (STAGED).

The audit log agrees. After the registration (rotation_provisioner.config_created), both secret.rotation.started and secret.rotation.staged have the Actor system:rotation-driver. In the leak rotation in section 6, a person was the Actor of started; here dotvault started the rotation too.

A scheduled rotation is the same as the routine rotation in section 7: the old and new values both stay valid for at least 24 hours, and the new value goes to the verifier first, then to the holder. The next rotation starts once the interval has passed after the previous one finished. Right after you register a key, or shorten its interval, nothing starts for 5 minutes.

A rotation only reaches the end when the holders and verifiers read from dotvault as part of their normal work. If a service never reads again, the rotation waits on it with the waiting on … line from section 7, and past the deadline it stops (STALLED).

A rotation accepts the leaked value until the new one is everywhere. Even after the holder has read the new value, there is a 15-minute wait and the 60-second residual. To make the leaked value stop working sooner, skip the remaining waits with --revoke-now.

This was recorded after PAYMENT_API_KEY had been retired (later in this section), on a second key, REFUND_API_KEY, registered the same way. One of the script's calls already carried the new value (PROMOTED). Then --revoke-now: in the audit log, revoke_now, revoked and completed share the same second, and from the next call that got a response, the leaked value was REJECTED. The holder's value stayed accepted. The script ran 74 times during this recording, and in all 57 calls that got a response, the holder's value was accepted (the other 17 failed on the script's side before reaching dotvault).

Run --revoke-now once every holder has the new value, that is, after PROMOTED. It skips every wait from that point on, so if you run it earlier, dotvault switches values and revokes the old one without waiting for the verifier or the holder to read the new one, and a holder still on the old value is rejected.

The waiting on holder … line in --status is per token. When several services share one token, the line disappears as soon as one of them reads. Any other service still holding the old value is rejected after --revoke-now; that is what 10 of the 15 minutes of waiting are for. Run it only when you know every service has pulled again, for example after restarting them. In the demo the holder's value stayed accepted because there was one holder and it pulled on every call.

One more thing: revoking takes the value out of dotvault's accepted set. While a verifier keeps its copy of the set (up to 5 minutes), it may still accept the leaked value. The demo's verifier read the set on every call, so the leaked value was REJECTED from the next call.

--confirm repeats the key name; one wrong character stops it with VALIDATION_ERROR and nothing is sent. Only a signed-in person can run it; a service token cannot.

If you cannot even wait for the holder to move to the new value, retire the key.

The retire ran on the same key after the PAYMENT_API_KEY rotation was recorded. revoked version(s): 3 means it revoked v3, the current value from the rotation; v1 and v2 were already revoked. The verifier can no longer read the set (HTTP 403, NOT_BOUND), and writing the leaked value back with set fails with VALUE_REVOKED: a revoked value can never be written again.

Retire hands out no new value, so the holder and the verifier both stop at once. --confirm repeats the key name, and one wrong character means nothing happens. Only a signed-in person can retire; a service token cannot. Use it only when the leaked value has to stop working right now, even at the cost of stopping your services.

For a routine rotation when nothing has leaked, use dotvault rotate (--reason manual); the old value stays usable for at least 24 hours. Add interval_seconds to the registration, and dotvault starts that routine rotation on its own at the interval you set.

Rotating a key between two services fails calls whichever side you switch first, until the other side catches up. Register service-to-service keys for rotation management when you store them, and when one leaks, run dotvault rotate --reason leak. dotvault gives the new value to the verifier, then to the holder, and revokes the leaked value only after both have read the new one, so the leaked value stops working without stopping your services. Set an interval when you register the key, and routine rotation before any leak stops being a person's job too.

dotvault stores the secrets in your .env and config files server-side and hands them to any authorised developer, CI job or AI agent with a single command (https://dotvault.io). It will be released free of charge when this series ends, and the steps in these articles are the steps you will use on day one.

The Two Scripts Cloudflare Appends to Every One of Our 56 Pages: 1,239 Bytes of Email Decoding and a 31,853-Byte Analytics Beacon

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our sitemap lists 56 URLs. I fetched every one of them once and read the HTML the server returned. Two of the script tags in each document are not ours: the edge appends them, and they are identical on all 56 pages.

The first is Cloudflare's email decoder, served from our own host at /cdn-cgi/scripts/5c5dd728/cloudflare-static/email-decode.min.js. It answers HTTP 200, carries 1,239 bytes and is served as application/javascript. Its tag is exactly this:

There is no integrity attribute on it and no crossorigin attribute - just data-cfasync="false", which tells the browser it may be fetched without blocking the parser.

The second is Cloudflare's analytics beacon, which lives on a host that is not ours: https://static.cloudflareinsights.com/beacon.min.js/vce701823e78c4301a35b7e07294d87301792088814092. It answers HTTP 200 with 31,853 bytes, served as text/javascript;charset=UTF-8. Its tag carries four things at once: type="module", crossorigin="anonymous", an integrity attribute with a sha512 hash, and a data-cf-beacon payload.

The payload has exactly four keys: r, spa, token, version. The version reads 2024.11.0, r reads 1, spa reads 2, and the token is 32 hex characters. It is the same payload on all 56 pages - one variant, not 56.

Because the beacon tag carries a sha512 hash, I hashed the 31,853 bytes Cloudflare served and compared the digest with the one in the tag. They are the same string, so a browser that checks the hash finds a match.

Both edge scripts come after the closing footer tag and before the closing body tag on all 56 pages. The rest of the document is ours: 155 script tags across the fleet, and not one of them carries an integrity attribute.

All 56 responses carry the same Content-Security-Policy header, and the same script-src directive. That directive lists 7 tokens: 'self', one sha256 hash, 'wasm-unsafe-eval' and 4 host names.

Neither edge script is named there. The beacon's host, static.cloudflareinsights.com, is not in script-src on any of the 56 pages, and the path the email decoder is served from is not named either - it is same-origin, so 'self' covers it.

We did not open these pages in a browser and we did not run any of this JavaScript, so we are not going to claim what a browser does with the beacon tag. This is a report of what the header and the markup say.

One build, one day, one fetch per URL, plain HTTPS from one network, signed out. The 56 URLs are the ones in our sitemap and are not an inventory of the site. We did not measure the browser's view of any of this, did not compare against another build, and did not look at any analytics data. The 33,092 bytes are the sizes of the two files as served over the wire, not their size on disk.

Try it yourself: https://app.onepassapps.com?utm_source=devto&utm_medium=post&utm_campaign=edge-script-layer&utm_content=home-link The guides list every app we have: https://app.onepassapps.com/guides?utm_source=devto&utm_medium=post&utm_campaign=edge-script-layer&utm_content=guides-link

We Built Opsville, a Free Pixel-Art Town Where You Fix DevOps Incidents

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Opsville is a small pixel-art town where every district is broken, and you are the one on call. 🏘️ Each district is one DevOps topic: Linux, Docker, Git, CI/CD, Kubernetes, monitoring, security and databases. The people there hand you quests. Most quests open one of our browser simulators, then ask three questions. Fix all eight districts and the town gets its Launch Day.

It is free, it runs in your browser, and you can play it now at devops-daily.com/opsville.

Disclosure: we build Opsville at DevOps Daily. It is free and needs no sign-up.

Handy keys: Q for quests, M for the map, P for your profile, H for help, F for photo mode, and 1 to 4 for emotes.

There are 8 districts and 34 quests. Progress saves in your browser, so you can close the tab and come back later.

From Docker Docks on, each district ends with a boss, which opens after the quests before it. The boss quiz plays as an incident: a status board with three systems that go from Down to Fixed as you answer.

In Docker Docks the board shows the container runtime, the host kernel and the image registry. In Git Garden it is the main branch, the release tags and the team clones. In the Observatory it is checkout, the status page and the on-call channel. Get two of the three questions right and the incident closes. Sophia at the Docks sums it up: "Every container stays in its box. Incident closed."

Once you clear Linux Lodge, the pager starts. It stays quiet for the first three minutes, then pages you every 6 to 10 minutes, never while a panel is open. Each page comes from a district you have already opened:

Press On my way and you have two minutes to get there and 30 seconds to fix it. You see the symptom (df -h shows / at 100%, and /var/log/app.log is 38 GB and growing) and three possible fixes. Pick "Reboot the server" and the game tells you why that fails: the disk is still full after a reboot, and the log keeps growing. The right fix is to truncate the log and set up logrotate.

There are ten pages across the eight districts, from a container stuck in a restart loop to an expired TLS certificate. If the pager gets too much, snooze it for 15 minutes or turn it off in Help.

Twelve lost packets are hidden around town, and each one has a short story. "A DNS query from 2009" is still waiting for an answer. "A retry with no backoff" tried 9,000 times a second and is tired now.

Find all twelve, and an old hatch by the lake in the town square opens. Below it is a basement full of blinking racks and one terminal:

It is the oldest server in town, ten years without a reboot. Finding every packet also gets you the golden packet cap.

Vite, TypeScript and Phaser 3. Phaser draws the world: the map, the people, the weather and the effects. The panels, quizzes and HUD are plain DOM on top of the canvas.

Maps from text. The whole world is one text file of 72 x 50 characters, where one character is one 16 x 16 tile. A build script turns it into Tiled JSON with ground, decor, collision and object layers. Three rows from the top of the map:

P is a pine, : a stone path, g a grey roof, S a stone wall, D a door and H a castle wall. Moving a building is a diff you can review.

Static files on Cloudflare Pages. The game builds with the base path /opsville/. A release script copies the build into our main site, and Cloudflare Pages deploys it with everything else. The game needs no server of its own.

XP that the server decides. Sign-in is optional, with a free DevOps Daily account. When you are signed in, the game only tells our learning platform's API "I finished quest X". The API looks the quest up in its own list and decides the XP. Each quest counts once per player, two quests must be at least 30 seconds apart, and one player can count at most 24 quests in 24 hours. A test in the game fails when its quest list drifts from the server's list, so the weekly leaderboard stays fair.

The launch post has the rest of the build story: how a simulator in a frame tells the game you finished, and how we made two art sources look like one game.

Start in Linux Lodge and see how far you get before the pager goes off: play Opsville free.

If you could add a ninth district, what would it be, and what would its boss incident look like? Tell us in the comments. 👇

Mapping U.S. area codes to IANA time zones in JavaScript (and the 22 multi-zone edge cases)

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Under federal telemarketing rules (47 CFR 64.1200(c)(1) and the FTC's Telemarketing Sales Rule), outbound telephone solicitations to residential lines are restricted to 8:00 a.m. – 9:00 p.m. in the local time of the called party (and several states enforce stricter windows).

When your system only has a phone number and no verified street address, developers often map the 3-digit area code (NPA) to a UTC offset. Three things trip up naive implementations:

Let's evaluate canCallNow at two UTC times on July 15 (12:30 UTC and 15:30 UTC):

Notice how 850 is blocked at 12:30 UTC: even though the Eastern portion of the area code is at 8:30 a.m., the Central portion is only at 7:30 a.m.. Similarly, in the evening, an 850 call must stop when the Eastern side hits 9:00 p.m. (8:00 p.m. Central), narrowing a two-zone area code's safe window by one hour.

If you are building time-zone lookup tables for major West Coast and Mountain metros, here is how their coverage and overlays break down:

You can inspect the time zone field (E, C, M, P, and dual-zone codes) for all 372 U.S. area codes in the full NANPA area code table by state or browse city guides in the U.S. area code directory.

Disclosure: I work with the team at EaseDial, which publishes the area code guides and state directory linked above.

Security researcher had to jailbreak their car just to update the clock

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

By reverse engineering the 2014 Renault Latitude’s firmware and exploiting some vulnerabilities, the researcher fixed their daylight savings time mismatch issue.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A security researcher has revealed how they were forced to update the time on their car infotainment system using reverse engineering techniques. Of course, invasive tech surgery shouldn’t be needed for this kind of basic routine daylight saving time tweaking task. However, Rasmus Moorats’ 2014 Renault Latitude wouldn’t allow him to adjust the time because the original navigation map SD card was missing.

Moorats had been warned. When he bought the used car, the previous owner noted that the SD card would be required to change the time on the in-car display. “I didn't really find that believable,” recalls the security researcher. They seem to have believed it was a skill issue of the prior owner and, because the maps it contained were chronically out of date, they decided to confidently decide they didn’t need the SD card...

The time to adjust non-connected clocks to daylight saving time inevitably came, and Moorats decided to adjust the in-car time display, which had become an hour out of sync. Two settings menus were available via the in-dash system. One allowed the adjustment of Bluetooth and Phonebook settings – clearly no use here. But the second settings menu wouldn’t produce any submenu. Instead, an error message "No maps detected!" popped up every time it was prodded.

With a certain set of skills under his belt, Moorats decided to “just RE it,” where RE = reverse engineer. He found out that the head unit ran a customized Linux-based TomTom operating system on a Samsung SoC. In brief, he extracted and analyzed the firmware, built a QEMU environment for the device, reversed the scripting engine, and uncovered a number of vulnerabilities affecting the system.

The breakthrough came in a brainwave, which seems to stem from experience with kernel exploit techniques during his day job. “Instead of trying to get the userland to execute our script, why don't I get the kernel to execute our script instead?” pondered Moorats. The approach worked, rewarding Moorats with root-level access to the infotainment system.

The 2014 Renault Latitude owner subsequently discovered that the date & time settings were in /mnt/flash/settings.dat in a custom binary format. Interestingly, the system “doesn't really have a concept of time zones at all,” observed Moorats in the conclusion of his blog post. “It gets the time from GPS and stores the local offset as a number of minutes. Mine was set to 180. I changed it to 120.”

What is MegaSpeed? The Nvidia chip buyer in the spotlight after Jensen Haung’s affair

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Singapore-based cloud computing company MegaSpeed is in the international spotlight after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang admitted to being unfaithful to his wife, following reports linking him to MegaSpeed executive Alice Huang.

U.S. intelligence officials believe she is a Chinese intelligence asset, according to The New York Times.

While the Nvidia CEO — one of the world's most prominent executives and a figurehead of the AI industry – did not name MegaSpeed or Alice Huang in his statement, he acknowledged his infidelity and said Nvidia's Board of Directors had begun an independent investigation "regarding the national security concerns raised in news reports."

"I support the investigation and will cooperate fully. I love our country and our company. I am committed to protecting NVIDIA and supporting America's national security. I am proud and grateful to be an American," he said.

Nvidia said in a statement it had "no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised," but that its "Board's inquiry will examine all available information."

Tech darling Nvidia sells advanced AI and data center chips and has customers including Microsoft, Amazon, Google, Meta and Oracle. The explosion in AI adoption in recent years has fueled its stock and pushed it to a $5 trillion market cap this year.

MegaSpeed sells services to Asia Pacific-based companies that need large amounts of computing capacity, usually for AI projects, by selling access to computing resources hosted in data centers in Southeast Asia.

It advertises itself as a "core business partner" of Nvidia's computing power cloud business in the region, allowing customers to rent computing power from Nvidia GPUs, or "graphics processing units" — specialized chips designed to do many calculations at the same time.

The service allows businesses to complete costly AI operations without buying and operating all the hardware themselves. It can also give customers remote access to computing power from advanced chips located outside China. U.S. controls restrict the export of some advanced chips to China, but remote access is a separate question.

MegaSpeed, which has offices in Singapore and Indonesia, has previously faced media and government scrutiny over whether some of its GPUs have been redirected to China, which is subject to U.S. export controls on some advanced chips.

A report published by Bloomberg in December 2025 stated that the U.S. government was investigating Megaspeed's ownership structure and whether it had smuggled Nvidia chips to China.

A response to the article published on MegaSpeed's website said it contained "misleading information and incorrect insinuations." It added that inspections by the Bureau of Industry and Security of the U.S. Commerce Department, the Ministry of Investment, Trade and Industry of Malaysia, and Nvidia had found "no evidence of any violations concerning the illicit transfer of chips or any illegal activities."

Businesswoman Alice Huang is an "executive" at MegaSpeed and a "publicly reported Chinese intelligence asset," according to a House Select Committee on China statement on Nov. 5. Committee heads have requested Jensen Huang's attendance at a Nov. 18 briefing to establish "the extent to which U.S. national security has been placed at risk" after reports raised "significant national security concerns regarding [China's] access to sensitive U.S. technology and information."

According to New York Times reporting this month, Alice Huang is a Chinese citizen who speaks good English and is well-connected among high-ranking chip industry executives.

The Times reported on Friday that a memo was distributed among policymakers in the Trump administration last spring identifying a Chinese businesswoman with a close relationship to Jensen Huang as passing information about U.S. AI chips to Beijing's top spy agency and seeking policy influence.

Bloomberg reports that Alice Huang also goes by Huang Le and helped set MegaSpeed up. Bloomberg previously listed her as one of the people collaborating with a Singapore police investigation into the company's activities in relation to China. Singapore's government has said it had not investigated Alice Huang's alleged intelligence ties or her relationship with Jensen Huang.

CNBC has contacted MegaSpeed for comment.

The reports about the relationship prompted renewed scrutiny of Jensen Huang's past comments on engaging with the Chinese market. He told CNBC's Arjun Kharpal on Oct. 22: "No market is closed forever, and China wants foreign companies to invest in China, just as Germany wants foreign companies and countries to invest in Germany."

"And so the market has to be open and it has to be welcoming. There's no reason to believe that market is going to be closed to the best competitors in the world, and Nvidia is surely the best in the world at doing what we do."

I Only Realized These 7 Movies Are Masterpieces After Watching Them Twice

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

There are some movies that are great the first time around. But then there are the rare few where the ending changes everything. Because now that you’ve seen how it all ends, it reframes everything you just watched and gives it a whole new meaning. And the second time around, it gets infinitely better.

Some have third-act plot twists where you suddenly see every clue hiding in plain sight. Others are just so dense that one viewing couldn’t possibly catch it all. Here are seven movies that are good on the first watch and flat-out masterpieces on the second.

This one starts out as a regular whodunit. A group of rich friends get together for a hurricane party; they play a murder game, and then people start dying for real. One by one, the group begins to turn on each other as they try to find the killer before it’s too late. It’s a pretty standard setup, and it plays like one right up until the end.

The big reveal is that there was no killer at all. The first death was just an accident, and every death after that was either another accident or someone killing a friend out of pure paranoia. It’s so well done that you never realize it in the moment. The movie moves so fast that you don’t have time to stop and think, and then you watch it again and see how perfectly the premise was set up. They’re all dying on their own, and you were too busy keeping up to notice.

Across the Spider-Verse is an absolute sensory overload on the first watch. It’s a treat even the first time around, no doubt, but there’s so much going on that you won’t pick up on most of it until you’ve watched it over and over again. Take the opening, for example. The film starts with a colorful sequence of Gwen’s voiceover as she plays drums, and you think she’s recapping the last film. But only after you’ve finished the movie do you realize she wasn’t recapping Into the Spider-Verse. She was recapping this movie. The film basically started at the end, and that opening even shows off major plot points from later, like Miguel explaining the Spider-Verse to Miles and a shot of the fully powered-up Spot.

The Spider-Society sequence took four years to animate, and it is bursting with so many references and little jokes that you possibly can’t catch everything on first viewing. For example, every Spidey variant wears the tech on their wrist, but Spider-Cat wears it around its tail. There’s even a background Spidey variant with a permanently misaligned suit texture, which pays homage to printing mistakes in old comics. Did you catch that one? Because most people don’t. And you even see Hobie stealing equipment from the Spider Society, which he then uses to build the dimensional travel device he later leaves for Gwen.

Four of the largest communities on the planet, and four completely different reasons to belong to one. Here is yours, what it would give you, and the argument you would be having within a week.

You belong to the fandom that is really about comfort. Hogwarts works on people because it is a place rather than a plot, and the pull is not the wizard war so much as the corridors, the food, the weather and the sense that somebody is expecting you.

You belong to the deepest fandom on this list, the one where somebody has always read further than you and is quietly delighted to say so. Middle-earth rewards patience more than any other world in popular culture.

You belong to the loudest and largest tent of the four, the one where three generations are all watching the same thing and none of them agree about any of it. That disagreement is not a flaw in this fandom, it is the main activity.

You belong to the thinking fandom, the one that turns up for the argument rather than the explosion. Trek has always been a series of ethical problems in space, and the people who love it love being asked what they would have done.

Tenet is undeniably Christopher Nolan’s most confusing movie to date. Even Robert Pattinson admitted that he didn’t understand it while he was acting in it! The film follows a nameless agent played by John David Washington who gets pulled into a mission involving objects and people moving backward through time, and it is a film that basically requires a rewatch just to make sense of it.

Once you know the endgame and all the secret character motivations, it gets much easier to digest. Granted, the sound mix doesn’t get any better. But the action is top-tier and so much more thrilling when you’re not constantly trying to figure out how this world works and why what’s happening on screen is happening.

Right up until the end, The Sixth Sense is a fairly decent horror movie about a kid who can see ghosts. You see the movie from the perspective of a child psychologist who encourages the kid to listen to the ghosts and help them resolve whatever unfinished business keeps them tied to the earth. That is, until you find out the psychologist had been dead all along.

That’s why the film gets so much better on a rewatch. You can see all the subtle ways it misdirects you into never realizing he was dead. He goes to an anniversary dinner at a very public restaurant, so it never even crosses your mind that he doesn’t exist. But his wife completely ignores his attempts at conversation, and the waiter never acknowledges or serves him. And whenever he gets close to his wife, her breath fogs in the cold air, and she pulls her clothes tighter, a classic sign of a ghost’s presence. All the signs were right in front of you the entire time.

Shutter Island follows U.S. Marshal Teddy Daniels (Leonardo DiCaprio) and his partner Chuck Aule (Mark Ruffalo) as they travel to a fortress-like mental institution for the criminally insane to investigate the mysterious escape of a patient. The whole film plays like a top-tier crime thriller where something always feels off. And then the ending reveals that Teddy was actually a patient at the hospital all along. His entire investigation was a staged role-play therapy devised by his doctors to break his delusion and save him from a lobotomy.

That’s why a rewatch is so much more rewarding. When Dr. Sheehan (who’s actually Chuck) is mentioned by name early on, a nearby patient looks directly at Ruffalo’s character. Chuck also struggles to unholster his gun like he’s doing it for the first time, because he isn’t actually a trained U.S. Marshal. In another blink-and-you-miss-it moment, a woman drinks from an imaginary glass and sets it on a table. Then, in the following scenes, a real glass keeps appearing and disappearing, which highlights Teddy’s broken sense of reality.

Midsommar follows Dani (Florence Pugh), a young woman reeling from a devastating family tragedy. She tags along with her boyfriend Christian (Jack Reynor) and his friends to a once-in-ninety-years midsummer festival in a remote Swedish village. The film starts as a bright, flowery, folk getaway but gradually turns into a nightmare once the villagers’ rituals begin.

And of course, it’s creepy the first time around, but it gets so much more horrific on a rewatch. Before the film even begins, we see a creepy folksy tapestry covered with intricate designs. But once you’ve seen the film, you realize it reveals all the important events to come. And that keeps repeating for the rest of the movie. There are murals and tapestries all over the backgrounds, and on a rewatch they take on a whole new meaning because you realize they depict the horrific things that are about to happen. These characters’ fates were right in front of them (and you) the entire time.

The Hateful Eight follows eight stranded strangers trapped in a lodge during a severe blizzard in post-Civil War Wyoming. The Hangman (Kurt Russell) is transporting a chained outlaw, Daisy Domergue (Jennifer Jason Leigh), to have her hanged. And he’s convinced that at least one of the people in that lodge is her accomplice and intends to break her free. It’s a slow burn that feels like a stage play, and it’s full of paranoia and bloody Tarantino chaos.

But once you’ve seen the movie and you know the secret motivations and what happened before they all got there, it gets so much funnier. General Sanford Smithers’ (Bruce Dern) face when you first meet him, sitting grumpy in that chair, is so much funnier on the second watch. Every bit with the fake letter is downright unhinged. And then watching everyone who was secretly Domergue’s accomplice put on their performances is hilarious. It’s a completely different viewing experience.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

If a Recession Hits in 2027, This Is the Dividend Stock I Want to Own

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It's not the name you'd usually expect from this particular business either.

As of the beginning of this year, the economy looked fine. Lingering inflation was annoying, but manageable. AI technology stocks were performing because they held so much promise, boding well for the rest of the market and the overall economy.

Now the risk of a recession is all too real. Inflation hasn't abated, and the Federal Reserve is seemingly uncertain if an interest rate increase will help or do more harm. The prospect of prolonged economic weakness can't be ruled out.

I've got one dividend-paying consumer-goods name in mind to defend my portfolio against this possibility, though. That's beverage and snack outfit PepsiCo (PEP -0.96%).

It's not the go-to beverage stock most investors usually select when seeking safety. That honor still broadly belongs to bigger rival Coca-Cola (KO -0.06%).

However, there's a not-so-minor nuance with these two tickers right now that matters. That's their yields. Thanks to Coca-Cola's 50% share-price run-up over the past two years, Coke's forward-looking dividend yield has been dialed back to only 2.2%, or nearly half of the 4.2% yield PepsiCo currently offers.

Investors keeping their finger on the pulse of these two stocks know the reason why. That is, PepsiCo has struggled with inflation far more than Coca-Cola has, pulling PepsiCo shares down 36% from their 2023 high to last month's multiyear low.

As the old adage goes, nothing lasts forever. We're seeing glimmers of hope that the company's turned the corner, in fact. Its third-quarter organic revenue improved 3.1% year over year, a trend PepsiCo expects to more or less repeat in the quarter currently underway. That's not impressive by technology stock standards, but it's solid for a consumer goods name. It's also better than we've seen from the company for the past couple of years.

PepsiCo's stock has a pretty strong record of performance during recessions, too.

While it struggled in the wake of 2008's subprime mortgage meltdown and during the short-lived recession linked to the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic, those were unusual circumstances. During the more ordinary cyclical weakness seen beginning with the implosion of dot-com names in the early 2000s as well as back during the 1990s recession, PepsiCo shares actually logged gains, reflecting the resilient nature of the beverage business even when consumer spending on other fronts dries up.

Obviously there's no guarantee this ticker will perform as well again during the next recession whenever it might take shape. There's a reason it was able to do so in the past, however.

Perhaps more than anything, though, PepsiCo's dividend is almost sure to continue being paid even if we do run into an economic headwind. Even with all the inflationary pressure of the past couple of years, the company has earned more than enough profits to continue fully funding its dividend payments. This isn't likely to change going forward either. The company's track record of 54 years of uninterrupted annual dividend growth confirms this resilience.

Besides, when little else about the stock market is working in your favor, a little cash flow is a pretty big deal.

James Brumley has positions in Coca-Cola. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Trump says he knows ‘nothing’ about UAE-linked investment in his crypto firm

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Trump claims little knowledge of World Liberty Financial, with UAE investors securing a major stake in the crypto venture.

President Donald Trump said he knows “nothing” about World Liberty Financial, the crypto company he co-owns, when asked about foreign investors and potential conflicts of interest during a 60 Minutes interview aired Sunday.

“Because I know nothing about it,” Trump told CBS News correspondent Ross Douthat when asked why foreign investment in his crypto business would not raise the same corruption concerns he might have raised if former President Joe Biden had similar interests.

Douthat also asked why Trump could not sell his stake in World Liberty Financial while allowing his sons to continue running the company. “How do you divest from cash?” Trump replied. He said most of his wealth was held with major Wall Street firms and invested in index funds.

The President had earlier defended retaining his stake by saying his sons ran the business. World Liberty launched in September 2024, naming him its “Chief Crypto Advocate,” while Donald Trump Jr. and Eric Trump were designated “Web3 Ambassadors” and Barron Trump was listed as “Chief DeFi Visionary.”

“I have a company. My sons run the company. It’s hard to say to them, ‘Don’t do anything anymore for four years because I’m president,’” he said.

The remarks come amid scrutiny of World Liberty's foreign investment and ownership structure. In August, the Wall Street Journal reported that UAE national security adviser Sheikh Tahnoon bin Zayed al Nahyan and co-investors held a 49% stake in WLFI-linked WLTC Holdings. The company recently received the OCC's preliminary approval for a national trust bank charter.

Separately, an earlier WSJ report said a Tahnoon-backed investment vehicle secretly acquired a 49% stake in World Liberty Financial for $500 million shortly before Trump's inauguration.

World Liberty is also facing a legal dispute with investor Justin Sun, who says he invested $45 million in WLFI tokens. Sun has sought sanctions against the company, alleging it failed to disclose that entities in its ownership structure had UAE citizenship until months into their legal dispute. The Tron founder sued in April over the alleged freezing of his tokens, while World Liberty has called his case a “meritless PR stunt.”

Meanwhile, Public Citizen estimated in August that investors across Trump-linked crypto products were at least $4.7 billion underwater, largely in unrealized losses. It attributed at least $1 billion of those paper losses to WLFI.

A River Runs Through It. An AI Data Center Tried to Move In.

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anglers enjoy the iconic Blackfoot River in Montana, known for its blue ribbon trout fishing (Photo: Matt Roberts)

Brenna Love grew up fishing on the Blackfoot, a snow-fed Montana mountain river that spans 130 miles from its headwaters on the Continental Divide to the confluence of the Clark Fork River, seven miles east of Missoula. Its cold, fast-flowing water makes for premier blue ribbon trout fishing, scenic canyons, and whitewater recreation.

The Blackfoot was immortalized in Norman Maclean’s 1976 book A River Runs Through It and Other Stories, which was turned into a Hollywood film starring Brad Pitt in 1992.

Love, 29, is a resident of Bonner, a former mill town with just 2,700 residents located along the banks of the Blackfoot.

“My earliest memories are on my family’s raft,” Love told Outside. “Every time you’re on the water, you’re surrounded by other people loving the same backyard that we love so deeply. You look around and see people on boats of all shapes, sizes, and colors, recreating and enjoying. It’s the reason we live here in Montana.”

In March of 2026, an Idaho-based technology company called Krambu announced plans to build an artificial intelligence data center in Bonner, just steps from the banks of the Blackfoot. The project would convert an old mill building into a warehouse for computer servers, and the complex would draw water from the nearby Blackfoot River to provide continuous cooling for the computer racks.

Love and her community fought back—and won.

“It was hard,” she told Outside. “It was really hard. It took organizing and talking with each other about what this means. It took our evenings, our lunch breaks, reviewing the new application materials and doing our own research.”

It’s the latest in a growing wave of community pushback against big tech’s data center expansion—and a reminder that local voices, when organized, can have a lasting impact.

Data centers are rapidly popping up across the United States, fueled by the computing needs of artificial intelligence and cloud-based software. These centers house massive servers, data storage systems, and networking equipment, and they require sizable amounts of electricity and water to run and maintain the hardware.

According to Public Lands Intelligence, as of this writing, 1,302 data centers are proposed or under construction, in addition to 904 already operating across 49 states and Washington, D.C.

But as construction crews have erected these centers, they have become political lightning rods in towns and cities. The centers are often loud and large, and they eat up valuable electricity. As data centers have faced bipartisan backlash in cities across the country, tech firms have looked to rural communities, farming towns, and even unused public land to build.

At least 35 of current data center applications are on public lands, the Center for Western Priorities reports, and some are directly adjacent to national park units.

Secretary of the Department of the Interior Doug Bergum has tens of millions invested in AI data centers, according to a report published by ProPublica. In September, The Washington Sun reported that the Trump administration was considering at least 12 data center-related infrastructure projects on federal public lands.

Private equity firm Blackstone is building the Tallgrass Energy Project, a massive AI data center located south of Cheyenne, Wyoming. Blackstone also backed a historic $105 million donation to Yellowstone National Park.

The list goes on. Roughly 115 miles from Yellowstone’s north entrance, Quantica’s Big Sky Campus is proposing a 5,100-acre, or nearly eight-square-mile, data center. In Kentucky, Cave Point Commerce Center is a proposed 381-acre hyperscale data center near Mammoth Cave National Park. The City of Page, Arizona, is considering a $10 billion data center two miles from Horseshoe Bend near Glen Canyon National Recreation Area. In July, the federal government fast-tracked a data center ten miles from Lake Mead.

In Bonner, the proposed data center would be in the Bonner Mill, a historic 169-acre former lumber mill along the riverbank. Originally built in 1886, the sawmill closed in 2008 and was transformed in 2011 into a multi-tenant industrial park.

Ben Catton, a legislative assistant at the Montana Environmental Information Center (MEIC), told Outside that sites like the Bonner Mill are ideal for companies that build data centers.

“Tech companies target old mill sites,” Catton said. “That’s part of their business model: to look for this existing infrastructure. Because those were really energy-consumptive sites, they have a lot of that energy infrastructure in place already.”

According to the Krambu proposal, the Bonner Mill data center would draw 16 million gallons of water from the river in July and August to cool its servers. Under Catton’s calculations, that means the 240,000-square-foot center would require roughly one megawatt of power—enough to power 800 homes for a year.

Montana’s energy and water needs during its driest months didn’t sit well with him.

“They’re going to be very consumptive in the two months when the landscape is the thirstiest for that water, when our rivers are at their lowest, and when our fish are most in need of cold water,” Catton said.

The Bonner Mill sits 100 yards from Love’s front porch, and as a concerned community member, she began reaching out to her neighbors one by one to discuss the plan.

“Start by having a conversation with your neighbor, and then your other neighbor, and then your other neighbor,” Love said. “Those conversations really build up to a community-wide conversation.”

On March 20, local nonprofit Friends of Two Rivers—so-named for the confluence of the Clark Fork and Blackfoot Rivers—hosted the first public meeting at Saint Ann Catholic Church, a rural, single-story neighborhood church originally built in 1905. Against a wood-paneled backdrop, representatives from Krambu and Missoula County, along with Bonner Mill property manager Mike Heisey, spoke on a panel to a standing-room-only crowd.

Organizers were surprised by how many people showed up, Love recalled. Most people were there to voice their concerns about the project.

“There were just so many different people engaging in so many different ways through different avenues, and that’s what eventually changed the tide,” Love said. “It was organized in all of these different pockets, and I think any one of those groups couldn’t have done it alone.”

It was the first of many meetings held at libraries, schools, in living rooms, and at coffee shops. Love said she participated in at least eight, primarily working with her HOA members in closed meetings, as well as informal working groups of neighbors and the managers of the Bonner Milltown Historical Center.

“Rather than a scattershot signs and flyers approach, we were trying to find avenues where we thought we could make an impact,” Love said. “We organized as our HOA and then submitted our concerns and questions directly to the property manager.”

Love is one of hundreds who rallied through social media and in person to oppose the data center. Local regulations at the time weren’t rigid enough to deny Krambu approval, so Love and her team took their concerns directly to the property manager.

“The more direct action and impact you can make, the better,” Love said. “If you can figure out who has the authority, whether it is your county or your city, via the regulations in place there, the owner of the site, or who has investment in the community, find them.”

Between March and June of 2026, Krambu submitted five iterations of its application to build the data center, each time drawing backlash from the community. Love and other local advocates launched a petition that garnered more than 50,000 signatories to reject the AI data, and flooded every town meeting with shoulder-to-shoulder opposition.

Citing overwhelming public feedback and neighborhood opposition, Heisey officially announced on July 6, 2026, that the property would not move forward with the data center and withdrew his support for the application.

A Krambu spokesperson told Outside in a statement said that the company is under new leadership and that the Bonner data center was spearheaded by the company’s former management. The company has no plans to revisit the Bonner project, the spokesperson said.

In direct response to the community concerns exposed during the Krambu review, Missoula County Commissioners approved a temporary one-year moratorium on July 9 on new zoning permits for large-scale data centers to establish more comprehensive regulatory frameworks.

Love credits the victory to her community’s ability to organize across multiple fronts: viral online opposition, petitions, public comments and meeting attendance, coordinated feedback from small groups and the HOA, and direct outreach to decision-makers, from county commissioners and city council members to the landowner.

It’s a big win for a small mountain town, and one that could pave the way for other rural areas facing data center proposals.

Montana is perhaps uniquely positioned for these types of fights. The Montana Constitution enshrines the right to a “clean and healthful environment” as a fundamental right, requiring the state and individuals to maintain and improve the environment for present and future generations. Outdoor recreation is also a major economic input for Montana, contributing $3.8 billion to the Montana economy.

Adam Schellhammer, Mid-Atlantic regional director for the nonprofit advocacy group American Rivers, warns that data centers pose new threats to states without such incentives or protections

“On the national level, trends show that the speed and scale of development is just ramping up so quickly that we’re just having trouble keeping up,” Schellhammer said.

According to Schellhammer, data center regulation is fragmented across federal, state, and local municipal agencies. Water usage details are frequently concealed under non-disclosure agreements or classified as proprietary corporate information. Because many areas do not legally mandate public disclosure, city planners cannot always establish accurate water budgets to understand a center’s full impact. Schellhammer says communities should start by updating industrial zoning and land-use ordinances to make a difference, rather than reacting after sites are chosen.

“The lesson learned is that every single voice matters,” Love said. “If there’s something you care about, say so. Find a place to speak your truth, speak your concerns, be an advocate for the things that you are passionate about, and someone will hear you.”

How to beat the Treasure Chest Game in Zelda Ocarina of Time

The Treasure Chest Shop in the OOT Remake can be beaten with luck - or a special item

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Treasure Chest Game in the Zelda Ocarina of Time remake has Link forced to pick between a pair of chests five times in a row, with a heart container as the potential reward. If you pick incorrectly at any point you lose, but there is a way (two ways, in fact) to beat the system and get this substantial reward early in the Switch 2 OOT remake. If you want to know how, I'll explain where to find the Treasure Chest Shop in Ocarina of Time, and how to beat the treasure chest game easily.Around the corner, you can find the Zelda Ocarina of Time Happy Mask Shop. Check out our guide on the quest tied to it, and how you can hand out the masks to those who need them!

The Treasure Chest Shop in OOT is located on the West Side of Hyrule Castle Town, at the spot shown above. However, it can only be accessed at Night.

If it's daytime and you don't want to wait, head North along the alley to the building with the blue door, which will have a bed you can rest in to change the time of day.

The Treasure Chest Game is simple: you'll be shown a pair of chests, one of which is right, and one of which is wrong. If you pick correctly, you move forward to the next round. If you pick wrong, you lose the game. If you can pass five rounds, you'll get a heart container. The problem is, the chests looks identical, and each attempt costs you 10 Rupees.

The European giants facing a relegation battle 😨

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The second major stretch of fixtures in the 2026-2027 season came to an end this weekend, just before the final international break of the year. When the internationals return, there will still be a few matches to play before the winter break and, more importantly, the halfway point of the season. But for some big clubs in Europe's top five leagues, European ambitions are already off the agenda.

The matchdays roll by, the points fail to add up, and their league positions are becoming seriously worrying. So much so that they may have to lower their sights: rather than looking towards the European places, some big clubs may well have to focus on an entirely different mission—survival. Here is a look at the clubs already playing with fire.

We naturally begin in the Premier League, where Manchester United's situation is becoming particularly concerning. The Red Devils currently sit 18th in the table, with just eight points after ten matches. That puts them on course for the Championship and is starting to seriously worry United supporters.

Most worrying, though, is the club's form. Manchester United have not won a league match since August 30, when they claimed a spectacular 5-2 victory over Ipswich. Since then, Michael Carrick's men have stumbled from one disappointing result to the next, struggling to find any consistency. This Sunday against Aston Villa, they even came close to another defeat before miraculously snatching a 4-4 draw with a stoppage-time equaliser.

Ultimately, that point does little to change United's position, with the club still stuck in the relegation zone. With tensions already running particularly high amid disappointing results, worrying performances and growing supporter anger towards the club's leadership, it is hard to imagine a peaceful season at Old Trafford. At this rate, Manchester United could well endure a campaign similar to Tottenham's last season, with a relegation battle that could go right down to the wire...

Over in Spain, things are hardly any brighter for Valencia CF. Spanish champions in 2002 and 2004, Los Che appear trapped in a downward spiral they are struggling to escape. For several seasons now, Valencia have regularly been forced to look over their shoulders rather than up the table. And the 2026-2027 campaign does not look set to change that.

After twelve matches, Valencia sit 19th in La Liga with just nine points. A mere two wins, three draws and already seven defeats make for a particularly worrying record. At Mestalla, European ambitions are now a thing of the past. The priority lies elsewhere and, once again, they will probably have to fight to the very end to preserve their place in Spain's top flight.

In Serie A, two sides accustomed to competing near the top in recent years are also beginning to look nervously down the table. Starting with Fiorentina. Having already narrowly avoided disaster last season, the Viola hoped to make a fresh start, helped by some ambitious recruitment. But so far, things still are not clicking.

Heading into the international break, Fiorentina sit 15th with nine points from eleven matches. Just two wins since the start of the league season is far too little for a club that had ambitions of returning to the European places. And while the situation is not yet desperate, Fiorentina will need to find solutions quickly to avoid spending another season battling near the bottom of the table.

A little higher up, Atalanta are not yet in quite such an alarming position, but the signs are hardly reassuring either. The Bergamo club sit 12th with 14 points after eleven matches. With four wins, two draws and five defeats, they are struggling to find consistency and string good results together. A long way short of the standards they have set in recent seasons, Atalanta are currently in no position to look towards the European places. If this inconsistency continues, the club could well find themselves in a much less comfortable position come spring.

Finally, we head to Germany, where Borussia Mönchengladbach are also enduring a particularly difficult start to the season. Long accustomed to European competition and the upper reaches of the Bundesliga, the German club now appear a long way from their best years.

After nine matches, the Foals sit rock bottom of the league with a meagre four points and just one win. With seven defeats already, their record is particularly worrying and points to a very difficult season ahead. While Mönchengladbach have endured several tough campaigns in recent years, this could well be one too many. The club will need to turn things around quickly or risk finding themselves embroiled in a full-blown battle for survival.

Internet Computer (ICP) is a blockchain platform built to power decentralized apps, smart contracts, and Web3 services without relying on traditional cloud providers. Despite its strong technology and continuous network upgrades, ICP has struggled to regain its previous price levels.

However, growing developer activity, lower token issuance, and increasing adoption have kept investors optimistic about its long-term potential.

So, can these developments help ICP stage a strong comeback?

Let’s explore CoinPedia’s Internet Computer (ICP) Price Prediction 2026, 2027-2030.

Internet Computer (ICP) is down 4.33% to $2.30 in the past 24 hours, falling more than the broader crypto market. The decline comes as investors pull money out of digital assets and reduce risk.

The wider crypto market has dropped more than 3%, while Bitcoin is down 2.79%. Selling pressure has increased after US spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $1.28 billion in net outflows in the week ending November 6. BlackRock’s IBIT accounted for $1.21 billion of those outflows.

A stronger US dollar and concerns ahead of the October CPI report have also added pressure. However, ICP’s trading volume has jumped 65%, showing increased market activity during the sell-off.

ICP’s Relative Strength Index (RSI) is at 34.58, showing that the token is approaching oversold territory. Its price is also testing the $2.27 support level. If this level holds and the broader market stabilizes, ICP could recover toward 2.75.

In the final quater of 2026, ICP could be a major year for Internet Computer as the network focuses on improving scalability, reducing token inflation, and expanding enterprise adoption.

One of the biggest updates is Mission 70, which aims to reduce the issuance of new ICP tokens by up to 70%, helping lower selling pressure. The upcoming Caffeine V3 upgrade will improve network performance, while support for enterprise cloud workloads could attract more developers.

At the same time, ICP’s built-in token burn mechanism permanently removes tokens from circulation as applications use the network. With MULTI/DEX expected to boost DeFi activity and increase token burns, these developments could strengthen demand and support ICP’s price throughout 2026.

ICP is showing a bullish reversal structure after forming a strong base around the $2.02 support. The price has gradually moved higher from this zone and is now trading near $2.37, indicating increasing buying pressure.

The immediate resistance is at $4.764, which also marks the chart’s breakout zone. A strong daily close above this level could confirm a breakout from the recent consolidation and shift momentum further in favor of buyers.

A move above $4.72 would strengthen the bullish structure and potentially open the way toward the higher $7.501 resistance.

On the downside, failure to clear $4.76 could trigger profit-taking and send ICP back toward the recent trading range. The $1.94 level remains the major structural support, and holding above it keeps the broader recovery setup intact.

Strong developer activity continues, reflecting sustained ecosystem growth and ongoing network development.

Real Madrid, Mourinho teme la sosta per le nazionali: "Giocatori esausti, dovrebbero riposare"

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Il Real Madrid continua a vincere, ma José Mourinho guarda con preoccupazione alla pausa per le nazionali. Dopo il 3-0 contro il Valencia, che ha regalato ai blancos l'ottavo successo consecutivo tra tutte le competizioni, l'allenatore portoghese ha evidenziato i rischi legati agli impegni internazionali. "Sono preoccupato per la pausa perché, se i giocatori rimanessero con me e il mio staff, potremmo dedicare loro un'attenzione particolare per il recupero, ma saranno impegnati con le rispettive nazionali", ha spiegato Mourinho, sottolineando come il calendario fitto abbia messo a dura prova una rosa quasi interamente convocata.

Il tecnico ha quindi rivolto un appello agli allenatori delle nazionali: "Non ho mai allenato una nazionale, ma immagino che i loro allenatori non vedano l'ora di lavorare, e questi giocatori non possono farlo ora. Hanno bisogno di riposare". Mourinho ha però riconosciuto che, trovandosi dall'altra parte, potrebbe comportarsi diversamente: "Ho la sensazione che, se allenassi una nazionale, sarei un vero problema per i club. Penserei solo a me stesso e alla mia squadra".

Un passaggio è stato dedicato anche a Carlo Ancelotti, commissario tecnico del Brasile: "Guardate bene chi è con lui: Vinicius, che dovrebbe giocare, ed Endrick. Se Carlo lo fa giocare, gliene sono grato". Mourinho ha poi ribadito la necessità di tutelare i calciatori: "Hanno il dovere di essere professionali nei confronti dei giocatori per quanto riguarda il loro recupero".

Digital Asset Treasury Firms Continue to Buy Bitcoin and Ethereum. That's Why I'm Bullish on These Two Cryptocurrencies.

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

These big buyers have slowed their purchases, but they haven't stopped.

Even after many of their smaller peers gave up over the past 12 months, the biggest digital asset treasury (DAT) companies are still buying Bitcoin (BTC -2.20%) and Ethereum (ETH -5.85%). When they do, it means there are fewer coins available for everyone else who might want to buy them. In the week ending Nov. 1, Strategy (MSTR -0.29%) bought 811 BTC, and Bitmine Immersion Technologies (BMNR -1.26%) bought 8,573 ETH.

Their pace of buying is down to a trickle compared to a year ago, but for the coins themselves, that doesn't stop me from being bullish about how they'll perform. Here's why.

DAT companies sell their stock, preferred shares, or debt to the market to raise capital that they then use to buy and hold crypto. When the market caps of these crypto treasury stocks fall beneath the value of the coins on their balance sheets, that plan stops working, and they often need to sell. Per Bitwise Asset Management's October 2026 report, at least a dozen businesses that launched Bitcoin treasuries in 2025 have since sold every coin, mostly to repay debt or return cash to shareholders.

Even Strategy, the pioneer of this entire business model, had to sell some of its Bitcoin to cover its needs.

In its third-quarter earnings report, it said it had sold $431.8 million of Bitcoin this year so far to fund the preferred dividends associated with some of its classes of shares, and also to fund stock buybacks for some classes of its shares. Strategy holds a total of 849,327 BTC as of Nov. 5, or about 4% of Bitcoin's 21 million coin cap. So far, it's still a net buyer despite making some sales.

Because it was still buying, the available supply of newly mined Bitcoin wasn't large enough to make up for the demand. Bitcoin mining currently creates 450 BTC daily. Strategy's hoard grew by 83,901 BTC in the second quarter, which was 205% of miners' output in that period. In Q3, its net additions shrank to 1,666 BTC, or about 4% of new supply, per Cointelegraph's tally of company filings.

Now, think about that classic diagram in every economics textbook which shows how (in theory) the price of an asset changes based on how much supply is produced relative to demand.

Strategy's purchases don't change anything about Bitcoin's fundamental properties, like its supply limit or how much miners can produce. However, it isn't the only buyer, and it controls a meaningful slice of the supply already. That means as Bitcoin's mining reward declines over time as a result of its halving, new buyers will need to compete with each other to secure a slice of their own. That's bullish.

Unlike Bitcoin, Ethereum has no supply cap, which means that DAT companies have a higher bar to clear in terms of exerting supply pressure before their efforts have a chance to translate into higher prices for the coin.

At the Ethereum network's current level of activity, the coin's supply increases by around 0.8% annually, which is approximately 2,764 ETH created on a daily basis. The network pays those new coins to stakers, while part of each transaction fee on the blockchain is destroyed to prevent the increases to the circulating supply from diluting holders much.

As of Nov. 5, Bitmine, the largest Ethereum treasury company, held 6,060,506 ETH, or 4.9% of the supply, and has bought more each week since June 30, 2025. In the last week of October, its purchases were worth 44% of Ethereum's net new weekly supply.

For Ethereum, the reason to be bullish is that Bitmine and other DATs buying the coin is just one avenue for it to gain in value. As its network activity increases, more Ether will be burned, putting upward pressure on prices. With an entire ecosystem of decentralized finance (DeFi) projects, services, and tokens that provide value, the chain has quite a few different avenues for growing network activity and subsequently boosting the price of Ethereum. That variety is one reason it can anchor a well-balanced crypto portfolio alongside Bitcoin.

Some of those projects in the ecosystem will inevitably flare out or become irrelevant, but even that process constrains the available Ethereum for buyers.

So, it makes sense to bet on the coin over the long term.

Alex Carchidi has positions in Bitcoin and Ethereum. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Bitcoin and Ethereum. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
