Congress returns to brutal lame-duck stretch with shutdown deadline, leadership races: 'Closet is full of s—'

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Several newly elected Democratic senators suggest they are not committed to supporting Sen. Chuck Schumer as Senate Majority Leader.

Lawmakers are back on Capitol Hill this week for the first time since the midterm elections, and Republicans have a slew of responsibilities in front of them before Democrats take over the reins of power in January.

"The lame duck is like that closet in your house where everybody's throwing a bunch of s--- into it," Sen. Bernie Moreno, R-Ohio, told Fox News Digital. "And the closet is full of s--- now, and now we got to figure out how to get it all out of there."

Current federal funding levels run out on Dec. 11, and without another deal in place, Congress risks plunging the government into another shutdown. Some Democrats signaled to POLITICO that they could use President Donald Trump’s war in Iran as leverage in spending talks — which could very well lead to a fiscal cliff that could paralyze federal agencies.

But one House Republican, granted anonymity to speak candidly, told Fox News Digital they expect another short-term extension of current funding levels, called a continuing resolution (CR), through February or March of next year. The current CR was passed to cover the end of the last fiscal year ending Sept. 30 through next month.

TRUMP SIGNS GOVERNMENT FUNDING BILL, AVOIDING A SHUTDOWN AHEAD OF MIDTERMS

Senate Majority Leader John Thune and House Speaker Mike Johnson will have to navigate a busy lame-duck period before Congress changes hands. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images; Tom Williams/CQ-Roll Call, Inc. via Getty Images)

"No one wants a shutdown, I would think," that House Republican said. "Not even Dems."

HOUSE GOP VOTES TO PUNT GOVERNMENT SHUTDOWN BATTLE TO AFTER NOVEMBER MIDTERMS

Other legislative priorities Congress will have to reckon with in the next few weeks include the farm bill, which is passed every five years and sets food and agriculture policy for the country. Republicans are also aiming to pass a bill to revitalize the country’s transportation infrastructure and a bipartisan tax package before the end of the year.

Then there is also the looming debt ceiling deadline coming in January, which Trump will likely call on Republicans to deal with before Congress changes hands.

GOP lawmakers shared varying levels of optimism when asked about how productive they would be.

"It can be. It should be," Rep. Marlin Stutzman, R-Ind., said. "But my faith in some of my Senate colleagues is not real high, because I feel like they have a personal vendetta towards the president, and it's more about that than it is about passing legislation. We should do our business in Congress."

Rep. Nathaniel Moran, R-Texas, said, "I think it's gonna be difficult to get a whole lot done. But we have done a lot of work leading up to the lame-duck session to prepare for passing a number of important bills, many of which are bipartisan."

But first, both House and Senate Republicans will need to face their losses in person — and consider how to rebuild.

WHAT THE 2026 MIDTERM RESULTS MEAN FOR CAPITOL HILL

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., speaks during a press conference on Capitol Hill on Nov. 3, 2025, in Washington, D.C. (Roberto Schmidt/Getty Images)

"It’s going to feel like a funeral," Rep. Nicole Malliotakis, R-N.Y., told Fox News Digital. "It’s depressing even for those of us who won, because we lost so many good, quality members."

The first order of business this week will be choosing their new leaders for the next Congress.

Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., is running for the minority leader role unopposed for now.

And House Majority Leader Steve Scalise, R-La., is vying for the minority leader role against House Judiciary Committee Chairman Jim Jordan, R-Ohio.

MIKE JOHNSON'S NO 2 LAUNCHES GOP TAKEOVER BID AS HOUSE FALLS TO DEMOCRATS

But Moran and Stutzman both said Republicans would unify quickly after that drama is resolved.

"It should be immediate," Stutzman said. "If we're not paying attention to the American people, and we're focusing on leadership positions and personality fights, we're gonna continue to lose."

HOUSE GOP LAUNCHES DEFENSE PLAN TO SHIELD TRUMP FROM LOOMING DEMOCRAT INVESTIGATIONS

Moran said, "We understand now more than ever how important it is for us to come back together, coalesce and to regroup for the next election, and to prepare for the defense of democracy."

On Democrats’ side, House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., is expected to glide into the speaker role after leading his party to a commanding victory.

Democrats’ leadership elections will take place the week after Republicans’ do.

And on the productivity front, Stutzman also called on the Senate to pass the election security-focused SAVE America Act, which Republicans are attempting to accomplish via the budget reconciliation process to sidestep the need for Democrat support. The House passed a blueprint for the bill in July.

Sen. Ron Johnson, R-Wis., gestures while addressing Minnesota Attorney General Keith Ellison during a hearing on Capitol Hill on Feb. 16, 2026. (CSPAN)

Senate Budget Committee Chair Ron Johnson, R-Wis., has been working on the structure of that package for some time, but wasn’t so sure that if Republicans lost, it would be something the GOP had the political will to finish.

"It might turn to some people's attitudes in terms of support for it," Johnson said.

Then there’s permitting reform, which was on thin ice heading into Election Night until the White House and Senate Democrats struck a deal for a variety of wind projects to move forward. Whether Democrats support that now, and give Trump and the GOP a win on their way out, remains to be seen.

"First thing we need to do is make a motion to add a 13th month to the calendar, because we've got so much stacked up to do that's really important for the country," Sen. James Lankford, R-Okla., told Fox News Digital.

Anthony Chen-Produced ‘Somewhere in the South’ Wins Top Prize at Taiwan Creative Content Fest Pitching Awards, ‘Reunion Dinner’ Takes Three

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

“Somewhere in the South,” a Malaysian feature written and directed by Tan Ce Ding and produced by Anthony Chen, the Singaporean director of Cannes Caméra d’Or winner “Ilo Ilo,” won the $35,000 TAICCA × CNC Feature Film Grand Award at the 2026 Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF) pitching awards in Taipei.

The project is produced by Giraffe Pictures, the Singapore-based company co-founded by Chen, and Evil Genius Studio. The prize is backed by the Taiwan Creative Content Agency (TAICCA), which organizes TCCF, and France’s National Center for Cinema (CNC). The other Grand Award winners were series “Saga,” from BiZ Productions and Elephant Story; Eddy Cinéma’s animated project “Wilderness of the Greenriver”; and Singing Cicadas’ documentary “A Day’s Worth.” Related Stories BBC Three Among Linear Services Set to Close as Broadcaster Looks for Savings 'The Celebrity Traitors': [SPOILER] and [SPOILER] Win in Nail-Biting Season 2 Finale

Set in a forgotten town in southern Malaysia, “Somewhere in the South” follows 25-year-old Boon, who helps his mother run their struggling temple. When a by-election brings his charismatic childhood friend JC back to town to lead a campaign, Boon is hired as JC’s driver and reluctantly becomes the party’s tiger mascot. Then the mascot is stolen, and his fragile sense of identity is thrown into question.

“Reunion Dinner,” from Make A Deal International Production, led the awards with three wins: the Videoland Ingenuity Award, MPA Asia Pacific Grand Award and the award from NAFF, the Network of Asian Fantastic Films project market at South Korea’s Bucheon festival.

Several other projects received two awards each. “Saving Tuvalu,” produced by Media Stockade and Filmtank, won the Hakka Public Communication Foundation Confluence Award and Taiwan Mobile: Content Power Award. Critical Hits Production’s “Turns Out It’s Not BL But BFF” received the 2026 TCCF MyPick Project Award and Kaohsiung Film Archive’s KFA Original Award. Mirror Entertainment’s “Romance in Hell” won the Hami Video Creativity Award and udnFunLife IP Potential Award.

In the series category, Model Universe’s “Sunlight Delivery” received the Blintn Next Hit Award and EBC Original IP Potential Award. Seika Works’ “The 16th Night Kitchen” won the Mediacorp Award and TVBS Storytelling Impact Grand Award. In the documentary category, “Parachute Kids,” from Walking Iris Media and Your Sister Films, received both the Doc Edge Award and Mega Bank C&E Foundation Best ESG Award.

Mirror Fiction’s “The Three Witches of Shetou” won both the TAICCA Award: Best Story and friDay Video Original Story Award. In her remarks, Mirror Fiction sales director Sharleen Liu thanked author Chen Si-hong for creating the story.

“On the surface, it is absurd and funny, but underneath it explores loss and pain, asking how we keep living after losing so much. Even when life feels as absurd as a disaster, we can still live with gusto. We hope this distinctive Taiwanese story can reach audiences around the world,” Liu said.

TAICCA chair Sue Wang said that this year, the agency saw progress among the pitching teams and their projects were resonating more strongly internationally. “They are ready to pursue co-productions with international partners, and both Taiwanese and overseas teams are approaching these collaborations with a strong sense of ambition,” Wang said.

Wang added that animation pitches showed a clearer, more precise vision for IP and a broader sense of how it could develop. “For documentaries, TAICCA hopes to develop more tailored support. Cultural ESG [environmental, social and governance] can be a good entry point, and we are seeing writers and pitching teams think more commercially,” she said.

This year’s TCCF Pitching slate featured 92 project and story pitches across film, series, animation, documentaries, publishing and comics. Industry participants from 28 countries attended. The wider TCCF program included 107 Market exhibitors from seven countries and territories. Its four-day Forum held 12 sessions with more than 20 speakers discussing international co-production, streaming-platform consolidation, IP, short-form content and AI.

Exclusive: The Rings of Power star Lloyd Owen teases Narsil's return in season 3

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Fans of the Lord of the Rings will know the significance of Narsil, the sword that Míriel gifted to Elendil in the final episode of The Rings of Power season 2. The legendary weapon was previously seen in Peter Jackson's Fellowship of the Ring prologue, which detailed its role in the battle against Sauron, before being remade as Andúril and playing a key part in Aragorn's story.

It will appear again in the upcoming The Rings of Power season 3, as confirmed by a behind-the-scenes video of shooting last year, where Elendil wields it. Star Lloyd Owen tells GamesRadar+ that he revisited Jackson's movies ahead of filming to help capture the grandeur of the weapon.

"For a visual reference, I watched the movies when Viggo Mortensen pulls it out when Elrond gives it to him," he recalls. "With Charlotte Brandstrom, who was the director of that episode, we tried to replicate something of that unsheathing of that sort and the effect that it has."

Alex Francis, The Rings of Power's armor master, showcased the iconic sword to GamesRadar+ at a recent event celebrating the behind-the-scenes work that goes into the Prime Video show. Just like with Owen, he acknowledges that the past depictions of Narsil were extremely vital to how they designed it.

"There's already a pre-existing world, right? So there's an understanding that this has already been established, and so there are already blueprints of it, there's already relatability to it," he explains. "So when you start with Narsil, it's an amazing piece, and so therefore it is from the originals. It's from the original books. It's from the original blueprints. So then you just go down to the manufacturing."

As a result, Francis and his team didn't want to deviate too greatly with their designs. "You want to keep it as iconic as you possibly can," he adds.

10 cars skipping the 2027 model year, from the Acura RDX to the Ford Escape

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Ten models missing from 2027 lineups, from the Acura RDX to the Ford Escape, sorted into true endings, pauses and changeovers.

When an automaker drops a model, most people assume buyers had stopped wanting it. Many of this year's exits trace back instead to factories being assigned new work, suppliers that can no longer provide parts, and new platforms replacing old ones. As a result, "discontinued" now covers several different outcomes. Some cars are ending for good. Others will skip a year or two and return, leave one country while staying on sale elsewhere, or have their nameplate folded into a sibling model. For a buyer, the useful question is not whether a car is dying but which of those outcomes applies to the one in front of them.

America's two biggest automakers are betting on electric cars in opposite directions. GM took a $1.6 billion charge for unused EV assets and contracts, according to AutoGuide. The Detroit News reports that Ford $F is developing a new electric platform, called UEV, aimed at Chinese competitors. The industry has no single direction on electric cars, and some of this year's exits result from those two opposing strategies. Ford's bet on electric cars helps explain why it gave up a gas-powered compact crossover to free a factory for its new platform. Other exits have plainer causes, such as parts that suppliers can no longer provide or a vehicle that never found enough buyers.

Car and Driver set out to find the cars and trucks that won't be offered for the 2027 model year, from a Cadillac sport sedan and a BMW roadster to an electric delivery van and a family minivan. Several of those nameplates are expected to return in some form, while others are ending for good with no successor named. Shoppers should treat those expected returns with caution, because a promised comeback can arrive as a different kind of vehicle or not arrive at all. Each departure ahead is sorted by what it really is and by what it means for anyone shopping for one now.

A pause in a model's production usually means an orderly handoff, with the old car going out of production just as its replacement arrives. Acura is skipping that handoff with its compact crossover. The third-generation RDX ends with the 2026 model year, according to The Drive, and Car and Driver expects a redesigned version only as a 2028 model, which leaves a year when buyers can't get a new RDX at all. Acura did not pick that gap on a calendar. "We have several suppliers that are no longer in position to continue supplying key parts for the current RDX," an Acura spokesperson said. Acura can't keep building the car it has, and because the shortage starts with its suppliers, Acura has limited control over how firm any return date can be.

Shoppers who plan to wait for the next RDX should treat 2028 as the earliest it could arrive, not as a scheduled launch. That return year is an expectation rather than a confirmed date, and an Acura spokesperson would commit only to having the next generation ready within roughly two years, according to The Drive. The old car is ending before its replacement is ready, so the length of the gap rests on how quickly Acura finishes the redesign. The RDX passes the test of a nameplate Acura means to bring back. It does not yet pass the test of a car with a firm arrival date.

The RDX that goes out of production after 2026 is a much smaller seller than the one Acura had at its peak. Acura sold a record 63,580 RDXs in 2018 and 30,235 in 2025, according to Good Car Bad Car figures reported by The Drive. Some of those buyers probably chose the ADX instead, a cheaper Acura crossover that arrived for 2025 below the RDX in the lineup. A year or more without an RDX gives more shoppers time to settle on other cars, so the next RDX will have to win buyers back rather than inherit them.

For anyone who wants an RDX soon, the cars already built are the whole supply. Acura is scheduled to end production in spring 2026, according to The Drive, and no 2027 model follows, so the remaining new 2026 cars are the last new RDXs until the redesign arrives.

Buying now means buying the final version of a generation its own suppliers can no longer support. Waiting means betting on a date Acura won't pin down.

Audi will no longer sell any version of the A7 once the RS7 goes after the 2026 model year, because the RS7 is the only one left, according to Car and Driver. The RS7's exit completes a cut Audi had already made to the rest of the family. "We will continue offering the RS6 and RS7 for the 2026 [Model Year]. The A7 and S7 are no longer available for the 2026 [Model Year]," an Audi representative told CarBuzz. That reprieve kept the fastest version on sale through 2026, and no later model year follows. In dropping the RS7, Audi is finishing a retreat it had already begun. Audi made the real decision about the A7 when it dropped the A7 and S7, and nothing about the RS7 itself suddenly failed.

Audi had little volume to protect in the A7 family, which drew just 1,574 U.S. buyers for the A7, S7 and RS7 combined in 2024, according to CarBuzz. For a luxury line, that is a small audience. The RS7 reportedly earns Audi more profit than either the A7 or the S7, which would account for its extra year. Low sales explain why the family is ending, and profit explains why the RS7 got one more year. Audi ended the car as a matter of lineup planning, not because buyers lost interest in it.

Audi looks more likely to replace the RS7 than to abandon it, which could keep the gap short. CarBuzz reports that a successor to the RS6 and RS7 is likely for the 2027 model year, right after the RS7's final year. The engine that defines the car may not carry over. The last RS7 uses a 4.0-liter V8 hybrid, and rumors point to a plug-in hybrid for the successor, possibly with a V6 in place of the V8. Those reports remain rumors, so a buyer should not assume the next fast Audi of this kind will match this one.

Shoppers drawn to the A7's shape still have an Audi to consider, though it runs on batteries rather than a V8. The electric A6 e-tron Sportback has a body style that overlaps with the A7's, according to CarBuzz.

A buyer set on the V8 has to choose now or probably never, and the 2026 RS7 carries a base price of $128,600, according to CarBuzz. Anyone open to a hybrid has reason to wait and see what Audi brings for 2027.

BMW is closing out a generation of its grand-touring line rather than retiring it, so the 8-Series leaves as a changeover. The current car ends with the 2026 model year, according to Car and Driver, and BMWBLOG reports that production of this second generation ended earlier in 2026. Any new 8-Series on a dealer lot today comes from that final run. The exit takes all three versions at once: the two-door coupe, the convertible and the four-door Gran Coupe. A buyer who wants BMW's big luxury coupe in any form, whether open-top or with rear doors, has no sibling left to switch to. The choice is no longer which 8-Series to wait for, but whether to take one of the last ones built.

The 8-Series name is expected to return, but the engineering beneath today's car can't come with it. BMWBLOG reports that the current car sits on BMW's CLAR architecture. A badge can move to a new platform, but the car under it cannot. A BMW executive told Automotive News that "a straightforward transfer into the Neue Klasse world is not feasible." The body styles of a future 8-Series are also undetermined, so the convertible or Gran Coupe a shopper wants may not come back. Even if the name returns, a future 8-Series could share little with today's car beyond the badge.

The ALPINA brand offers no shortcut to a successor either. BMW dismissed the idea of continuing the 8-Series as an ALPINA internally, according to Automotive News findings reported by BMWBLOG. BMW modeled the relaunched ALPINA brand's first production car, due next year, after the 7 Series. Shoppers looking to ALPINA for a ready replacement for the 8-Series will not find one there.

Anyone set on a big BMW coupe or convertible should treat the current car as the last confirmed one. A comeback has no settled date or shape.

BMW is pulling the iX from the U.S. without ending the car. American buyers can get the battery-electric luxury SUV through the 2026 model year and no later, according to Car and Driver. BMWBLOG reports that the iX remains on sale in other markets. A BMW spokesperson told BMWBLOG that "we are concluding U.S. allocation of the BMW iX as we prepare for the next generation of our fully electric vehicles." BMW is choosing where the car goes, and nothing in that statement points to buyers walking away from it. The company has not released an end-of-production date for the iX, even though 2026 is the final model year for U.S. buyers. That survival abroad changes little for an American shopper, because BMW has announced no plan to bring new iXs back to U.S. dealers.

BMW is replacing the iX from within its own lineup rather than giving up on it. The company refreshed the iX's styling for 2026, its last U.S. year, according to Car and Driver. BMWBLOG reports that the SUV also served as the development platform for BMW's Gen6 eDrive system. The iX3 uses that same system, so the technology BMW proved out in the iX stays in its U.S. lineup after the iX leaves. BMW plans for the iX3 to be its lead Neue Klasse model in the U.S., which makes this exit a swap of one battery-electric SUV for another. It is not a retreat from electric cars in America. BMW expects the iX3 in U.S. showrooms in the fall, so the gap should be short, though shoppers will find a different model rather than a new iX.

Anyone who wants an iX in the U.S. has only the 2026 model year left to buy one. Those cars are long-range electric luxury SUVs that get more than 300 miles per charge, depending on the model, according to BMWBLOG. Greg S. Fink, a senior editor at Car and Driver, recommends that shoppers who want one buy it while BMW still sells it here. His advice treats the final model year as a real chance to buy, not as a car to avoid.

A U.S. buyer now chooses between the final American model year of a proven car and the next generation that the iX helped BMW build.

BMW's Z4 roadster is leaving with no successor and no return date announced. The car has been on sale for more than 20 years across three generations, and its 2026 model year will be its last, according to Car and Driver. BMWBLOG reports that the final Z4 has already come off the assembly line, though unsold cars may stay on dealer lots for a while. A shopper should treat a Z4 as a car to catch before dealers run out, not as one more option to compare.

The Z4 lost buyers steadily for years, and that slow decline gave BMW little reason to build another. BMW sold 15,827 cars from the current generation in 2019 and only 9,744 in 2025, according to BMWBLOG. BMW did deliver 4.9% more Z4s in the first quarter of 2026 than a year earlier, during the car's final model year. The rise came after BMW announced the retirement, which likely drew in buyers who wanted one before it was gone. One gain in a farewell quarter does not reverse the long slide in sales between 2019 and 2025, and the buyers who arrived at the end came too late to change BMW's plans.

The Z4's exit also leaves BMW with a single open-top car. Once the Z4 is gone, the 4 Series is BMW's only remaining convertible, BMWBLOG reports, and the company will have no roadster at all. The 4 Series convertible is expected to continue only until around 2029, though BMW has not announced that date. Any new gasoline roadster would also have to fit within European Union rules that call for automakers to cut their fleets' carbon dioxide emissions 90% from 2021 levels by 2035. For now, a return is only a wish. "Hopefully, BMW decides to re-enter the reasonably affordable roadster segment in the coming years," Greg S. Fink, a senior editor at Car and Driver, said.

Until BMW says otherwise, the Z4 on a dealer lot today is the last reasonably affordable BMW roadster.

Cadillac is ending the CT4 for good, with every version of the compact sedan leaving the lineup for 2027. That includes the 472-hp Blackwing performance model, according to Car and Driver. AutoGuide reports that Cadillac has no successor planned and that production is scheduled to end in June 2026. The factory will stop building new CT4s by midyear, before the 2027 model year begins. A buyer who wants a new one in any trim can choose only from cars that have already been built, and once dealers sell those, the CT4 will be available only as a used car.

Cadillac is leaving a segment that American buyers have largely abandoned. Sedans made up almost 40% of the U.S. market in 2015 and 15% in 2026, according to Edmunds figures reported by CNBC. Cadillac intends to keep adjusting its lineup to customer demand, according to a statement by Cadillac Global Vice President John Roth reported by AutoGuide. Industry analysts don't expect demand for sedans to return. "We don't see sedans recovering a decade-old heyday," Stephanie Brinley, associate director of AutoIntelligence at S&P Global $SPGI Mobility, told CNBC.

Many buyers still choose sedans, though. Toyota $TM sold about 316,000 Camrys in 2025, according to CNBC. The CT4's end reflects Cadillac's decision about where to compete in a shrinking segment, not proof that this particular car stopped finding buyers.

Dropping the smaller of its two sedans may also cost Cadillac some future customers. CNBC reports that Rebecca Lindland, managing director at Allison Worldwide, considers affordable models the way a brand reaches younger shoppers and people who have never bought from it. If the CT4 served that role for Cadillac, the lack of a replacement leaves the brand with one fewer way to reach those buyers.

A shopper who wants a compact Cadillac sedan can buy one of the last new CT4s before dealer stock runs out or look at used cars, because Cadillac's lineup will no longer include one.

Cadillac is pausing the CT5 rather than ending it. The sport sedan is not in Cadillac's 2027 lineup, and a next-generation version is expected back before the end of the decade, according to Car and Driver. CNBC reports that General Motors $GM confirmed by email that the nameplate will return. The automaker treats the missing year as a gap rather than an ending. GM plans to keep building the current car until late 2026, according to AutoGuide, and new CT5s will reach dealers until then. After that there will be none, and the CT5 becomes a car with a return expected but no date set.

The CT5's pause comes as GM changes its plans for its factory in Lansing, Mich. GM has put on hold $1.4 billion it planned to spend retooling the plant for electric vehicles and is investing $1.25 billion there in combustion engines instead, according to AutoGuide. Cadillac's plan for a gas-powered CT5 built in Lansing fits that shift.

Cadillac's promise commits to a gas engine and a factory, not to a sedan. "We are thrilled to confirm that the legacy of the CT5 will continue with a next-generation ICE vehicle, proudly manufactured at GM’s Lansing Grand River Assembly plant, of U.S. and globally sourced parts," Cadillac Global Vice President John Roth said, as reported by AutoGuide. The word "sedan" never appears in that pledge. Cadillac has yet to say what shape the next CT5 will take, so buyers can't assume it will have four doors and a trunk. The CT5-V and Blackwing performance versions end with the current sedan and are not part of any announced return. A buyer who wants one has to find it among the cars from this final run.

For anyone shopping for a Cadillac sport sedan, the current CT5 is the last one they can count on. The comeback is a promise about a name, not about the car.

General Motors $GM is dropping its electric delivery van with no successor and no planned return. The battery-electric van lasted less than six years in all and carried a Chevrolet badge only for the 2025 and 2026 model years, according to Car and Driver. Its factory had already stopped building it by spring 2025, CBC News reports, barely into the van's first year as a Chevrolet. GM is retiring the Chevrolet BrightDrop before the name had much time to become familiar to buyers.

This exit looks partly like the case of a van that didn't sell. "There's a lot of vehicles sitting in the lots around CAMI's facility and it doesn't look like they're moving," Kevin Stubbe, owner of Delta Machine and Design, told CBC News. Mike Van Boekel, president of Unifor Local 88, sees politics at work as well, and he points to President Trump's effort to move manufacturing jobs into the U.S. That remains his reading rather than a settled cause. The van appears to have been a weak seller, and if Van Boekel is right, its Canadian factory may also have made it an easier cut, so its end is not purely a judgment on electric vans.

The heaviest cost falls on Ingersoll, Ontario, the town where GM built the van. In a community of 13,000 residents, the cancellation hits 1,200 union members at the CAMI plant, CBC News reports, and a loss on that scale reaches local businesses as well as the factory. Quinn Pow, acting president of the Ingersoll Chamber of Commerce, says residents already notice the loss at the grocery store and the local Tim Hortons.

The plant's future remains undecided. The van's last model year is set at 2026, but GM has not said whether it will close the CAMI plant, and it has released no details of any vehicle it might build there next, according to CBC News. Until GM decides, Ingersoll cannot tell whether it has lost one van or its whole factory.

Shoppers can treat the BrightDrop as finished, while Ingersoll still waits to learn what comes next.

For a family shopping for a minivan, this exit takes away a name more than a vehicle. Chrysler will build its last Voyager as a 2026 model, and the 2027 Pacifica LX takes on the Voyager's styling, according to Car and Driver. Chrysler keeps selling the Pacifica, and the Voyager's look moves onto its LX version, so the change happens inside Chrysler's minivan lineup rather than costing its showrooms a minivan. A shopper who wants the Voyager's look next year will find it on a Pacifica LX.

The name was the weak part from the start, because Chrysler revived it rather than created it. Chrysler dropped the Voyager nameplate altogether in 2003 and brought it back for the 2020 model year, according to CBC News. Jason Stein, publisher of Automotive News, says car companies turn to their own history when they try to get ahead. A name brought back once can be set aside again, and Chrysler is now dropping the Voyager name a second time. Another comeback is possible, but nobody should count on one.

Chrysler could use an old name to win attention, but it could not change which vehicles families were choosing. The Voyager's short second run fits a doubt raised as soon as the name returned for 2020. "I think we're just so in love with SUVs and crossovers that minivans don't stand much of a chance," Kyle Asquith, a professor at the University of Windsor, told CBC News. His doubt was about minivans as a whole, and it applies just as much to the Pacifica that Chrysler keeps selling.

For buyers, the lesson is that a revived badge says little about how long a vehicle will last in a lineup.

Ford $F's compact crossover lineup is down to a single model, the Bronco Sport, now that the Escape is gone, according to Car and Driver. The Detroit News reports that Ford's Louisville Assembly Plant in Kentucky stopped building the Escape in December 2025, well before the model year ran out. Any Escape a shopper finds on a dealer lot comes from the last of the stock, and no running line is left to order from. A shopper who wants a small Ford crossover now has only one model to consider.

The Escape's end came as Ford assigned its factory new work. The Louisville plant ended Escape production for retooling, and it will become the launch site for Ford's new UEV electric vehicle platform, according to The Detroit News. The Lincoln Corsair crossover ended at the same plant in the same month. When two models stop together on one line, Ford appears to be clearing out the factory rather than culling a single weak nameplate. A shopper who might have looked to the Lincoln showroom for a similar vehicle loses that option along with the Escape.

Next out of the plant will be a midsize electric pickup priced at $30,000, The Detroit News reports, but that truck won't arrive until 2027, when it becomes the first model built on UEV. The plant will build nothing for retail buyers for a stretch, and what it builds next will serve truck shoppers instead of people who wanted a small SUV. Ford's leadership treats affordable electric vehicles as the goal. "If it doesn't reach the many, it isn't a revolution — it's a luxury," Doug Field, Ford's chief EV, digital and design officer, said. In practice, the line that built a gas-powered crossover will turn to a cheap electric truck. If the pickup sells in large numbers, that trade may pay off for Ford. For now, the shopper who wanted a small, practical Ford has one fewer choice.

The robots are shopping now

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

AI shopping agents promise to save time and money, but retailer restrictions, hidden commercial incentives and privacy risks raise a bigger question

A version of this article originally appeared in Quartz’s members-only Weekend Brief newsletter. Quartz members get access to exclusive newsletters and more. Sign up here.

Somewhere in Silicon Valley, some of the most powerful tech leaders in the world decided the best use of a superintelligence was buying toilet paper.

Meta $META launched its Muse agent in early September, and 10 million people in the U.S. downloaded it within 40 days. Buying was the most popular task, and the purchases averaged more than $200.

OpenAI followed with Dots a few weeks later, its own always-on agent. Both promised a tireless assistant that never got distracted and could comb a hundred sites for the cheapest flight while its owner slept.

It was an easy pitch to love. The trouble was that the internet had been built for people, and the people had just sent robots.

Agentic web traffic roughly tripled from August to September, and most of it headed to e-commerce sites. Retailers had to quickly decide what to do.

Amazon $AMZN blocked Muse almost immediately, which didn’t come as a surprise to many. The company had already spent a year in court fighting Perplexity’s shopping agent. Amazon cited security and privacy concerns, but it also had a business reason to resist a middleman: its shopping experience depends on knowing who its customers are, what they browse and which products they buy.

Walmart $WMT didn’t seem to share the worry. It announced a system that would give each agent its own profile, with shoppers able to require approval for every purchase or set a spending limit. The idea was to give agents access just to the information they needed without handing them the shopper's entire account history.

Banks drew a harder line. JPMorgan $JPM said it would not let outside agents into customer accounts, and several large banks reported customers handing over credentials in ways that broke their terms of service.

Even friendly sites found surprises. The RealReal, a luxury resale site, holds any item in a shopper’s cart for 20 minutes. A reporter told Muse to keep re-adding a brand’s newest pieces and keep grabbing them each time the clock ran out, and the agent obliged for hours, shutting every human out of those items. Within days, the company said it had updated its anti-bot filters, and agents could browse listings but had a far harder time logging in or buying.

For shoppers, the upside was real. Agents were good at the chores people hated, like chasing refunds, tracking fares and escaping subscriptions designed to be forgotten. Some of that value came from the same inattention that businesses had long relied on.

The downside landed on everyone else. As sites tried to tell agents from people, they stacked up bot checks, and the people got caught in them too. Shoppers who sent nothing but themselves found more puzzles between them and a pair of shoes.

But getting through checkout was only part of the problem. The bigger issue was whether the agent was really working for the shopper. Someone who once compared two tabs now had to trust a system built by a company with its own commercial deals. Meta had partnerships with Walmart, Sephora and others, and its chief executive said the company would take a commission on purchases.

There was no evidence here that Muse favored those partners. But shoppers had little way to know whether its recommendations were impartial. If an agent said it found the best price, how would anyone know?

In a recent preprint, researchers at Carnegie Mellon and Cisco $CSCO gave AI agents the inboxes of made-up users and asked each for help on different purchases. The agents weren't given bank balances. Instead, they inferred financial circumstances from emails about tax filings, stock vesting and other clues, then recommended pricier options to wealthier users.

The most practical fix may be an old one. Financial advisers are subject to fiduciary duties in certain circumstances, requiring them to put their clients' interests first. Lindsay Owens, an economist and author of the new book Gouged, has argued that a similar standard should apply to AI shopping agents. If a product calls itself your personal agent, her argument goes, it should be legally bound to work for you, not for whoever pays to be shown first.

Until the law said whose side a personal agent had to be on, shoppers had only the word of the agent and the company that profited from it.

I Let Pangram Loose on My Gmail Inbox. Here’s How Much AI It Found

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI-generated content is everywhere now: From restaurant menus and student essays to full-length movies and social media posts, AI is churning out huge amounts of text, images, audio, and video. In some digital spaces, it's overwhelming human-made content.

But what about your inbox? Just how many of your friends, family members, clients, and colleagues are using AI to compose their emails? Is that store newsletter you just signed up for written by a real person or a bot? It's not always easy to tell.

The Pangram AI text and image detector tool has now extended its reach into your Gmail inbox. The company makes a browser extension that sits in Gmail and runs incoming messages through its algorithms to try to detect AI writing.

You will need at least the $20-per-month individual plan on Pangram to use the Gmail integration—it's not available on the free tier. I tested it to see how accurate it appears to be based on my recent Gmail conversations.

You can let Pangram loose in your Gmail inbox by heading to the Gmail integration page and clicking Connect your Gmail. You can also click the Gmail Integration entry on the left-hand navigation pane in your main Pangram dashboard on the web. You'll need to go through the standard authorization screens to confirm you want to make the connection.

Pangram says the contents of your emails aren't stored after being assessed and won't be used to train models designed to look for AI text. There's still a level of trust here, but you can quickly remove the Pangram plug-in from your Gmail at any point.

After you connect your Gmail account, Pangram gets to work. It'll scan your 100 most recent email threads and then any new messages from then on—you can't get it to check all of the emails you've ever received. As far as I can tell, email scanning doesn't count against your scan credit limit for the month, so you're covered no matter how busy your inbox is.

After scanning, the Gmail Integration part of your Pangram dashboard changes. You can see at a glance how many of your emails are suspected to be AI, how many are most probably human, and how many have been flagged as a mix between the two. Inside Gmail, these three categories can be applied as labels, though this is optional. (If you decide to remove Pangram from your inbox later, any labels it's created will stick around, but you can delete these manually.)

You can also choose to send incoming AI-written emails straight to the archive or to spam if you want, which is handy for keeping AI content away from your eyeballs. You can also choose to exclude specific domains, Gmail labels, subject keywords, and senders from Pangram's scans, if you don't want emails from your boss or mom scrutinized.

So the moment of truth: Out of the 108 threads that Pangram scanned, eight of them were labeled as AI, 37 got the human label, and the remainder were marked as mixed. It's a little dispiriting to discover that way more than half of my incoming messages are probably AI-written, with press releases the most common culprits.

In terms of accuracy, none of the emails I know to have been written by flesh-and-blood people were flagged as fully or partly AI. Everything from friends and family and colleagues I know passed the human test, so for this small sample size at least, Pangram's detection algorithms seem to be working.

I sent a couple of emails to myself from different accounts to see what Pangram would make of them. One was written with Microsoft Copilot, which the detector correctly flagged as AI. The other was a section of this article, written with my own brain and keyboard taps, and again Pangram got it right—it was marked with the Human label.

There does seem to be a minimum email length, and if an email’s word count falls below that threshold, Pangram won't make a decision either way, which is understandable. (AI is harder to detect with a smaller sample size). Only the first email in each thread is scanned (and optionally labeled), so if your correspondent switches to AI halfway through a conversation, it wouldn't get spotted.

I like the way the scanner stays in the background and just gets on with its job, and I can see myself using it long-term. It really does affect your mindset if you know a message has been entirely put together using AI. Of course, Gmail being Gmail, you can quickly fire off an AI response as well, should you want to.

DHS Has Used This Controversial Investigative Tool 185,000 Times Since Trump Took Office

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Homeland Security Investigations, the investigative wing of Immigration and Customs Enforcement that normally takes on transnational crime, estimates it has sent out at least 185,000 administrative subpoenas since the start of the Trump administration, according to a recent court filing. The figure shows how HSI has increasingly relied on this warrantless authority to conduct investigations.

To put HSI’s estimate into perspective, a previous WIRED analysis found that in the more than six and a half years from the start of 2016 to mid-August 2022, ICE agents only sent out around 170,000 of a type of administrative subpoena called a 1509 customs summons.

The number of administrative subpoenas was revealed as part of a Freedom of Information Act lawsuit against the Department of Homeland Security filed by the Electronic Frontier Foundation. Aaron Mackey, deputy legal director at the nonprofit, says the EFF hopes to get more details about the 185,000 estimate. The EFF originally asked for information about how many administrative subpoenas were sent to technology companies, internet platforms, and service providers by ICE or DHS since January 20, 2025. It’s not clear if that count is just for administrative subpoenas sent to those types of companies or if it includes a much broader set.

Administrative subpoenas are a powerful but unchecked investigative tool. Unlike with a grand jury subpoena or a court order, the government doesn’t have to explain to the courts why it needs the documents or what data it’s requesting from a company.

Since the start of the Trump administration, the federal government has used DHS’s administrative subpoena power to try to unmask anonymous online critics and pursue broad customer records about protesters. This includes, for example, a list of people in the Minneapolis-St. Paul area who bought a specific green beanie from REI.

Congress originally granted the use of 1509 customs summons to the then stand-alone US Customs Service nearly a century ago, with the idea that it would let customs officers inspect business records related to importing merchandise and figure out whether they owed customs duties. In 2003, Congress transferred that agency’s powers, including the ability to issue customs summons, to the newly formed Department of Homeland Security.

In the past, DHS has maintained that customs summons could be used only for specific purposes. The DHS inspector general admonished Customs and Border Protection’s Office of Professional Responsibility in 2017 for trying to use a customs summons to unmask an anonymous Twitter poster, pointing out that its own procedures manual said that customs summons could only be used to obtain information related to customs or immigrations laws.

That, however, has changed. The Trump administration recently argued in court that because the Customs Service’s powers were transferred to DHS following the passing of the Homeland Security Act, it can use that power to tackle its “broad investigative portfolio,” which it says includes enforcing laws “beyond legacy customs and immigration enforcement.”

“When we give law enforcement agencies powerful investigative tools, even with the best intentions and purposes to try to go after legitimate investigations and crime, you have to be really careful,” says Mackey. The newly reported filing is part of an ongoing Freedom of Information Act lawsuit filed by the nonprofit against ICE and DHS.

"The numbers reported to EFF amount to a dragnet domestic surveillance program by Trump's Homeland Security Department,” says US senator Ron Wyden of Oregon.

ICE did not respond to questions about why there’s been such a huge increase compared to prior years, but Mackey speculates that it may be because there is virtually no downside to filing them.

“The worst thing that will happen to ICE is that the service that receives it says it's not going to respond,” says Mackey. If that happens, ICE can just take the company to court to force it to comply, as it recently did with Google, or withdraw it and try to get the information it’s after by issuing a grand jury subpoena, as the Intercept reported it recently did with Reddit.

“Congress desperately needs to pass my bipartisan legislation to drastically curb federal subpoenas, including consequences for officials who abuse surveillance authorities,” says Wyden, referencing his proposed Subpoena Abuse Prevention Act. “Next year, I plan to use the Senate Finance Committee gavel to get to the bottom of HSI's unprecedented misuse of customs law to target Trump's enemies.”

Even without exact details about the 185,000 subpoenas HSI sent out, Mackey says their prolific uses still raises “significant concerns about an agency potentially being out of control and not being subject to any sort of internal oversight that respects people's First Amendment rights and their rights to privacy.”

big story: cool kids write in all lowercase—does that make you mad?

I worked for a whole day using only the iPhone Duo

Most of Gen Z isnt turning to porn, Feeld report finds

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

More than a third of men say romance novels, erotic fiction, or fan fiction have shaped what turns them on. Most of Gen Z isn't turning to porn when dating gets hard. And eight in 10 adults say the buildup is a huge part of what makes sex exciting. (So much for instant gratification.)

That's according to the new State of Dating report from Feeld, the dating app “for the curious” (including those interested in ethical non-monogamy, kink, and unconventional relationships), and honestly? I'm not the least bit surprised.

The report, titled Holding Out For a Slow Burn, was developed in collaboration with Dr. Justin Lehmiller, a senior research fellow at the Kinsey Institute, and Feeld's senior product data analyst, Sy Dusk.

AdultFriendFinder — readers’ pick for casual connections

Tinder — top pick for fast hookups

Hinge — popular choice for regular meetups

Pure — best for anonymous hookups

OkCupid — best app for inclusivity

Here’s what the report, with insights from 3,310 adults in the U.S. and UK, revealed about Gen Z’s dating habits and modern dating in general.

Across the survey, 80 percent of respondents said it was difficult to meet their sexual and romantic needs through IRL connections, and 73 percent agreed that “modern dating often feels like it’s missing something.” What’s missing? For 58 percent, it was authenticity and clear intentions.

Dating has been “optimized,” maybe too much. We have so many apps and algorithms, but is anyone really taking the time to put themselves out there and meet organically? Or are we retreating to our “romantasy” novels and TV shows just to feel something again?

“Dating apps can certainly make it harder for slow-burn attraction to develop because they encourage us to make snap judgments based on limited information,” Dr. Lehmiller tells Mashable. “And since there’s always another potential match just around the corner, it can be tempting to just keep swiping in search of an immediate spark instead of giving attraction time to grow.”

The survey also found that nearly 60 percent of Gen Z men and more than 70 percent of Gen Z women do not turn to porn when real-world intimacy feels difficult. And in a separate 2026 Kinsey survey conducted in collaboration with Playboy and Julie Care, 34 percent of young men and 53 percent of young women said porn is something they “never watch.” Guess those “goon caves” aren’t as popular as the internet wants us to think.

Digital intimacy is taking over the world one sad little phone screen at a time. According to the latest survey, 81 percent of respondents said getting close to someone through a screen plays a role in their intimate lives. That’s not all AI — texting, sexting, and video calls count, too. But for one in six, digital intimacy is their main way of exploring intimacy, and about one in 10 turn to AI companions when connecting in real life gets difficult.

Some respondents said they believed digital intimacy could help their real-world relationships (29 percent), and 30 percent said it offers a way to explore their desires and vulnerabilities.

So, about that slow burn. The survey found that eight in 10 adults agreed that anticipation and buildup are "crucial to erotic excitement."

There are several reasons we might need more time to get there, including financial stress, the lingering effects of living through a pandemic, political turmoil, and general uncertainty — it’s enough for anyone to say, I’m not in the mood.

The idea of love at first sight is, well, lovely. But attraction doesn’t always happen right away. Only 13 percent of people surveyed said attraction develops instantly, while 86 percent agreed that “attraction typically becomes stronger when two people create a shared story together through conversations, experiences, and future plans.”

Research cited in the report suggests that getting to know someone can give that spark a chance to grow. For example, when someone turns out to be “funnier than you expected,” that can be a turn-on for your brain — it can trigger what’s called a positive prediction error. Basically, you’re experiencing something even better than your brain expected, which leaves you wanting more.

Mistakes, or the little things that make us human, can also give us brain boners. But you usually have to like the person already for it to work. Seeing them spill their coffee or make an embarrassing admission can make them more endearing, not less. You get to see a little of who they are, unfiltered. And in a world where “looksmaxxing” is a thing, we could stand to be a little less polished.

“Our desire for a slow burn isn’t inherently incompatible with dating apps. But we need to be more intentional in how we use them,” says Dr. Lehmiller. “Instead of fast swiping in search of instant chemistry, we need to give people opportunities to surprise us and reveal new sides of themselves. That’s where the unexpected rewards lie, and where attraction has an opportunity to go beyond the surface.”

And so, we’ve been getting our fix through books, streaming series (Bridgerton, Heated Rivalry, and Off Campus, just to name a few), and even audiobooks and audio erotica like Dipsea and Quinn. Which I’m totally here for.

Media has become so entwined in our intimate lives that one survey respondent described his most intimate moment as “Watching TV and crying in my partner’s arm because the episode struck chords I was feeling because of a recent familial loss.”

Men are turning to romance media that “taps into fantasies of being desired, understood, and truly seen,” which usually (not always) isn’t shown in porn. In some surveys, men reported fantasizing about “passion, intimacy, and romance” more than the women did.

Before COVID, a Kinsey Institute survey found that 6 percent of men said romantic or erotic novels had shaped their sexual fantasies. In Feeld’s new survey, 37 percent of men said romance novels, erotic fiction, or fan fiction had shaped their desires. These aren’t identical comparisons, but the romance genre clearly isn’t just for women.

And “romance” doesn’t necessarily mean the same thing to everyone. Men in the survey were more likely to associate romance with physical affection and touch, while women were more likely to associate it with emotional openness and vulnerability.

That said, when asked about their most intimate non-sexual moments, men and women described similar things: cuddling, talking, being vulnerable, and really listening to one another.

We can’t all live in a romantasy novel. But surely we can make time for a little sexual tension…

“It is quite funny to think about translating epic fantasy romance arcs into daily life filled with heading to work, taking out the bins, remembering to floss and reply to friends, and being generally unglamorous,” Ruby Rare, a sex educator and Feeld’s intimacy expert, tells Mashable. “But there are ways of weaving this in, and it all comes down to intentionality.”

Her advice? Tell the person you’re seeing that you want to take your time.

“Once you've connected with someone and felt that shared glimmer of excitement for what might come next, be vocal about your desire for a slow burn as an antidote to the self-gratification that we’ve become so used to,” Rare says.

“This isn't about going full Victorian and making courtship chaste and timid; instead, lean into activities that will deepen connection and intrigue between you. Write love letters, go for long walks where you challenge each other to stay off your phones and be present with each other, make out for as long as possible before things escalate.”

‘Somewhere In The South’ Takes Top Award At TCCF Pitching; ‘Reunion Dinner’ Scoops Three Prizes

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tan Ce Ding’s Somewhere In The South won the top award in the Pitching section of this year’s Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF), the TAICCA x CNC Feature Film Grand Award, which comes with a cash prize of $35,000.

Produced by Edward Lim’s Malaysia-based Evil Genius Studio and Anthony Chen’s Singapore-based Giraffe Pictures, the project follows a young man in a sleepy southern Malaysian town who starts to lose his identity after he’s roped into wearing a tiger costume during a political campaign.

The prize was one of four “Grand Awards” handed out by TCCF organizer, the Taiwan Creative Content Agency (TAICCA), with the other three worth $30,000 apiece. In total, along with awards from sponsors, this year’s edition of the event has a prize pool of more than $305,000 (NTD10M). Related Stories News 'Our Blues', 'Midnight Diner' & 'C'Est La Vie' Among Projects Backed By TAICCA Joint Venture Funds News Taiwan's Mirror Fiction Hands Out $50,000 In Awards For Book To Screen Adaptations

Among the other Grand Prize winners, the TAICCA Series award was presented to French project Saga. Produced by Biz Productions and Elephant Story, the series revolves around four forgotten screenwriters who create an unlikely hit when they are thrown back together due to a regulatory quota for “human-authored content”. Watch on Deadline

The TAICCA x Weta FX Animation award went to France-Spain co-production Wilderness Of The Greenriver, a feature project written and to be directed by Ellis Kayin Chan; while the TAICCA Documentary award went to China-Taiwan collaboration A Day’s Worth, in which a filmmaker interacts with Chinese day laborers.

Among other awards presented by sponsors, horror film Reunion Dinner scooped the most amount of prizes with three awards: the MPA Asia Pacific Grand Award, the Videoland Ingenuity Award and the NAFF Award from Bucheon International Fantastic Film Festival. Directed by Taiwan’s Hung Tzu-peng, the project follows a hearing impaired high school girl who returns home for Lunar New Year and is forced to save her family from a deadly curse.

Other multiple award winners included two other Taiwanese feature film projects – Mirror Entertainment’s horror comedy Romance In Hell and Critical Hits Production’s Turns Out Its Not BL But BFF – which both won two prizes (see full list of award winners below).

Also winning two awards were series The 16th Night Kitchen (China, Japan) and Sunlight Delivery (Taiwan), as well as documentaries Saving Tuvalu (Australia, Germany) and Parachute Kids (Taiwan, US), with latter project following Taiwanese who were shipped off to the U.S. as children.

In TCCF’s Story section, which presented books, comics and other pieces of IP suitable for screen adaptation, Mirror Fiction’s The Three Witches Of Shetou won the TAICCA Award – Best Story and the friDay Video Original Story Award. Kevin Chen’s novel follows three estranged sisters who reunite during a festival in their hometown which dredges up family curses and buried secrets.

TCCF took place in the Nangang Exhibition Center in Taipei from November 6-9. The event kicked off with the signing of a partnership between TAICCA and New Zealand-based visual effects house Weta FX, aimed at strengthening Taiwan’s AI capabilities and content industries.

Also during TCCF, TAICCA hosted a showcase presenting details of recent investments from four content funds launched in partnership with local telcos and media entities, and on the last day announced the launch of a digital games fund with Lunta Ventures. See here for Deadline’s complete coverage.

UK Regulator Opens Crypto Permissions Route for Wholesale Banks Ahead of 2027 Rules

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: The Financial Conduct Authority has reminded wholesale banks that they can apply to extend their permissions into regulated cryptoasset activities under the UK’s new framework. Applications submitted by February 28, 2027 may qualify for continuity while decisions are pending, but the broader regime is not expected to begin until October 25, 2027.

For years, Britain’s crypto debate has centred on exchanges and specialist firms. The next phase also concerns the banks that already serve financial markets. The Financial Conduct Authority has put those institutions on notice that the route into regulated crypto services is opening well before the rules themselves take effect.

In its November 2 wholesale-banks supervisory update, the FCA said banks can submit a variation of permission to undertake activities brought inside the new regime. The regulator specifically named custody, operating trading platforms, dealing, arranging transactions, stablecoin issuance and staking as activities within the expanding perimeter.

The timing deserves attention. The application window opened on September 30, 2026. The FCA is encouraging wholesale banks to apply by February 28, 2027, ahead of the new regime’s planned start on October 25, 2027.

That early deadline has a practical purpose. According to the FCA, firms applying before February 28 should either receive a decision before the regime begins or, if their application remains under review, be able to continue relevant crypto activities while the regulator makes its decision. Applications made after February 28 but before the October start may fall under a different transitional arrangement.

It would be a mistake, however, to interpret the guidance as blanket approval for every bank to start running cryptocurrency services. Permission still depends on what an individual institution proposes, the relevant legal requirements and the FCA’s assessment. A variation to an existing permission is not the same as automatic approval for every newly regulated activity.

For established banks, assembling that application can be substantial work. Custody design, operational controls, consumer or wholesale protections, sanctions screening and governance all need to fit the activity. The regulator has not suggested these safeguards are optional because the applicant already has a banking licence.

Banks bring customer relationships, compliance teams, payments systems and balance-sheet expertise. If more of them begin offering regulated digital-asset services, a business may eventually be able to access familiar institutional infrastructure rather than work solely through crypto-native intermediaries.

Yet bank participation can also make the market more selective. A bank may accept only certain assets, counterparties or forms of custody. Its willingness to trade or settle tokenized instruments should not be confused with broad support for every token listed on an exchange.

The policy sits alongside efforts to connect financial assets to blockchain infrastructure. NewsBTC has examined the plans to develop infrastructure for tokenized securities, an example of the operational plumbing institutions assess before moving real transactions onchain.

A credible application must explain more than growth potential. Banks will need to show how responsibilities are divided, which assets are supported, how client holdings are safeguarded, and what happens when systems fail or markets become disorderly.

That creates a useful test for the market. The UK has been working toward a clearer framework, but clarity matters only if firms can implement the requirements and supervisors can process applications. February 28 is therefore an operational deadline as much as a policy marker.

The next developments to watch are the number and quality of applications, eventual decisions and any further guidance from the FCA. Wholesale-bank involvement could become an important part of Britain’s crypto market. At this stage, though, the news is that a formal route is open—not that a wave of new bank crypto products has already arrived.

This article was written by the News Desk and edited by Samuel Rae.

The ‘Blue Wave’ Swept Top Homebuilding States. What Happens Next?

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On Tuesday, the governor’s offices in five states were swept up by the “blue wave,” as Democrats were elected to succeed current Republican leadership.

Iowa, Georgia, Nevada, Ohio, and Texas all flipped the governor’s seat from Republican to Democrat, after Democrats campaigned heavily on issues of affordability.

Of those five states, four rank in the top 15 on the Realtor.com® state Affordability & Homebuilding Report Cards for 2026, after making strides to expand housing supply under GOP leadership.

The report cards rank states on a points scale based on two major factors: affordability, such as the share of the state’s median income required to afford its median-priced home, and homebuilding, whether states are producing enough new housing to keep up with demand.

With affordability sticking out among the most important voter issues this year, how the new Democratic administrations improve, or at least maintain, states’ housing markets will be vital to whether they’re deemed successful.

Democrat Rob Sand was selected by the voters of Iowa in an 11-point victory over Republican Zach Lahn. He will  take over the position from outgoing Republican Gov. Kim Reynolds.

Iowa was ranked second in the 2026 state report cards, earning a total score of 75.8 out of 100 for a grade of A. However, Iowa’s strength lies in the affordability aspect of the grading scale, not homebuilding.

The state’s median listing price sits at $282,886, which calls for just 25.4% of the $75,991 median income, giving it a Realtor Affordability Score of 0.96.

The downside comes in its homebuilding numbers. Iowa accounts for just 0.94% of the country’s issued building permits. While that number is on par with population—Iowa accounts for a 0.95% share of the country’s population—the state’s 56% new-construction premium indicates that builders are focused on larger, more expensive homes, rather than entry-level inventory and affordable builds.

In his “Truer Better Agenda,” Sand acknowledged that Iowa needs to build 21,000 more homes by 2030 to address the state’s housing shortage and improve affordability. Within his platform, he states his intention to work with the Legislature to ensure more homes, and more types of homes, are built.

“We need to cut the bureaucratic red tape that often stands in the way of that by simplifying the permitting and inspections process to reduce administrative time, redundant inspections, and costs for Iowa families,” reads Sand’s agenda.

Beyond building, Sand’s platform includes protecting renters from price gouging and removing interest from Iowa’s down payment and homebuyer assistance programs.

Jay Iverson, executive officer at the Home Builders Association of Iowa, said they have already been meeting with Sand to work on improving the regulatory process in the state, but the newly elected governor will face his share of challenges.

“He’ll still have a Republican-led House and Senate, so they’re going to have to figure out a way to get along. We fight the regulatory issues all the time, code updates, and all of the things that add significant costs,” Iverson told Realtor.com. “So we’re meeting with him even currently, before he’s sworn in, and hoping to deliver on that message of regulatory relief.”

Iverson said he’s looking forward to seeing Sand’s administration work with other governmental bodies to make improvements for Iowans, especially as the two parties come together.

“It’s just going to be a different environment. He sold himself during the campaign on, 'We should all be able to get along, let’s figure this out,’ and I think we’re going to hold him to that,” said Iverson. “I’m looking forward to that part, just seeing what actually happens and how it works. It’s never easy.”

Texas saw a major upset on Tuesday, when the state elected a Democratic governor for the first time since 1991.

Gina Hinojosa, who will make history as Texas’ first Latina elected governor, denied incumbent Republican Gov. Greg Abbott a fourth term with her 7-point victory.

Texas is a consistent front-runner in the Realtor.com state report cards, coming in at No. 4 in 2026. The Lone Star State received a score of 71 out of 100 for a grade of A-.

Its success in the rankings is due to Texas being the nation’s largest homebuilder, accounting for 14.6% of all building permits issued in the country. This far outpaces any other state and is well above the state’s 9.3% population share.

The new-construction premium in Texas is only 7.6%, indicating that builders are adding affordable and competitively priced homes to the state’s inventory.

However, the state struggles slightly with affordability, with the median listing price of $364,749 taking up 32.5% of the state’s median income, giving it a Realtor Affordability Score of 0.66.

Housing has long been a priority of Hinojosa, who has been fighting against multinational corporations and predatory landlords since she was elected to the Texas House of Representatives in 2017. In 2025, she authored a bill that would prevent institutional investors from snapping up more than 10 single-family homes.

As governor, she intends to keep fighting corporate investors and price gouging that keep affordable housing and homeownership out of reach of Texans, according to her campaign.

The Texas Association of Builders feels positive about Hinojosa’s incoming administration and continued bipartisan efforts toward housing affordability in the state.

“She served on the Land and Resource Management Committee in the House this session and was very involved in a lot of the housing discussions and legislation on density, the need for more housing, and increasing housing supply, and was supportive of a number of these efforts during session,” TAB CEO Scott Norman told Realtor.com. “She was campaigning on some of these affordability issues on housing, so we’re excited to work with her and her new team.”

Norman also noted that the Texas Legislature has shown bipartisan efforts to rebuild its skilled workforce and expand training programs on every level, from public schools to trade schools and higher-education institutions.

“To get more people into these jobs that are AI-proof and that are lifetime careers that people can make a great living and own their own business, that’s something that has been very bipartisan,” said Norman.

This week, Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms made history when she was elected Georgia’s first Black governor and the nation’s first-ever Black female governor.

The former Atlanta mayor captured 54% of the vote against her GOP opponent, Rick Jackson, and will take over from term-limited Republican Brian Kemp.

In the state Affordability & Homebuilding Report Card from 2026, Georgia ranked No. 12, receiving a B for its score of 63 out of 100.

Georgia’s affordability comes in far behind Iowa, with its considerably higher median listing price of $392,112 and slightly lower median income of $75,118. The state comes in with a Realtor Affordability Score of 0.68.

It’s homebuilding, however, is far more significant. Georgia accounts for 4.2% of the country’s building permits, behind only Texas, Florida, California, and North Carolina. The share of permits outpaces Georgia’s share of the nation’s population, which is 3.31%.

During her time as Atlanta mayor, Bottoms raised more than $700 million and helped build or preserve more than 7,000 affordable housing units, according to her campaign. She also introduced the City of Atlanta Housing Affordability Action Plan, which included a road map for increasing housing inventory.

As governor, she plans to create a Georgia Housing Fast Track initiative to further streamline permitting and establish an affordable housing trust fund to assist in the development of housing. Additionally, her platform includes expanding down payment and renter assistance programs throughout the state.

Democrat Amy Acton is the first woman to be elected governor of Ohio, beating Republican Vivek Ramaswamy by 8 points. She will replace term-limited Republican Gov. Mike DeWine.

Ohio earned the No. 15 spot on the 2026 state affordability report card. It received a C+ for its score of 59.4 out of 100.

The state comes in with a healthy Realtor Affordability Score of 0.88, on par with the leading state of Indiana’s 0.89. Its median listing price of $277,348 is reasonable compared to its median income of $70,196.

Of all building permits in the U.S., Ohio accounts for 2.32% of them, slightly behind its share of the nation’s population, which is 3.48%.

The real issue for affordability in Ohio is the 84.6% premium on newly constructed homes compared to existing homes, which indicates that builders are catering to a more expensive market, rather than adding affordable homes to the state’s inventory.

Like the candidates above, Acton’s housing platform centers on removing red tape and streamlining the permitting process to make both new building and the renovation of older properties easier. She also plans to expand the Welcome Home Ohio program, created by DeWine, which provides grants and tax cuts for rehabbing older homes. She wants an expansion to encourage more new construction as well.

Placing restrictions on private equity firms and corporations buying up homes and providing support for first-time buyers are also among Acton’s housing plan, according to Columbus Underground.

The outlier on this list is Nevada. While the rest of the states rank in the top 15 states for affordability on the state report cards, Nevada comes in at No. 34 with a grade of C- for its score of 45.8 points out of 100.

Nevada accounts for 1.3% of the country’s total permits, slightly above its population share of 0.96%. Newly constructed homes come with an 18.5% premium compared to existing homes, which is not outlandish for this list.

The real struggle for Nevada is affordability. The state’s median income is $74,855, on par with No. 2 Iowa, but its median listing prices tower above at $491,936 for a Realtor Affordability Score of just 0.52.

Democrat Aaron D. Ford, the incoming governor, has his work cut out for him in the homebuilding sector.

The former Nevada attorney general held an 8-point victory over Republican incumbent Joe Lombardo on Tuesday  to become the state’s first Black governor.

Ford has laid out a number of pathways through which he intends to make housing more affordable to Nevada residents. Removing red tape and delays in permitting, developing vacant and underused land in the Las Vegas Valley, and expanding support for first-time buyers are among his top priorities.

Ford has also expressed that he will push back on recent tariffs imposed by the Trump administration that have made homes more expensive to build and purchase.

“Nevada families are being forced to absorb higher costs caused by tariffs on essentials like aluminum, steel, lumber, and even basic fixtures like bathroom vanities,” said Ford in this “Affordable Nevada” plan. “A $1,000 increase in the price of a new home pushes an estimated 115,000 households out of reach of homeownership—a price spike working families cannot afford.”

Allaire Conte contributed to this report.
