How the New Urbanism took on sprawl and won

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It’s 1996. You just moved to a suburb of Atlanta, to a roomy house on a quiet street. Every morning at dawn, you kiss your family goodbye, and then hop in your car to drive 35 miles to your office in the city. It takes more than an hour to get there. 

You have plenty of company on the road at that hour, as you’re one of the more than 600,000 people who moved to the northern outskirts of Atlanta over the last six years. Between the traffic and constant construction, the swelling suburbs are starting to show their fault lines.

Its rapid growth during the 1990s makes Atlanta the quintessential story of suburban growth gone awry, though it is far from alone. Between 1950 and 1990, the share of Americans who lived in suburbs jumped from 23% to 46%. Decades of post-World War II expansion had transformed the American landscape from one built around small-scale neighborhood development and center city density to tabula rasa growth on the edge. Families moved to cul-de-sac subdivisions spread across car-dependent exurbs. These places were not so much cities as they were lily pads of domesticity, linked together by steadily growing interstate highways.

For many Americans, a suburban lifestyle was an outward signal of success: big house, good schools, safe streets. But that wasn’t the case for everyone. In the 1990s, at the peak of suburban sprawl, a small but vocal group of detractors began to take shape. Comprising progressive urban planners, designers, architects, and policymakers, this group coalesced around the idea that there should be an alternative way to live—one that embraced the charms of small town centers, the convenience of walkable neighborhoods, and the diversity of mixed-use and mixed-income districts. 

These people would come to be known as the New Urbanists.

In 1996, the New Urbanists wrote a 27-point charter that laid out its core principles for better city planning. At a gathering in Charleston, South Carolina, a line of 262 people ceremoniously walked up onto a stage and signed their names to the charter, legitimizing once-fringe ideas as a new kind of rule book for neighborhood design and city building. Signatories included architects, urban planners, transportation engineers, and government officials, from unknown environmental activists all the way up to the U.S. secretary of Housing and Urban Development (HUD).

Over the past 30 years, the charter and its principles of planning and design have gone on to radically change the face of cities and communities across the U.S. and around the world. New Urbanism has grown into a nonprofit organization, the Congress for the New Urbanism, and a movement that’s changed the way cities are planned and built, upending rigid zoning laws, eliminating parking minimums, and bringing walkability back into the hearts of communities. 

The objectives of New Urbanism still resonate today, and have been gathering new currency in the face of worsening housing affordability and growing disappointment with mainstream urban design priorities. The abundance movement, inspired by Ezra Klein and Derek Thompson’s book Abundance, calls for a proactive approach to economic and social development that can affect everything from the supply of housing to renewable energy access. Similarly, a recent push by Stripe’s cofounder, Patrick Collison, and the libertarian economist Tyler Cowen aims to spur the creation of “new aesthetics” that can change the course of cities and the architecture within them. Post-Great Recession, post-COVID-19 pandemic, there is an evident hunger for change.

Anyone now arguing for reimagining cities can take lessons from how New Urbanism emerged and rose to prominence. Three decades after the historic charter signing, Fast Company spoke with the people who were most involved in creating the New Urbanism movement—all still alive, all still working—about how such an unlikely crusade took hold. In a rare retrospective, they look back on how their movement took shape, and the legacies of sprawl that New Urbanism is still fighting today.

Roots of a design movement

The New Urbanism didn’t appear out of nowhere. Its roots grew out of a mix of disillusionment and optimism that started taking shape in the 1970s. At that time, a small group of young planners and architects were starting to feel fed up with the built environment. They believed that many of the social and environmental issues cities faced were the result of development patterns that prioritized economic returns over the experiences of the people who lived there. 

With highways expanding to reach new subdivisions and new subdivisions arising alongside new stretches of highway, the traditional logic of urbanization was being supplanted by a perverse pattern of growth for the sake of growth. How people lived in these places, what they did there, and why they’d even want to be there were seemingly irrelevant. The environmental costs of populating these areas and keeping them going were afterthoughts at best.

A group of young designers believed development needed a more comprehensive, interdisciplinary, and thoughtful approach, and they set out to show how it could work. They included the Miami-based urban planners Andrés Duany and Elizabeth Plater-Zyberk, the Los Angeles-based architects Elizabeth Moule and Stefanos Polyzoides, and a regional planner in California named Peter Calthorpe. Now all in their 70s and 80s, their paths began crossing after coming out of various architecture and design programs in the 1970s and working as architects and planners across the country.

“We began practicing when the collapse of suburbia became evident. The promise of suburbia, which was plenty of green, plenty of parking, highways that really work, was betrayed,” says Duany, cofounder of Duany Plater-Zyberk & Co. (now DPZ CoDesign). “You didn’t get nature. You got a whole bunch of traffic congestion in commuting, and all the great parking fields were miserable places. So there was a feeling of betrayal in the air and we were able to perceive it.”

After studying under the influential architectural scholar Vincent Scully, Duany and Plater-Zyberk graduated from Yale University in 1974 and moved to Miami, where they cofounded the architecture firm Arquitectonica, and then, in 1980, Duany Plater-Zyberk & Co., a firm focused on counteracting suburban sprawl. 

Calthorpe had also studied at Yale but left in the mid-’70s before graduating to work under the California architect Sim Van der Ryn, who in 1975 was appointed state architect by Governor Jerry Brown. During Brown’s administration, Calthorpe worked on a new plan for downtown Sacramento built around a burgeoning light rail system, an early example of transit-oriented development, which would become Calthorpe’s specialty.

Meanwhile, Moule and Polyzoides had started an architecture firm in Southern California centered on bringing traditional architectural approaches to housing beyond the typical single family home, designing courtyard homes and clusters of housing that would, decades later, become known as “missing middle” housing.

“All of us were working on parallel paths from the mid-’70s on,” Calthorpe says.

Those paths began to intersect in the 1980s as the designers’ alternative takes on urban and suburban development garnered more attention. An early standout was Seaside, a Gulf Coast resort town in Florida planned by Duany and Plater-Zyberk and founded in 1981. The pastel-toned walkable village-like development, later made famous as the set of the 1998 film The Truman Show, is considered the first New Urbanist community. 

“We were architects who were networking. There was a lot of travel to give lectures in the world of architecture about the latest things people were working on. And we realized there were people around the country that were heading in the same direction,” says Plater-Zyberk.

In 1986, Calthorpe and Van der Ryn published a book called Sustainable Communities, which outlined emerging elements in the planning and urban design fields that were changing the shape of cities. It included case studies of places that were designed to reduce resource consumption and lessen reliance on cars, as well as a focus on utopian-leaning new town development. Later, Calthorpe was teaching a course on transit-oriented development at the University of California, Berkeley, with the architect and urban designer Dan Solomon, who would later become one of the founders of New Urbanism, and they invited Duany to participate in a symposium on new forms of suburban development.

Slowly, the individual and varied work of these practitioners would intermingle. “We were working on different sides of the same coin,” Calthorpe says.

The founders coalesce

By the beginning of the 1990s, the future founders of the New Urbanism movement joined forces. Their first real meeting was around a plate of pasta.

During Calthorpe’s time working under Governor Brown, California had enacted new energy efficiency and building standards, and created a statewide organization called the Local Government Commission to spread best building practices out to all the jurisdictions across the state. Run by Judy Corbett, a pioneer in sustainable development, the commission had a role that was interpreted broadly. Around 1990, Corbett saw an opportunity to tie energy efficiency to larger neighborhood planning, land use, and transportation issues.

“Judy called me up and said, ‘I want to get some design principles that are clear and firm, but something I can pass around to elected officials throughout the state of California,’” Calthorpe recalls. Duany and Plater-Zyberk were in California working alongside Moule and Polyzoides on the Los Angeles redevelopment project Playa Vista, so Corbett and Calthorpe organized a meeting. They saw the chance to use California’s push for energy efficient buildings as an opening to get more people thinking about ways growth and development should change. “We all met in Judy’s kitchen in Davis, California, and had a pasta dinner and talked about shaping some kind of movement,” Calthorpe says.

“I don’t know that we thought so much as being a rebellion as much as feeling like we were profound outsiders and the forces in front of us that were creating the cities that we live in were remarkable,” Moule adds.

This led to Corbett organizing a Local Government Commission-led conference at Yosemite National Park’s historic Ahwahnee hotel in 1991, bringing together architects, planners, transportation engineers, government officials, and policymakers from across California. Calthorpe, Duany, Plater-Zyberk, Moule, and Polyzoides helped drive the discussion.

Moule recalls that during the meeting they created a rudimentary series of tenets called the Ahwahnee Principles that helped define their thinking at different scales, from the building block to the street, neighborhood, district, and region. The principles called for planning “complete and integrated communities,” with a variety of activities, housing types, and jobs, and designs that conserve resources and minimize waste. 

“We knew that if we put a little bit more meat on the bones and fleshed them out a little bit more, and then gathered people together to see what they thought about it, then we might be able to get somewhere,” Moule says.

The group agreed that the Ahwahnee Principles should be considered a starting point for a large-scale rethinking of the developer-led, piecemeal urban development patterns of the previous decades. “We were talking about it and saying, you know, these principles are right but they’re not going to get us there. We need more detail. We need to be more specific,” Plater-Zyberk says. “It was one of the things that pushed us to make our own group that was urban design oriented, with greater detail or depth than planning and much more specific goals.”

Around this time, Duany was having discussions with the architect Léon Krier. A harsh critic of modernism, Krier was the future master planner behind the New Urbanist town of Poundbury, England, initiated in the late 1980s by Prince Charles (now King Charles III). Krier, who died in 2025 at 79, impressed on Duany how modernist architects had had such a deep influence on 20th century architecture and urban planning through a formal organization called Congrès Internationaux d’Architecture Moderne (CIAM), founded in 1928. He suggested the anti-sprawl movement needed its own formal organization.

“I was aware of just a few colleagues that were on the same page. And we basically called them,” Duany says.

Calthorpe says Duany proposed a group that would “repeal and replace” CIAM. “They had congresses and they issued a charter. So we thought, well, we’re going to do exactly the same thing. We can just cut and paste, take out the bad shit and put in the good,” Calthorpe says.

New Urbanism’s first congresses

Around 1992, Duany, Plater-Zyberk, Moule, Polyzoides, and Calthorpe gathered in New York, along with a planner named Peter Katz, who was working on a book about new approaches to urban planning and design (The New Urbanism: Toward an Architecture of Community, published in 1994). The six discussed the overlapping intentions of their design and planning work, and what it would look like to bring a more interdisciplinary approach to creating a more livable urban environment.

“We started with nothing, just fumes,” says Polyzoides, cofounder of Moule & Polyzoides Architects and Urbanists, and now dean of architecture at the University of Notre Dame. “There was so much to learn. There was so much to unveil, really, because the subject is so deep.”

From their work, they knew that reimagining the built environment would be a complicated undertaking. Interrelated forces shape the world as we know it, from land use planning to neighborhood design to traffic engineering to natural resource consumption. Making wholesale change hinged on understanding and reforming how all those interrelated pieces fit together. They walked away from the meeting with a plan to break everything down to three scales: the regional, the neighborhood, and the building. They also came up with a name for themselves: New Urbanists.

Following the model of CIAM, they decided to hold a series of congresses, or concept-focused meetings where the group could collectively debate exactly how each scale of city-making should be redirected. With the Congress for the New Urbanism formally established, Duany and Plater-Zyberk led the organization of the first congress in 1993, focused on the neighborhood level and set inside a columned meeting hall in Alexandria, Virginia, called the Lyceum. Each of the founders invited people to participate. 

“It was really coming out of our Rolodexes. That I even said Rolodex probably gives you an idea of the time frame,” Moule says. “Instead of it being sort of a board meeting, we decided to make it a larger call to arms.”

About 200 people were invited, including planners, landscape architects, traffic engineers, civic activists, and developers. People made presentations about their experience and projects. “It started out as a very diversely disciplined group who were all working on the same kinds of issues,” Plater-Zyberk says.

The next congress, held in 1994, was organized by Moule and Polyzoides at the Biltmore Hotel in downtown Los Angeles, and focused on the building block and the street. “That was quite provocative at the time, largely because Los Angeles was known for being the home of the automobile and the home of suburban sprawl,” Moule says. “It was sort of a stick-it-in-your-eye kind of conference.”

Calthorpe and Solomon organized the third congress in San Francisco in 1995, focused on the regional level, with an emphasis on their work planning transit-oriented development projects. The founders cast an even wider net for attendees, inviting participants from groups like AARP, the Urban Land Institute, the Trust for Public Land, and the Natural Resources Defense Council, as well as heads of departments of transportation, mayors, and government officials. “Unlike CIAM, which was only architects, we really wanted to bring in every last person that we thought had some kind of say in how the urban environment was created and made, so it was pretty diverse group,” Moule says. “It might have had 1,500 people. So there was momentum.”

Organized like conventional conferences, these three congresses had the typical hallmarks of presentations, panels, post-meeting dinners, and post-dinner parties. But unlike most conferences, where the event ends and everyone goes home, attendees were trying to build a movement—and set a new standard for neighborhood design and city building.

“People were doing things and coming back and talking to each other about the way they did it,” Polyzoides says. “We were constructing the theory out of experience, not constructing it out of theory.”

A charter signed, and an early win

New Urbanism’s fourth congress was held on May 4, 1996, in a rented hall in Charleston, South Carolina. The concepts and ideas generated through the previous three annual gatherings had been consolidated down into 27 principles, each roughly 25 to 50 words, and broken into three categories:

  • The metropolis, city, and town
  • The neighborhood, the district, and the corridor
  • The block, the street, and the building. 

The charter, written primarily by the founders, was presented to the invited audience. With a large white paper mounted on an easel in the center of the stage, the attendees were invited to add their signatures to adopt the charter. Though there are no records of how many people were in attendance that day, 262 signed the charter and New Urbanism became an official movement.

“It was signed dramatically. Everybody walked up on stage to sign it,” Plater-Zyberk says.

The charter signing was considered a starting point for New Urbanism, and an opening of the invitation-only organization to a broader membership. And with so much of the American landscape dominated by disconnected concentrations of single family homes or office parks or big box retail clusters, people were ready to get behind an alternative. 

By the end of 1996, CNU would claim more than 500 official members. Attendance numbers would rise with each annual congress, as did interest in the organization’s anti-sprawl sentiment. The organization’s openness and its challenges to the status quo brought attention that grew into a movement.

“We do not protect the ideas or even the use of the term New Urbanism. It’s totally freeware,” Duany says. “You can’t just get a Harvard degree. You can’t just get LEED [Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design] certification. Well, we say these are the principles called New Urbanism, don’t mangle it and get on with it.”

Sounds straightforward, but the principles called for a widespread rethinking of the accepted practices of the day, and systemic changes in the financial infrastructure of real estate development. “Segregated land use was something that was so baked into the system. You had to change how appraisals were done. You had to change how banks lent on buildings,” Moule says. “They wouldn’t lend you something if you wanted to put retail on the ground floor and housing or offices on top. It just didn’t happen.”

Some were skeptical of New Urbanism from the start. Writing in Harvard Design Magazine in 1997, the urban geographer and Marxist scholar David Harvey called out the movement as naive, arguing that it “connects to a facile contemporary attempt to transform large and teeming cities, so seemingly out of control, into an interlinked series of ‘urban villages’ where, it is believed, everyone can relate in a civil and urbane fashion to everyone else.” The head of Harvard’s Department of Urban Planning and Design in the late 1990s and early 2000s, Alex Krieger, was another critic of the movement and its emphasis on designing new communities with traditional styles instead of fixing the communities that already existed.

Not long after its official birth, New Urbanism got the chance to do just that. The HUD secretary Henry Cisneros, the highest profile signatory of the Charter of the New Urbanism, ended up enabling one of the movement’s biggest successes through the HOPE VI program. This was a HUD effort that launched in 1992 to undertake a large-scale redevelopment and revitalization of the country’s public housing projects. Throughout the late 1990s and early 2000s, CNU members and the nonprofit CNU organization itself worked directly with HUD to establish the design guidelines for the program, transforming decaying public housing barracks and towers into mixed-income neighborhoods.

The work spread across more than 260 sites in cities like Atlanta, Boston, Los Angeles, Pittsburgh, San Francisco, Charlotte, North Carolina, and Washington, D.C., and resulted in the redesign of 100,000 units of public housing. Decaying housing towers and bleak low-rise housing clusters were replaced with walkable neighborhoods that blended into their context and erased the line between public housing and the rest of the city. “Most of the members of the early CNU were very involved,” says Calthorpe. “That was really the beauty of the thing. Unlike the architects who are constantly competing with each other, we just wanted to collaborate.”

Research led by Harvard University’s Raj Chetty and released in 2026 found that children growing up in the neighborhoods revitalized through the HOPE VI program earned more money as adults, were more likely to attend college, and were less likely to be incarcerated.

“HOPE VI made policy of the idea that the design of community can have a profound influence on people’s lives,” says Mallory Baches, the current president of CNU. “It takes 30 years to do a longitudinal study on the effects of those projects, but 30 years later we have the evidence that that early influence made a big difference.”

Edging into the mainstream

Over time, many of the main principles of New Urbanism have steadily moved from the fringe of city planning and development to the mainstream. Mixed use and higher density development, and the zoning reform they tended to require, have become commonplace policies in cities across the U.S. Walkable town centers, infill development, alternative transportation, an emphasis on local economies, form-based codes, and transit-oriented development are all normal elements of many cities and their approach to growth and development. (The movement has even spawned a partly serious progressive urbanist online community called New Urbanist Memes for Transit-Oriented Teens.)

“There’s been enormous success,” says Polyzoides. “It is impossible to imagine that in a continental country like ours, you could move within a generation from people doing crap everywhere to doing the right thing on so many fronts when it comes to urbanism.”

New Urbanism and the CNU continue to affect city planning, urban development, policy, and governance, but the movement hasn’t risen to CIAM’s level of modernist influence on the shape and form of the built environment, nor has it thoroughly changed de facto development industry practices.

“New Urbanism has had a big effect, but there’s still the large companies that are buying land to build a single unit-type subdivision in the old-fashioned way,” says Plater-Zyberk. “And there’s too much of that going on. But under the current affordability crisis, in some cases, people are trying to make more of that happen.”

Calthorpe argues New Urbanism is essentially a progressive movement, with roots in the counterculture of the 1960s, and its modest uptake in the planning and development world has a lot to do with the politics of the current era. “In the end, sprawl just kept happening and we were more of a footnote or counterpoint. We never became normative in America,” he says.

One challenge that’s dogged the movement is its aesthetic lean. Style has always been a lightning rod within New Urbanism, with some embracing the movement’s goals but balking at its tendency toward classical or neotraditional architectural approaches. Plater-Zyberk recalls the issue of style being one of the biggest points of contention ahead of the charter signing back in 1996. “That was a very sensitive point,” she says. 

This aesthetic approach has recently taken on political undertones, as President Donald Trump has controversially made traditional architecture the preferred style for federal buildings.

Polyzoides, who is dean of one of the most traditionally-focused American architecture schools, rejects any connection between New Urbanism and Trump’s architectural choices, calling them an aberration. “What ties this particular president to the Mussolinis and the others in the world is they see classical architecture as an opportunity to exert power. But what classical architecture is about at its source is an architecture of community,” he says. “It’s an architecture of common expression, common ideals. So, an architecture of democracy.”

The future of the New Urbanism movement may hinge on larger concerns, and on bringing younger people into the fold. Baches, who now leads the organization, says she’s focused on helping younger generations engage with the principles and ideals set out by the founding generation of New Urbanists, many now at the tail end of their careers.

In 2023, CNU initiated a process of amending the original Charter of the New Urbanism. The amended charter now includes principles around increasing diversity, equity, and inclusion; countering involuntary displacement; ensuring the attainability of housing; and contextualizing the built environment’s role in climate change. These new principles, she argues, will keep the movement relevant into the future, while also making the output of New Urbanism—a better alternative to sprawl—even more attractive.

“Communities across the country have so much more of an appetite for great urbanism, but there’s a limited supply. And until we have created more, or adapted sprawl into better urbanism, the attainability of living the kind of quality life that great urbanism can provide is going to be only available to those who can afford it,” she says. “We need to expand access to that great urbanism.”

CNU’s 35th congress will take place in June.

Cadillac Is Bringing GM’s Super Cruise Hands-Free Driving System To Europe

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

Super Cruise, General Motors’ hands-free driving assistance system, is heading to Europe, where it will be available starting in mid-December on a handful of Cadillac EVs.

The 2025 Optiq, as well as the 2026 Lyriq and Vistiq electric crossovers, will be able to do semi-automated driving on more than 180,000 kilometers (111,000 miles) of compatible roads across 16 European countries beginning at the end of the year. Germany, France, and Switzerland are among the first European countries where the system will go live.

Super Cruise, which is already available in the United States, Canada, China, and South Korea, uses pre-mapped roads, real-time cameras, radar sensors, GPS data, and adaptive cruise control to enable hands-free driving. That said, the system is still considered a Level 2 driver assistant, according to the Society of Automotive Engineers, so it doesn’t make the car run autonomously, and the driver still needs to pay attention at all times when driving.

A driver monitoring system makes sure that the person behind the steering wheel is alert and can take control at any time, if needed.

When active, Super Cruise displays a green light bar at the top of the steering wheel. The system keeps the vehicle centered in the lane and maintains an appropriate distance from the car in front, without the driver having to touch the steering wheel or pedals. It can do lane changes, but the driver has to initiate the maneuver by activating the indicator.

The feature can be used on compatible roads, including highways and dual carriageways, at speeds of up to 130 kilometers per hour (81 miles per hour), including in heavy stop-and-go traffic.

Cadillac has integrated Google Maps into the navigation map display, which shows roads where Super Cruise can be used and how much of a trip can be driven hands-free. The automaker says the driver assistance feature is designed to make driving more comfortable and convenient—so drivers shouldn’t expect to be able to take a nap while on the road.

“Super Cruise brings together Cadillac’s premium driving experience, connected services and advanced driver assistance technology to make everyday journeys more comfortable,” said Maximilian Wittich, Managing Director, Digital Services and Experiences. “We are excited to bring this hands-free experience to customers in Europe and to offer technology that has been tested and adapted for European roads and requirements.”

GM said it did a lot of performance testing and validation, as well as regional route testing and Europe-specific software tweaks to make Super Cruise compatible with European legislation and driving conditions.

Since debuting in 2017, Super Cruise has delivered over a billion hands-free driving miles, GM said. In North America alone, there are roughly 750,000 Super Cruise-enabled vehicles, but the American auto giant estimates that the number of users will exceed 850,000 by the end of the year.

In the U.S., over 20 GM models are compatible with Super Cruise. The system is included for free for the first three years from the date of first registration. When the free trial ends, owners need to pay for a Super Cruise subscription and a Connected Services plan to keep using hands-free driving.

What teams AI thinks will be in the EFL Championship in the 2050/51 season

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It's always fun to predict how things will look in 20, 50 or even 100 years time, but how will the EFL tables look in 2050?

The Championship is ever-changing. The likes of Lincoln City and Wrexham have made their returns in recent years after decades away, while certain established Premier League clubs are always threatened with dropping into the second tier, too.

Predicting how things will look in the 2050/51 campaign, or in 24 seasons, is almost impossible. The second tier in the 2002/03 season saw Portsmouth win the title and be promoted alongside Leicester City and Wolves, but the likes of Gillingham, Walsall and Grimsby Town were all alongside them in the division.

So, which clubs does AI believe will be in the Championship come 2050? FLW has consulted ChatGPT to find out.

In an ideal world, Birmingham City would be in the Champions League, rather than the Championship, in the future, given the ambition of owner Tom Wagner and the amount of investment put into the Blues side in recent years.

That ownership and investment may not be there in 24 years, though, which could lead to this project crashing and burning.

By 2025, Birmingham will be more than settled in their new 62,000-capacity 'powerhouse stadium', but AI feels that it will be hosting second-tier football.

Blackburn Rovers are one of the longer-serving Championship clubs right now, with this their ninth successive campaign since they were promoted out of League One.

Recent years have seen them bounce between relegation battles and play-off pushes, and under Tony Mowbray, they look to be pretty steady. AI feels that they'll level out back in the second tier come 2050.

Bolton Wanderers' seven-year exile from the Championship came to an end this season, with their long-awaited promotion from League One serving as the final stage of their fightback from near liquidation back in 2019.

The passionate Trotters fanbase will be hoping that the coming years will see them become an established second-tier outfit with the prospect of making a return to the Premier League, and perhaps they will do that in the next couple of decades.

But by 2050/51, AI feels that Bolton will once again be a Championship side.

The first non-current Championship side in the list is Bradford City. The Bantams haven't been a second-tier side in over two decades, but AI feels that they will be in the next 24 years.

Many felt that the West Yorkshire outfit could have gotten into the Championship next season, and they still could, but the departure of Graham Alexander has definitely wounded them, and new boss Michael Duff still has a lot of work to do to get fans onside.

But this is a well-supported club on the rise, and even if it isn't this season, that promotion from League One doesn't seem too far away.

Bristol City being a Championship club at any point feels inevitable. This year marks 18 of the last 20 seasons spent at this level, so perhaps 24 years on from this term, they'll still be finishing 12th in the second tier.

The Robins are at the start of a project led by Michael Skubala, off the back of a summer with some decent investment in promising players across the field, most of whom have come from League One.

If all goes well, perhaps Bristol City could exceed this expectation and be a well-run Premier League side?

Cardiff City haven't historically been a side famed for sticking around in one division. Since the 1974/75 season, the longest stint they've had in one league was a ten-year one in the Championship before their promotion in 2012/13.

Their last second-tier run lasted just six years before relegation in 2024/25, and they answered that with promotion back at the first time of asking.

With Brian Barry-Murphy at the helm, the focus right now is on going back up again, but there'll be no doubt there will be a different direction and another man at the helm come 2050.

Bluebirds fans hope that they'll be able to take on what they hope is a Premier League job after BBM leaves, but AI sees them back in the second tier by 2050.

Charlton Athletic are in a bit of a sticky situation in the Championship right now, failing to win any of their last 11 games and losing four straight. Nathan Jones is praying that Burnley don't get any better anytime soon, else they risk falling into the bottom three.

24 seasons is a long time. Perhaps there's a drop back into League One on the horizon for the Addicks, but ChatGPT at least believes they'll get over that hump and get back into the second tier.

Derby County are another side in real peril at this moment in time. They're also a team that has become synonymous with the Championship over the past couple of decades.

Since being relegated from the Premier League in 2002, 24 years ago, they've spent one year back in the top flight, two in League One, and 21 in the second tier. Given the Rams have been a play-off side in quite a few of those seasons, too, it's quite an achievement for them not to have gone up more than once.

So, in the coming 24 years, it makes sense to put Derby in amongst this Championship lineup.

The second non-current Championship side in the 2050/51 lineup is a club many expect to be a second-tier outfit in 2027/28.

Huddersfield Town have enjoyed a brilliant start to life under Martin Drury, dominating both the attacking and defensive metrics. They have a duo in Joe Taylor and Derensili Sanches Fernandes who have 25 goal involvements between them, and they sit atop League One, looking less and less likely to relinquish that position anytime soon.

With ambitious owner Kevin Nagle overseeing things, he'll be hoping to be a Premier League regular by the time he's ready to relinquish control. By 2050, though, AI feels that the Terriers will be back in the second tier.

Both Bradford and Huddersfield will be joined by West Yorkshire rivals and the first current Premier League side on the list, Leeds United, which will make for some tasty matchups over the year for sure.

The Whites put in a brilliant showing in the second half of last season to retain their top-flight status, and after starting this year on the front foot, some Leeds fans are expressing some frustration at the number of draws their side are seemingly settling for.

Again, 24 years is a long time, so perhaps Daniel Farke can turn things around again and bring the Yorkshire giants back to being European contenders. But the German won't be in charge come 2050, and ChatGPT believes that they'll have slid back into the Championship by then.

Nvidia Board Investigates Jensen Huang After Report He Was Target of Chinese Spy Effort

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia Board Investigates Jensen Huang After Report He Was Target of Chinese Spy Effort

Nvidia's board of directors has opened an independent investigation following reports that CEO Jensen Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort, the company said on Sunday.

A committee of independent directors, assisted by outside counsel, will oversee the inquiry. "While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information," it said, adding that it "has confidence in Jensen and his leadership team."

We just issued a press release from our board of directors. Read the release: https://t.co/W9yXWJzbkk

— NVIDIA Newsroom (@nvidianewsroom) November 8, 2026

The investigation follows a Financial Times report on Saturday that a U.S. government memo, written earlier this year based on intelligence information, said the Nvidia CEO had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based Nvidia customer Megaspeed, citing people familiar with the memo. U.S. intelligence believes she was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security, the FT reported. Nvidia declined to comment to the newspaper on the memo. Huang's email to staff, sent on Sunday, did not name the person with whom he had an affair.

Lawmakers from both parties are seeking answers. Republican leaders have asked Huang to appear privately before the House China, foreign affairs and intelligence committees, according to the FT, while Sen. Chris Coons (D-DE), who will chair the Senate Foreign Relations Committee from January, pledged in a tweet that "Congress will investigate and get answers."

The FT's report built on a New York Times investigation last month, which said U.S. intelligence agencies had concluded that Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, a Chinese citizen, was a Chinese intelligence asset, and that surveillance of her had revealed a close relationship with Jensen Huang. President Donald Trump was briefed on the findings roughly a year ago and chose not to act on them, according to the Times.

U.S. officials suspected Megaspeed of helping Chinese companies get around restrictions on advanced AI chips, and the Commerce Department's investigation into the company has been handed to the Justice Department, the Times reported. Megaspeed's sister company in Malaysia bought at least $4.8 billion worth of AI equipment containing Nvidia chips between June 2024 and February 2026, according to trade records cited by the newspaper.

Nvidia told the Times that Huang's interactions with Ms. Huang were "not unusual or unexpected," and that customers like Megaspeed receive "ordinary business information." Megaspeed said it had retained outside counsel to conduct an internal investigation, Ms. Huang did not respond to the newspaper's requests for comment, and China's embassy in Washington dismissed the allegations as "groundless smears and slanders."

In an email to employees on Sunday, Huang apologized to his family and colleagues. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," he wrote, according to a copy published by CNBC.

"Regarding the national security concerns raised in news reports, NVIDIA's Board of Directors has initiated an independent investigation. I support the investigation and will cooperate fully," Huang wrote. "I am committed to protecting NVIDIA and supporting America's national security." He asked for privacy for his family.

"Today's news will not slow NVIDIA. Jensen is committed to continuing to lead NVIDIA as CEO without interruption," the company told CNBC.

Nvidia shares were down 5% at $177.32 in premarket trading early on Monday, after closing at $186.64 on Friday, according to Yahoo Finance.

Zelda Switch 2 restock: Live restock updates at Walmart, Target, and Best Buy (Nov. 9)

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If your current morning ritual involves violently hitting refresh on a dozen retail tabs, you already know the vibe: finding the Nintendo Switch 2: The Legend of Zelda 40th Anniversary Edition is turning into an Olympic sport.

Between automated bot armies scooping digital drops, surprise order cancellations at Walmart, and inventory vanishing in seconds on Amazon, this custom black-and-gold console feels like a myth. But while digital storefronts look completely barren, stock is actively circulating behind the scenes.

Before getting into the restock details, bookmark these retailer pages to check for surprise drops:

Nintendo Store — Out of stock right now, but you can add it to your wishlist to get pinged the second a restock hits.

Amazon — Dropping tiny batches of consoles randomly throughout the day.

Walmart — Stock exists but only for third-party listings selling at way over market price, so make sure your in-stock email alerts are turned on.

Target — Sold out online, but two drops went live in-store on Thursday, Nov. 5, and online on Friday, Nov. 6.

Best Buy — Sold out online, but select stores ran midnight openings on Nov. 5 with walk-in stock.

GameStop — Online orders are off; you will have to walk into a store to find one.

The feeding frenzy is being supercharged by Nintendo’s staggered release calendar. While the custom Zelda 40th Anniversary hardware dropped first on Oct. 29, the flagship game driving demand — The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time for Nintendo Switch 2 — arrived on Thursday, Nov. 5.

The remake rebuilds the 1998 classic from the ground up with modernized controls, a fully orchestrated soundtrack, reworked dungeon puzzles, and expanded Hyrule environments. Since the $520 console doesn’t include the game, everyone is rushing to lock down a system before launch day.

The package includes the special-edition Switch 2 system sporting a custom black-and-gold finish, themed Joy-Con 2 controllers, and a matching dock decorated with the gold Triforce emblem. It retains the platform's signature flexibility, letting you switch between TV, Tabletop, and Handheld modes whenever you want. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Inside the themed chassis, you get 256GB of built-in storage (expandable with microSD Express cards), a vivid display in handheld mode, faster load times, and support for up to 4K resolution on compatible TVs when the console sits in the dock.

The magnetic Joy-Con 2 controllers add mouse-style tracking in supported games, while the new GameChat hub lets you voice chat and share your screen directly as you play. It also plays your older Switch games and supports standard multiplayer options.

Landing limited-edition Nintendo hardware takes a little prep work, but these battle-tested habits will give you a real edge over the bots:

Save your checkout details everywhere — Log in to Target, Best Buy, Walmart, and the Nintendo Store to save your shipping address and default card. Stopping to type a card number or billing zip code during a live drop is how you lose an order.

Hands off the refresh button in Best Buy's queue — When an online drop goes live, Best Buy puts shoppers into a randomized waiting line to filter out scrapers. Touching the reload button resets your spot and boots you straight to the back.

Watch for Walmart+ early-access paywalls — Walmart frequently locks headline console drops behind paid Walmart+ subscriptions for the first 30 to 60 minutes. If you have a membership, keep that tab signed in; if not, wait for the general public window to unlock.

Dodge third-party sellers — Double-check the seller before buying. Marketplace scalpers frequently list units for jacked-up prices; make sure it says "Sold and shipped by [Retailer]" at the standard price.

Skip the web wars and head to physical stores — Online checkout bots grab website stock in under 15 seconds. Showing up in person is easily your most reliable path to securing one without paying scalper markups.

Stores like Walmart, Amazon, and Best Buy often hold onto popular gaming hardware to use as headline deals during Black Friday and Cyber Monday week.

With that said, Black Friday restocks are usually the hardest ones to catch. You will be fighting website crashes, gift shoppers, and automated bots all at once. If you can grab a console during the Nov. 5 launch drops at Target or Best Buy, take it. Think of Black Friday as a backup plan, not your main bet.

Console hunting sucks right now, period. But between the surprise micro-drops and Thursday's physical store restocks, you still have real shots at retail price — just be ready to move the second that button turns yellow.

The Latest Shift in How Appraisals Are Handled Could Save Homebuyers Hundreds in Closing Costs—Eventually

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It’s typically the last hurdle for homebuyers—and the process is about to get a face-lift.

Last week, the Uniform Appraisal Dataset (UAD) 3.6, a major update to property valuation standards introduced by Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac, became mandatory for all new UCDP appraisal report submissions.

UAD 3.6 is a new, digital form that appraisers will use to evaluate homes. Now, rather than the appraiser meticulously jotting down notes in longform paragraphs, the new system will allow appraisers to use extensive drop-down menus and checkboxes to make their evaluations.

A proper appraisal can determine not only whether a buyer can qualify for a loan, but also whether they can save significant money at closing.

After a buyer makes an offer, their mortgage lender will order an independent appraisal to confirm the home is worth at least what's being borrowed against it.

If the appraised value comes in at or above the purchase price, the process moves forward as planned. But if it comes in low, that gap has to be resolved before the loan can close.

The logic behind the UAD 3.6 update, according to Fannie and Freddie, is to create a “data-driven, dynamic reporting structure to capture property and market information.”

This new form includes significantly more data fields, helping lenders and buyers better understand the property’s characteristics.

But with more detail comes a longer commitment.

“Be prepared for a lot more questions from the appraiser during the inspection,” explains Michael Mignogna, president of the Appraisal Institute.

“There’s a lot more data to include, so be prepared to answer questions about renovations, repairs, new systems like heating and air conditioning, appliances, etc., or have that information ready if no one with that type of information can be at the inspection.”

To that end, with UAD 3.6, appraisers will now not only grade individual rooms, but they can also highlight upgrades and features that might’ve been lost in the old system. So, features of a newly remodeled kitchen or new bathroom flooring get specifically noted, which will aid in the ease of deciding the home’s value.

Still, Mignogna is skeptical that this will have much of an impact on the final value.

“Not as much information is always known about the comparables, so expectations about condition/quality adjustments to the comps on a room-by-room basis should be tempered,” he adds.

The buyer almost always pays for the appraisal. The cost is typically a few hundred dollars, and it’s often rolled into closing costs. This is the case even though the appraiser is hired by the lender, not the buyer.

The hope is that because this new appraisal process is a clean, digital checklist, banks can review and approve it much faster, reducing delays.

For example, under the former process, lenders had the option to kick back the report for corrections if anything about the report was unclear, which could happen if the appraiser’s report didn’t cover every facet necessary.

When that happens, buyers may find themselves having to pay for expensive mortgage rate-lock extensions to keep their rates while they wait for the appraisal to clear.

Extension fees typically range from 0.125% to 0.250% (or up to 0.500% or more) of the buyer's total loan balance per extension period, often broken into 15-day increments.

On a $450,000 mortgage, a 15-day extension would usually cost between $500 and $1,000.

Given that closing costs already run a buyer anywhere from 2% to 5% of the total loan amount, or about $8,000 to $20,000 for a $400,000 loan, according to Bankrate, another couple hundred to a thousand dollars is nothing to scoff at.

But as the saying goes, all good things come with time, and right now, this new digital infrastructure may actually be coming at a cost to buyers as it finds its footing.

Clear Capital, a real estate valuation technology company, reported via National Mortgage Professional an average $65 increase in appraisal fees in markets where it has enough production volume to compare UAD 3.6 with the older UAD 2.6 format.

Additionally, Michael Farrell, president of the National Association of Mortgage Brokers, said that the number of field appraisers equipped to do UAD 3.6 is so limited at this point, demand bottlenecks are driving up prices for modernized reports. While older appraisals are being priced at around $550, modernized reports may cost borrowers closer to $850.

“It’s basically all roads lead to chaos,” Farrell explained to Scotsman Guide.

“From an originator standpoint, it's a delay. Delays cost money. Delays cost the consumer money.”

Mignogna, meanwhile, says it’s important to remember that fees are affected by far more than just the process.

“Appraisal fees are very specific to markets and are affected by the number of appraisers in a market, the demand for appraisals in that market, interest rates, and a host of other factors,” he says.

Having said that, the transition to the UAD 3.6 format continues to be slow and Mignogna concedes that lenders may bid up prices for the few field appraisers who are fully onboarded like in any “supply-demand situation.”

But while delays are happening as appraisers go through a “transition mode,” Mignogna says this won’t affect buyers' need for expensive mortgage rate-lock extensions.

“This is really only meaningful or significant in times of interest rate uncertainty or expected rate increases,” he says.

“The overall UAD 3.6 process is longer right now for the reasons above, but as the learning curve flattens, turnaround times will improve. It remains to be seen if the increased data requirements offset by UAD 3.6 familiarity gets us to the turnaround times seen before UAD 3.6 or faster turn times.”

With that in mind, it’s a good idea to ask your loan officer upfront if their specific appraisal management company has fully integrated the new digital system into their team.

While the UAD 3.6 mandate took effect on Nov. 2, 2026,  lenders may continue to submit revisions for existing UAD 2.6 reports through May 19, 2027.

Her work has also appeared on MSN, Yahoo News, and BlogHer. She is a proud graduate of Emerson College in Boston and is originally from New Jersey.

Nvidia stock falls 4% as scrutiny turns to Jensen Huang’s relationship with alleged Chinese spy

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia shares were down around 5% in premarket trading Monday after the tech giant launched an investigation into CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with a businesswoman who intelligence agencies say is a Chinese spy.

U.S. intelligence agencies said Alice Huang, an executive at cloud company Megaspeed, has been passing information to Beijing, according to reports in The New York Times and The Financial Times.

Nvidia said its board has launched an independent investigation following the reports that Huang was the target of a "foreign intelligence effort."

Huang "fully supports the review and has committed to cooperating with it," the company said in a Sunday statement.

The co-founder and CEO told staff in an email that he had "fallen short" of his responsibility to serve his colleagues, the board, partners and shareholders around the world.

"I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry," Huang said in the email.

China's Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun said on Monday the "so-called Chinese spy narrative" was unsubstantiated, Reuters reported.

Nvidia was last trading down 4.4% in premarket trading.

The key question for investors will be whether Huang will remain as CEO, according to Dan Ives, senior managing director at Yorkville Ives & Co.

"This is a black eye moment for Jensen and will spark multiple congressional inquiries at a minimum and will create an overhang on the stock for a myriad of reasons," Ives said in a Sunday note.

"There is clearly risk that Jensen could be removed as CEO depending on the Board investigation and this will add risk to the stock as he is so important to the Nvidia story and strategy globally. This will also increase major scrutiny regarding selling chips to China which remains a key battleground issue in DC," Ives added.

Tech stocks took a hammering last week as investors reassessed the data center build-out after Democrats won control of Congress.

Asian tech stocks rose across the region on Monday, rebounding from Friday's losses. Shares of U.S. chipmakers were slightly lower in premarket trading, with Intel, Marvell, Qualcomm and Arm all down over 1%.

Joachim Klement, investment strategist at Panmure Liberum, said the impact is currently contained to Nvidia.

But if Huang's position is "endangered," then "Nvidia might cut back on supporting other companies, whether it's in Asia or in the U.S., that might then spread a little bit more into the other semiconductor names," he said.

U.S. President Donald Trump on Sunday praised Huang's leadership but said he hadn't spoken to the CEO about the investigation.

"I think he's an honorable guy. I think he probably has an excuse for it. You have to hear it," the president told reporters.

"He's done an amazing job for our country," Trump added.

Shaun Maguire, partner at Sequoia, also defended the tech giant CEO, saying in a post on X that Huang is "one of the greatest entrepreneurs of this generation."

He added: "two mistakes don't make a right, and I believe Jensen is irreplaceable for America's AI leadership."

SpaceX and Tesla CEO Elon Musk reshared Maguire's post, adding that he agrees with the venture capitalist's statements.

Nvidia reports third-quarter earnings on Nov. 17 and will hold a major conference in Washington, D.C., at the end of the month.

‘You’re the Bacon’: A blunt warning to Realtors about AI

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NAR reports 23% of agents use AI daily and 25% weekly, while 12% do not use it and do not plan to. NAR’s Mike Woodward highlighted risks tied to free tier data use, hallucinations, and potential misrepresentation from AI edited listing photos.  AI Summary

More and more real estate professionals are turning to AI tools to automate rote tasks, manage their client relationship management systems and generate marketing collateral.

According to the National Association of Realtors’ (NAR) 2026 Realtors Technology Report released in September, nearly half of agents now use AI daily (23%) or weekly (25%), while 31% are experimenting occasionally. Only 12% said they are not using AI and do not plan to, down from 32% who had not yet tried AI in 2025.

And while AI may help agents save time or create more sophisticated looking marketing, as Mike Woodward, NAR’s director of data science, put it, quoting Spider Man “With great power, comes great responsibility.”

In a session at NAR’s 2026 NXT conference on Sunday, Woodward explored what AI can do for real estate professionals and the housing industry, as well as what it could do to agents and the industry if not used without proper caution and guardrails.

Woodward began by exploring the business model used by AI companies, because, he argues, you cannot understand the risk until you understand how AI companies make their money.

“There ain’t no such thing as a free lunch,” he says, before reaching for a darker metaphor. Two pigs in a barn admire the free shelter and the great food — right up until breakfast. “For those of you who had bacon this morning, you know how that story ends. You’re the bacon.”

As he put it, large language models are “voracious beasts” for data. Feed a free service your documents and it can ingest them, learn from them and surface what it learned to the next user.

Due to this, for anything real or confidential, pay for the service and tick the data-retention opt-out boxes, which stop the model from keeping or training on your inputs.

If the business model is the first trap, the overconfidence of LLMs is the second. Woodward warns against treating AI like “the Oracle of Delphi” — gods walking among us, dispensing truth.

To prove the point, he ran the same kinds of queries his clients run. He asked Copilot about supermarkets and schools near a Massachusetts town; it took the question too literally, omitting stores and schools in neighbouring towns. He asked Claude to pull up records for his home and five of the seven facts came back wrong.

The lesson for agents, he said, is less about the errors themselves and more about their clients.

Buyers now arrive armed with AI-generated impressions of a town or a property and it is in the agent’s interest to know what those buyers think they know.

Woodward also sought to dispel the belief that one AI model is better than the others, noting that agents need to simply pick out and learn how to make it work for them and their business. In addition to this, he also noted that there were no fancy tricks for prompting, instructing agents to “talk to it like a child”: short sentences, simple words, clear instructions.

But AI isn’t all just doomsday scenarios, according to Woodward, who noted that there were many low-risk and genuinely valuable use cases.

Some of these uses included asking Claude to create a plain-English summary of soil-analysis report and provide a step-by-step plan to fix the issues. He also used AI to create a listing description from a property fact sheet, a task that he said might take an agent 15 minutes, but done in seconds instead.

Additional applications he feels are low-risk include using AI to research companies, build charts and generate presentations that real estate professionals can use as a jumping off point.

The caveat running through all of it: on free tiers, use dummy data, and never feed in anything confidential. A performance review he rewrote for a fictional ex–Air Force employee was his cautionary tale — exactly the kind of sensitive, personal text that should never go near a free or overseas-based model.

Fixing technical faults like exposure, white balance or a tilted horizon are things industry professionals have long done using tools like photoshop. But corrections like these are now accessible to everyone thanks to AI regardless of their proficiency with photoshop.

Corrections like these, according to Woodward are fine, but removing temporary blemishes or even inventing property features like adding a fireplace, moving a window or dropping in a swimming pool can cross firmly into misrepresentation, a breach of the Realtor Code of Ethics.

But even when making image corrections, Woodward warned that sometimes the AI will make changes you never ask for.

That is the real hazard, he argues, especially given that the image goes out under the agent’s name.

“You are responsible,” he said. “You cannot say the dog ate my homework.”

The backlash, he warns, is already here: a recent front-page New York Times article in which real estate professionals “did not look good,” and pages of angry posts on Reddit. State legislatures and New York City are weighing regulation. And AI-detection tools, he believes, work well.

“If you use AI and you don’t confess to it, you’re going to get caught,” he said. “People are going to get sued for what I’m going to show you. Consumers are white-hot angry.”

But there is a legitimate version of all this, which Woodward said his lawyers were the ones to note: showing a buyer how a property could look. Virtual staging can turn an empty rental’s “prison cell” walls into something welcoming. The condition is disclosure, noting that agents should show the original alongside the edited image and label the edit clearly as AI-generated.

For Woodward one of the most important takeaways for agents is that they should pay for the tools they want to use when working with confidential information. In addition he said they should reread the code of ethics, ensure they understand the laws coming to their state and when uncertain consult with their legal counsel.

What’s Fogging Your Brain? Signs of Aging vs. ADHD

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cognitive changes may stem from perimenopause, ADHD, or early dementia, each of which requires distinct treatment.

Q: How do I know whether hormonal changes related to perimenopause are causing my cognitive function problems, or my ADHD is getting worse, or I might be experiencing mild cognitive impairment or early dementia?

ADHD and mild cognitive impairment (MCI) exhibit clinically different cognitive deficits that help me differentiate one from the other. While ADHD symptoms involve forgetfulness, inattention, and distractibility, MCI has specific cognitive symptoms of word-finding challenges, misspellings, and the use of generic terms like “stuff” and “things” because of word-finding difficulty. Also, MCI is progressive over two to three years, whereas ADHD doesn’t generally worsen.

In perimenopause, a decline in estrogen leads to cognitive difficulties. In contrast to MCI, we know that the cognitive changes from estrogen decline are not the result of neurodegeneration.

However, the specific deficits related to declining estrogen haven’t been well differentiated from those associated with ADHD. So if you have pre-existing ADHD and you experience heightened cognitive difficulties in perimenopause and menopause, you may think, “My ADHD is getting worse.” But it’s likely not worsening ADHD. It’s declining estrogen.

Okay, so what do you do about it? There is a paucity of research for clinical guidance. Hormone replacement therapy (HRT) was not always recommended because it was alleged to increase risk for breast cancer and other illnesses. That claim has now been refuted by the most recent research, which has led to the removal of the black box warning from the FDA on HRT product labels. And so, if I have a female patient with ADHD who is 50 years old and says, “For the past two years, I’ve had cognitive decline,” the question to consider is this: “Do I increase her stimulant medication or do I put her on HRT?”

I am inclined at that point to put her on HRT for three months, keep her stimulant medication dosage stable, and monitor the qualitative changes in her cognitive functioning. If there is improvement on HRT, then the question becomes, “Does she stay on HRT for some extended period of time along with the stimulant medication?” If the HRT doesn’t work or she can’t take HRT for medical reasons, then I might increase the stimulant medication dosage to see if we can’t overcome the perimenopausal changes from the estrogen decline. This advice comes from my clinical experience, and it is unfortunate that we don’t have more research for clinical guidance here.

[Take This Self-Test: Is It Perimenopause or ADHD?]

Q: Could ADHD stimulant medication mask symptoms of mild cognitive impairment?

There is very limited research on stimulants and mild cognitive impairment (MCI). My clinical impression is that stimulants likely improve symptoms of MCI, but do not take that as an endorsement to seek treatment for MCI with stimulants. There is emerging research on MCI and stimulants that has not yet been published. The follow-up question to that is, if you have ADHD and MCI, will treating the ADHD delay the development of MCI or Alzheimer’s? There is no clinical research for me to answer that question. But if this were me, I’d be treating my ADHD, keeping my fingers crossed, and appreciating the fact that the quality of my life on medicine is better than it would be without my ADHD treated.

ADHD should not get worse; however, the experience of ADHD may be worse if life’s demands start to increase and exceed your ability to compensate. However, if you’re having cognitive changes that are beyond the scope of your ADHD, you may have two neurologic processes going on: your ADHD and something else. In this case, your ADHD may, indeed, be getting worse because you have a second neurological process that needs to be evaluated.

David W. Goodman, M.D., is an internationally recognized expert on adult ADHD.

Addleshaw Goddard to Deploy Harvey Firmwide and Co-Develop Legal AI

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

London-headquartered Addleshaw Goddard will provide Harvey’s legal AI platform to more than 3,400 lawyers and business services professionals across Europe, the Middle East and Asia from January, alongside a co-development program covering legal work and client delivery, Harvey announced on November 9, 2026.

Addleshaw Goddard’s Innovation Group will work directly with Harvey’s product and legal engineering teams. Lawyers, business services professionals and clients will help shape and test priority capabilities, establish consistent adoption practices and develop new AI-enabled services, making product feedback and client involvement part of the rollout itself.

Addleshaw Goddard said the collaboration followed a three-month assessment led by its 80-person Innovation Group, which drew on a decade of experience developing and deploying AI and legal technology. During the assessment, 400 partners, lawyers, legal technologists and business services professionals tested leading platforms on real legal and business tasks.

From the outset, AG practitioners worked with Harvey’s product and legal engineering teams to test emerging capabilities and provide detailed feedback on complex legal work and delivery at pace and scale. Managing Partner Andrew Johnston described the agreement as the firm’s most significant technology transformation and said the co-development element takes it beyond a firmwide deployment.

The announcement presents Review Tables as an early result of that work. Harvey’s product team and AG’s Innovation Group further developed the feature, which helps lawyers organize, compare and verify large volumes of information on complex matters. AG’s feedback shaped source checking, preservation of lawyer-reviewed work, sharing and performance, and the firm received early access to AI-led features for creating and updating tables at scale.

A Harvey engineering post published October 22, 2026 explains the underlying workflow. Lawyers can ask natural-language questions across uploaded documents, with rows representing individual documents and columns mapping to questions. Each answer cell includes the model’s reasoning and citations from the source document, allowing reviewers to inspect the material behind an answer.

Rows can be assigned to collaborators, answers verified or locked, and comments added for discussion. Once verified, a table can serve as a reusable, validated source for a later memo or presentation. Those controls let large teams divide review work and retain checked answers instead of recreating the analysis.

Scale has also imposed an engineering constraint. Harvey engineers said loading complete tables in the browser caused lag and, in the largest cases, failures after tables exceeded thousands of rows. Their redesign fetches ordered rows page by page while preserving sorting and filtering, so a large table no longer has to load every row into the browser at once.

Addleshaw Goddard and Harvey plan to meet quarterly with selected clients to identify shared challenges, explore new approaches to legal service delivery and, where appropriate, co-create solutions. The sessions extend the collaboration beyond internal adoption by bringing client requirements into the same co-development model.

Kerry Westland, partner and head of the Innovation Group, described the agreement as a decisive next step in the firm’s buy-and-build AI approach. She identified better outcomes, greater quality and consistency, increased capacity and new ways of working with clients as opportunities, while emphasizing that “human judgement, supervision and accountability” should remain central.

The collaboration also sits within AG2030, the firm’s international growth strategy, in which innovation is a central component. Its practical test is to turn co-development and client input into consistent legal services across the workforce while preserving the professional oversight Westland described.

NYU Langone’s PREVIA Flags PTSD Risk From Emergency Narratives

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NYU Langone Health announced on November 9, 2026, that its PREVIA AI ensemble analyzed emergency-department trauma narratives to estimate one-month post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) risk, reporting 95.4% sensitivity for the best configuration in a 145-patient study. The experimental screener is designed to identify at-risk patients without adding a lengthy mental-health questionnaire to emergency intake.

The Nature Communications paper, also published November 9, reports 78.5% AUROC and 46.8% precision across three cross-validation folds and calls for validation in larger, independent cohorts. Sensitivity measures the share of later PTSD cases detected, precision measures the share of flagged patients who actually developed PTSD, and AUROC summarizes how well the scores separate the two outcomes. That precision means many positive screens would be false positives, potentially adding follow-up staff time, patient burden and resource-allocation costs, although the next step would typically be a brief assessment rather than invasive intervention.

PREVIA is designed around a practical gap: only about 28% of US emergency departments routinely screen for PTSD, and only two-thirds of those use validated questionnaires, according to the study. Standard assessments compete with immediate medical needs when clinician time is typically around 15–20 minutes per patient. The first month after trauma is a critical identification window because many patients are no longer in contact with the hospital by then.

Senior author Katharina Schultebraucks, co-director of computational psychiatry at NYU Grossman School of Medicine, said: “PREVIA can serve as an AI screener that identifies at-risk patients and explains why, without adding any time to intake in busy emergency rooms.”

Research coordinators asked patients at two New York City level-1 trauma centers to describe the event that brought them to the emergency department. Participants were enrolled within 24 hours of trauma and given up to three minutes to respond while an iPad recorded video. Narratives averaged about 374 words. Whisper transcribed patient speech, and native English-speaking research staff reviewed the transcripts. One-month PTSD outcomes were determined using the Clinician-Administered PTSD Scale for DSM-5, a clinical diagnostic interview.

PREVIA divides each transcript across three layers. In the first, Llama and Mistral independently assess early post-traumatic stress symptoms, reactions during the traumatic event, risk factors and protective factors. DeepSeek reconciles their eight assessments into structured summaries, while the two models’ numerical scores are averaged with equal weighting. A final assessor combines the summaries with age, sex, trauma type and PTSD prevalence information to produce a risk score from 0 to 100 and a short explanation.

The pipeline uses zero-shot prompts, with no parameter training or fine-tuning on the study sample. Cross-validation was used to optimize and evaluate the classification threshold.

The paper reports broadly comparable overall performance between PREVIA and single zero-shot configurations of O3, Llama and Mistral on many metrics, with PREVIA’s clearest advantage in sensitivity. For subthreshold PTSD, meaning clinically meaningful symptoms below the full diagnostic threshold, PREVIA reached 89.7% sensitivity and a 69.4% F1 score, a combined measure of precision and sensitivity.

The study reports a separate comparison of 30 randomly selected narratives, stratified to include nine PTSD cases, in which PREVIA recorded 100% sensitivity and 84.4% AUROC. One emergency clinician recorded 88.9% sensitivity and 83.9% AUROC; both recorded 50% precision. The clinician had more than 24 years of emergency-medicine experience and specialized PTSD research experience. The single-rater design leaves open whether the comparison generalizes across experience levels and clinical settings.

Participants were English-speaking, medically stable adults aged 18–80, and women accounted for 31% of the sample. Medically unstable patients were excluded, potentially omitting some patients at highest risk of chronic PTSD. The authors identify testing in other languages, longer-term PTSD trajectories, comorbidities such as depression, and integration with clinician judgment and emergency workflows as future work.

The authors propose a clinical workflow that pairs a brief audio recording with those demographic and trauma variables from the electronic health record, then returns a risk score and explanation without additional clinician input. The study did not establish that such screening improves patient outcomes. PREVIA ran in a HIPAA-compliant high-performance-computing environment on NVIDIA A100 GPUs. Self-hosting supports control over patient data, but adoption depends on hospitals having suitable computing and governance infrastructure.

The public PREVIA repository provides the complete pipeline and prompts for open-weight models that run locally, plus five fabricated narratives for an end-to-end demonstration. Identifiable clinical narratives and psychiatric assessments remain restricted for privacy and consent reasons, so the demo can test operation but cannot reproduce the paper’s quantitative results.

PREVIA’s immediate contribution is an independently testable screening approach built from a routine emergency encounter and clinician-assessed outcomes. Its clinical value now hinges on independent validation: whether larger cohorts preserve its sensitivity, whether services can manage the false positives, and whether hospitals can incorporate recording, follow-up and secure computing without disrupting emergency care.

Pierre Fabre Taps XtalPi’s AI and Robotics for Drug Discovery

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Laboratoires Pierre Fabre and XtalPi announced on November 9, 2026 an agreement to discover novel small-molecule clinical candidates across multiple research programs. XtalPi will contribute integrated AI capabilities and a robotics platform, while Pierre Fabre will select and advance compounds in oncology, potentially dermatology, and rare diseases.

The partners are directing that work toward challenging and historically hard-to-drug targets, which they associate with significant unmet medical need and substantial opportunities for innovative drug discovery.

That focus gives context to the companies’ stated goal of accelerating the identification of high-quality clinical candidates. The emphasis on both quality and speed means the intended output is compounds strong enough to become clinical candidates in areas where discovery has been difficult.

XtalPi will handle de novo design, meaning the creation of new molecular structures, and deliver novel small-molecule therapeutics against undisclosed targets. This places its integrated AI capabilities and robotics platform at the front end of the partnered discovery process, with the resulting molecules moving into Pierre Fabre’s selection work.

Pierre Fabre will apply expertise in disease biology, discovery, translational research, and clinical development to select and advance the most promising compounds. It will also be responsible for subsequent clinical and commercial development. The agreement thus gives Pierre Fabre continuity across the selection decision and the later development path, while XtalPi’s defined responsibility remains the design and delivery of the underlying molecules.

The acceleration goal expressly spans multiple programs in oncology and potentially other therapeutic areas. That scope gives XtalPi’s platform several research settings in which its designs can be assessed using Pierre Fabre’s disease-biology and translational expertise.

The agreement includes an upfront payment, multiple milestone payments, and downstream economic participation.

For Laboratoires Pierre Fabre, the agreement is a strategic addition to its R&D roadmap and strengthens its drug-discovery capabilities. Francesco Hofmann, head of pharma research and development at Laboratoires Pierre Fabre, described XtalPi’s AI expertise as “a critical addition to our R&D Data and AI roadmap and to our discovery engine.”

For XtalPi, the collaboration advances its effort to apply AI and robotics to de novo drug discovery. The two stated rationales are complementary: Pierre Fabre is adding an AI-enabled discovery component to its research roadmap, while XtalPi is applying its platform within partnered therapeutic programs that have a defined route into clinical and commercial development.

The consequential test sits at the handoff between molecular design and development. For targets that have historically resisted drug discovery, the partnership’s value depends on whether XtalPi’s designed molecules become compounds Pierre Fabre considers promising enough to advance, and ultimately whether they meet the companies’ goal of high-quality clinical candidates in areas of unmet medical need.

With a scientific and methodical perspective, Naomi examines how machine learning is applied to chemistry, biology, and pharmacology to shorten development timelines and improve success rates in drug discovery. A Former Programmer’s AI Reality Check: What Changed Me From an AI Skeptic into an AI Believer, The High Costs that Many Got Wrong, and Opportunities I’m Investing In

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Terms and Restrictions Apply Physician on FIRE has partnered with CardRatings and other partners for our coverage of credit card products.
