Editorial Disclosure: Opinions, reviews, analyses & recommendations are the author’s alone, and have not been reviewed, endorsed, or approved by any of these entities.

Ian Ippolito, a former professional software developer, is an investor and serial entrepreneur. He founded Private Investor Club and The Real Estate Crowdfunding Review, where his five-part series on AI and investing begins here.

For years, I dismissed AI as hype. Then in February 2026, it finished in a few hours the Monte Carlo program for our family’s finances that had defeated it for months. Since then many have been surprised at how expensive and scarce AI is turning out to be. Here’s what I found, and how it’s changing where I invest.

(Disclaimer: I’m an investor sharing my personal opinion. I’m not your attorney, accountant or financial advisor, so consult your own professionals before making any financial decisions.)

Before starting companies, I worked as a professional software developer. Later I founded and sold vWorker (formerly Rent a Coder), an early online marketplace for hiring programmers.

Today I run Private Investor Club, where accredited investors share due diligence on private investments.

So when AI companies claimed their tools could write software, I tested the claim myself. In the fall of 2025, I used AI to help build a Monte Carlo simulation for our family’s finances: the kind of program that runs thousands of what-if market scenarios, which many of you have used to test your own retirement plans.

It was miserable. The AI forgot what it had done a few minutes earlier, ignored instructions and broke code that already worked. After a few months of workarounds, I gave up.

By December 2025, I expected AI to join the long list of technologies that never lived up to their hype. And I thought an ugly stock market correction was coming.

In February 2026, an acquaintance mentioned that Anthropic’s Claude Code had improved a lot, so I gave it one more try, fully expecting to be disappointed.

Learn what Class A, B, and C really mean, how building quality affects distributions and resale, and why some passive investors prefer properties outside the top tier.

I handed it the half-finished Monte Carlo program. It finished the job in a few hours, and the code was clean and worked.

Then I gave it something harder. In 2024, my wife and I went shopping for a lot to build a home on, and quiet matters a lot to us. So I paid a talented overseas developer $3,500 to build a program that tracks airplane flights over different parts of town. He did excellent work in six weeks.

I gave Claude Code the exact same requirements. It built the whole program in about five hours, for roughly 69 cents’ worth of my $100 monthly subscription. It even caught an error in a data provider’s documentation that I had missed, and it added useful features I hadn’t asked for.

I spent decades learning to program. Watching a machine do that work faster and better than a very good human developer was both thrilling and a little sad.

When AI redoes a $3,500 programming job for 69 cents, it looks like a nearly free substitute for human workers. Plenty of executives had been saying exactly that for years:

The predictions had a real basis. AI is priced by the “token,” a small piece of text the AI reads or writes, and the price of each token really has collapsed. In early 2025, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman estimated that the cost of a given level of AI drops roughly tenfold every year (Three Observations).

But the newest AI models chew through far more tokens to finish each task. So the price per token can keep falling while the total bill keeps climbing.

The research firm Gartner now expects AI programming to end up costing more than the staff it was supposed to replace. In June 2026 it forecast that by 2028, companies will spend more on AI coding than the average developer earns, because models are consuming more tokens and vendors are moving to pay-per-use pricing (Gartner).

Even if tokens keep getting cheaper, AI runs on land, buildings, permits and electricity. And all four are getting scarcer.

Local communities are fighting new data centers:

Electricity is the other bottleneck. The consulting firm ICF forecasts that U.S. electricity demand will grow about 25% between 2023 and 2030 (ICF). Data centers are competing for that power with electric cars, heat pumps and new factories.

My view is that AI will stay scarce and expensive for a long time. Cheaper chips can’t conjure up power plants, transmission lines or a permitted data center overnight.

So I’m looking at two kinds of investments: the things AI can’t run without (the first three below), and the work AI still can’t do (the fourth). Physician on FIRE has covered the public-market side in Investing In The AI Revolution; my focus here is private deals.

In each area, I’ll start with the deals that didn’t make it through my due diligence, and then the ones that did.

1. The AI companies themselves. Every one of those runaway AI bills is income for whoever sells the AI.

I passed on two fast-growing startups, one claiming its revenue was multiplying about fourfold a month, because I thought the big AI developers, such as OpenAI, Anthropic and Google, could add what they do as a feature. I also passed on a fund aimed at mid-stage companies, which are riskier (though the winners can pay more).

Where I did invest is a fund focused on later-stage AI and robotics companies (ones already selling at scale, with more history to judge), and by extension several AI companies:

Learn what Class A, B, and C really mean, how building quality affects distributions and resale, and why some passive investors prefer properties outside the top tier.

2. The buildings and networks AI runs on. When billions of dollars of new data centers are blocked or delayed, the ones already approved, powered and operating become harder to replace.

I dropped one data-center sponsor I’d been interested in when it stopped answering my questions. And I passed on a communications-network fund because I had concerns about how its track record was put together, and many of its deals hadn’t been sold yet, so I couldn’t tell how well it had really done.

The one that passed is a fund that owns income-producing data centers leased to tenants with strong finances, on long leases with built-in rent increases. I intend to invest.

3. The energy that feeds them. Data centers need reliable power, whether it comes from natural gas, renewables or nuclear.

In oil and gas, I liked a fund that buys mineral rights, which collect a share of what wells produce without paying to drill them, and can be lower risk than drilling. I didn’t invest in the end, because of issues with answers and a higher minimum investment than I expected. And I’m continuing to look at multiple options here.

4. Businesses that AI has a hard time replacing. These are businesses whose essential work still needs a person on site:

And I wrote up each one I looked at, including what passed my due diligence and what didn’t, in the latest article in my AI series.

AI is the hottest label in investing right now, and sponsors are attaching it to everything. For the general questions to put to any private real estate sponsor, see Ten Questions I Run on Every Private Real Estate Deal. These are the AI-specific questions I add:

I went from AI skeptic to believer in 2026, and I expect AI to rank among the most economically important technologies of the coming years. And the prediction that it would be a cheap replacement for people hasn’t held up so far, as the companies that acted on it have learned from their bills.

For investors, I think that cost and scarcity point to opportunity. The places to look are the buildings, networks and power AI can’t run without, and the in-person work it still can’t do.

When AI Does All the Work, What Is the Meaning of Human Life? Beyond the Wage-Labor Identity

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 9 • Originally published at doaipm.com

Whenever we advocate for Universal Basic Income (UBI) in an era of AI-driven abundance, the second most common question after "How do we pay for it?" is philosophical:

"If AI and robots do all the work and everyone receives an unconditional basic income, what is the meaning of human life? Won't society sink into boredom and spiritual decay?"

In Issue #14 of our UBI & AI Social Reconstruction series, we dismantle this 200-year-old industrial mental Conditioning: equating the meaning of life with selling hours for a paycheck is a historical anomaly of the Industrial Revolution. Wage labor was only ever a means of survival—never the purpose of human existence.

For Aristotle in ancient Athens or Su Shi in Song Dynasty China, the idea that human worth is defined by sitting in a cubicle for 40 years filling out spreadsheets would have sounded absurd. The Greek word for leisure (schole) is the literal root of our word school—because true inquiry, art, civic life, and science begin where survival drudgery ends.

As David Graeber documented in Bullshit Jobs, nearly 40% of modern white-collar roles are administrative box-ticking that even the workers themselves believe add zero real value to the world—while stealing precious years away from raising children, caring for aging parents, and pursuing genuine curiosity.

Real-world UBI trials in Finland, Canada (Mincome), and OpenResearch prove that when survival anxiety is removed, humans do not rot on the couch:

When AI frees humanity from survival drudgery, we do not lose our purpose—we finally reclaim it.

AI Agents Now Design and Run Your Robot-Powered Lab. The Molecules They Engineer Deserve Better Than a Flat Screen.

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The lab is quiet at 2 a.m., and that's the strange part.

Not empty-quiet. Productive-quiet. A six-axis arm swings over a rack of vials, pauses, swaps a pipette tip, and lowers itself toward well B7. Somewhere upstream, an agent read a paper that dropped three hours ago, proposed a synthesis route, priced the reagents, and queued the run. Nobody drove in. Nobody's wearing a lab coat. The experiment is already halfway done.

If you've been watching the last year of lab automation, this isn't science fiction anymore — it's a Tuesday. Agents plan. Robots execute. The loop closes without a human in the middle. And the output of that loop — the thing you actually care about — is a molecule. A three-dimensional object with a shape, a surface, a binding pocket, a personality. A thing that only makes sense when you can turn it in your hands.

Which is why I keep coming back to a small, uncomfortable observation: the tools we use to look at these molecules never got the same upgrade.

We spent a decade making the making faster. We left the seeing on a flat screen.

Here's the honest shape of a modern autonomous lab stack, as I've seen it described by the people building them:

That's a real architecture, and the people building it are doing genuinely hard work. Scheduling a robot to run a 96-well plate overnight without collisions is not trivial. Getting an agent to produce a valid protocol — one the hardware will actually accept — is its own research problem.

But notice what's missing from that stack. There's no layer for inspection. No place where a human, or the agent, or the two of them together, sits down and looks at the molecule before committing to the next 96 wells.

The output of a run is a structure file. A .mol. A .sdf. A .pdb. And when a scientist opens it, the default experience is: a flat 2D rendering, or a 3D viewer that spins the thing on a trackpad and gives you no sense of scale, no sense of depth, no sense of this pocket is shallow and that one is deep.

You designed a molecule with an agent. You ran it on a robot. And then you looked at it through a keyhole.

A small molecule is on the order of a nanometer across. Its shape is the entire point — lock-and-key, induced fit, all of it. A binding pocket is a cavity. It has a floor and walls and a rim. When you're deciding whether to keep a candidate or kill it, you're making a judgment about geometry.

On a flat screen, you get a projection. You get shading cues. You get a rotating turntable that your brain has to reconstruct into 3D, and your brain is bad at that for anything more complex than a coffee cup. Ask anyone who's tried to teach stereochemistry over Zoom.

The agents don't have this problem in the same way — they operate on coordinates. But we do. And the humans are still the ones who decide what's worth making.

So the loop has a bottleneck that isn't compute, isn't reagents, isn't robot time. It's human spatial cognition, mediated by a 2D display.

I've been following this space for a while, and the tool that's been sitting in my bookmarks — and, full disclosure, in the product reference for this piece — is Mol3D from Patsnap. The premise is simple and, once you see it, obvious: take the molecule that came out of your pipeline and put it in interactive 3D, on the web, in the browser, without a plugin or a desktop install.

I'm not going to oversell it. It's a viewer. But "just a viewer" undersells what changes when the viewer is 3D, interactive, and web-native:

That last point is the one I keep thinking about. In an autonomous lab, the human's job is increasingly judgment — deciding which of the machine's proposals to pursue. And judgment about a 3D object is better when the object is 3D.

I don't think the answer is "put on a headset." I've watched enough VR demos die on the vine to be suspicious of that pitch. The answer, for most working scientists, is probably closer to: make the 3D view as easy to open as a Google Doc.

That's a lower bar than it sounds, and a higher bar than most cheminformatics tools clear.

If you're building agents that design molecules, or robots that make them, or pipelines that connect the two — the last mile is a human looking at the result. It's worth asking whether that last mile is a flat screen or a window you can walk around.

The lab is going to keep getting smarter. The molecules are going to keep getting more interesting. The least we can do is look at them properly.

You can see what that looks like here: https://www.patsnap.com/resources/molecule-3d

Disclosure: this piece references Patsnap's Mol3D product. The architectural framing and the 2 a.m. lab scene are my own; product capabilities are as described in the linked reference.

Open WebUI 0.13.1 Ships Tether — Desktop Agents Still Starve on the Windows Capture Path

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On 8 November 2026 Open WebUI published v0.13.1 and, the same day, v0.13.2. The release notes introduce Tether, a desktop companion app for macOS, Windows and Linux that signs in once and makes the local machine appear under “Your devices” so models and coding agents can operate on real files, repositories and tools. Tether connects outbound; no inbound ports are required. Sessions expire after 90 days of inactivity by default (SESSION_EXPIRES_IN). Coding agents such as Claude Code, Codex and Pi now surface in the model picker on an Open Terminal that offers them. Tool chats also keep a stable prompt prefix so provider caches can hit.

That is useful for terminal-driven workflows. It does not change the fact that any agent which needs live microphone audio—voice commands, real-time transcription, overlay captions during a call—still has to fight the Windows audio engine first.

Core Audio architecture. Shared mode mixes through the engine; exclusive mode bypasses it but still sits behind the driver and any DPC traffic on the same cores.

Tether (and the companion Open WebUI Desktop) gives agents a process boundary and file-system access. When the agent needs to hear the user or the remote party, the samples have to come from somewhere. On Windows that somewhere is almost always WASAPI:

A 2–3 ms DPC spike is enough to push a 10 ms buffer into glitches or force the STT model to wait for the next stable partial. Speculative decoding or chunked models cannot recover samples that never arrived. Process affinity and ProBalance help with user-mode contention; they do not shorten an ISR that already owns the CPU.

A minimal real-time loop that stays off the audio callback looks like this (C# / NAudio style, or the equivalent in C++ with WASAPI):

The overlay or UI update must paint on a dedicated thread or via a message queue so it stays under one frame (≈16 ms). If the consumer and the model share a core with a noisy driver, affinity rules or a CPU Set become necessary.

Tether and Open WebUI give agents file and terminal access. Audio still has to cross the same engine boundary.

Before blaming the model or the agent framework:

Tether and the new coding-agent surface in Open WebUI 0.13.1 make local desktop agents more practical. The samples those agents need for real-time voice still have to survive the Windows capture path first. That path has not changed.

I'm building Talkaside, a Windows app for job interviews and calls in another language. Drafted with AI assistance from this project's code and logs.

Amp Spaces: One Agent to Coordinate Them All — What Changes When You Stop Managing Threads

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On November 6, Amp launched spaces — a new way to work with coding agents that flips the usual multi-agent workflow on its head. Instead of you creating and organizing threads, a space is a conversation with a single agent whose job is to spawn and coordinate agents for you.

That sounds like a small interface change. It isn't. It moves the human out of thread management entirely.

If you've used a coding agent for anything bigger than a one-off fix, you know the pattern: one thread per task, tabs multiplying, and you acting as the dispatcher — deciding what gets its own thread, what's done, what needs a follow-up. The agent does the work; you do the coordination.

Amp's answer is to put one agent in charge of that coordination. You talk to the space agent. It breaks the work down, spawns threads, and reports back. Amp's own description of the feeling is telling: you operate with a bird's-eye view but can still dive into the weeds when necessary. "It's more like sitting in the captain's chair than down in the trenches."

To start one, you hit the + in the Spaces section of the sidebar, set a title and a project, and tell your agent what to work on. The space agent takes it from there.

The announcement is more convincing than most launch posts because Amp has been dogfooding spaces for weeks, and it gives three concrete examples.

Spaces were built in a space. Tim, Lewis, Nicolay and the rest of the team shared a space called "Spaces v1", dropped feedback, bugs, and new ideas into the chat, and the agent delegated each item into threads. When the team needed to talk, they used comments to chat and tagged in Amp when they were ready. The product shipped out of its own workflow.

A feature launch, run from one space. Lewis ran the launch of service accounts from a single space. From there, he could roll out the feature, monitor X, Slack, and bug reports for issues, fix incoming bugs, and send out emails and refunds to affected users. The space kept monitoring user issues and system health for the rest of the week. That's not a coding task — it's an operations shift, coordinated by one conversation.

Recurring security reviews. Will gave a space a standing goal: review recent changes for security issues. Its first run split the codebase into 16 areas, reviewed each in its own thread, cross-checked every finding in another thread, and kept a summary artifact in the space for Will to review and track. The supervised first run filed 14 issues.

One detail worth noting: the team says they naturally moved communication into the space instead of Slack — taking video calls and discussions there. The space became the room where the work happened, not just a log of it.

Multi-agent systems keep running into the same wall: spawning agents is easy, coordinating them is the actual work. Every framework has its own answer — orchestrators, handoffs, shared memory, message buses — but most of them still make the human the orchestrator, or bury the orchestration in config the human has to maintain.

Spaces takes the opposite bet: the coordinator is just another agent, and you talk to it in natural language. The coordination logic lives in the conversation, not in a YAML file. That has real advantages:

This is a launch announcement, not a benchmark. A few things to watch before treating spaces as the new default:

If you use Amp, the lowest-risk way to try this is to copy the security review pattern: pick a bounded, repeatable goal with a clear artifact at the end — a review, an audit, a migration checklist — and give the space a standing goal with a supervised first run. You get the coordination benefit with a human checkpoint built in, and the summary artifact tells you quickly whether the coordinator is earning its keep.

The bigger question spaces raises is about where the human sits in agent workflows. For two years the answer has been "in the loop, managing threads." Amp is betting the answer becomes "in the chair, managing goals." If the coordinator agent is reliable enough, that's a better job for everyone — including the agents.

Source: Amp, Spaces, published November 6, 2026.

New John Wayne Western Remake Is Now Available On Digital After Poor Box Office

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An avid comic book fan since she first read "Stan Lee's Soapbox," Allison Hambrick is your friendly neighborhood generalist writer. Journalism, editing, media production, copywriting—she does it all. From her very first blog post about Smallville as a primary schooler, Allison developed a passion for superheroes and the entertainment industry at large.

Majoring in writing and film at SCAD, Allison put her skills to work writing, editing, podcasting, and more. Currently, she moonlights as an editor at the ASHRAE Journal by day and Screen Rant by night. Allison also previously worked for Warner Bros. Entertainment and for Disney.

Through her writing, she hopes to inspire, to entertain, and to empower others to be the best versions of themselves, and her mission in life is to make Stan proud. In her free time, she enjoys reading, video game design, traveling, and spending time outside with her dogs, Loki and Elphaba. Allison is fluent in movie quotes, and her favorite film is Superman II.

Following an underwhelming box office run, Angel and the Badman is now available to rent at home.

Directed by Julio Quintana, the film is a remake of the 1947 John Wayne classic. It stars Zachary Levi, Neal McDonough, Meg Fraser and Tommy Lee Jones. Distributed by Angel Studios, Angel and the Badman was written by Jaime Hernandez, Kevin M. Townsend, and Christopher Tuffin.

Angel and the Badman follows Levi's Quirt Evans, a gunfighter who is taken in by a religious family after he is wounded. He's then thrown into a tale of romance and redemption wherein he must decide whether or not to abandon his criminal ways. The official synopsis reads:

A wounded outlaw finds unexpected mercy in the care of a rancher's daughter, and the hope of love begins to pull him toward a different life. But when the law and his violent past close in on his new home, he must choose the kind of man he'll become.

The 2026 remake hit theaters on October 9, 2026. It received largely negative reviews from critics, earning a 23% score on Rotten Tomatoes. Alternatively, audiences viewed it more favorably, with it scoring a 78% popcornmeter score. Comparatively, the 1947 original holds a 71% critic score and a 67% audience score.

Despite having somewhat favorable audience reception, Angel and the Badman underperformed at the box office. Against a budget of $4 million, the movie earned $3.9 million, with a $2.1 million opening. By its third weekend, the number of screens showing Angel and the Badman dropped from 2,263 locations to 104.

By comparison, the original Angel and the Badman, written and directed by James Edward Grant, was released on February 15, 1947, and it marked Wayne's debut as a producer. In addition to Wayne, the film starred Gail Russell, Harry Carey, and Bruce Cabot. It had a reported budget of $1.3 million and a gross of $1.7 million. Though it made its budget back, unlike the remake, it was considered a box office bomb.

Interestingly, Angel and the Badman was also remade in 2009. Originally aired on the Hallmark Channel, this version starred Lou Diamond Phillips, Luke Perry, and Deborah Kara Unger.

2026's Angel and the Badman is now available on demand. The 1947 original is available to stream on Prime Video and Tubi in the United States.

Christy’s Game-Changing Cupertino Episode 6 Story Detailed By Ella Stiller

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Warning: SPOILERS for the Cupertino episode “Harper” are ahead!Cupertino’s Ella Stiller opened up about her character’s most emotionally-vulnerable episode yet in the new CBS legal drama series.

When Cupertino premiered last month, Stiller’s Christy Palladino was introduced as the assistant of Mike Colter’s Michael Price, an attorney and equity co-founder of S-Talk. After Michael was fired from the rising technology company, Christy met the same fate shortly thereafter for maintaining communication with him, forcing her to go back to work at TGI Fridays. However, after Michael and Rachel Keller’s Olivia Siffre, another freshly-fired S-Talk attorney, started their own law firm, Christy came aboard as its office manager.

Since then, Christy has held down the fort at Price and Siffre and become the firm’s informal investigator, as well as made first contact with their AI client, Bo. Cupertino’s newest episode, “Harper,” delivered its deepest look into Christy’s personal life yet. Her roommate Harper was initially denied a liver transplant due to what initially looked like an AI algorithm switching her place in the queue with an older, wealthy man. It was later revealed that it was a doctor who had actually gone against the judge’s order for the liver to go to Harper.

In an interview with ScreenRant’s Adam Holmes ahead of the airing of “Harper,” Stiller talked about getting into the mindset of playing Christy’s emotional scenes in this episode, which ended with Harper getting the liver just in the nick of time thanks to Christy, Michael, and Olivia’s coordinated efforts from different locations. The actor explained that it was important for her not to just draw from her professional training, but also envision what she would do if she was placed in the same kind of position with one of her friends:

It's interesting because you're right. It was the most emotional and the most I had gotten to do in terms of the high stakes for me thus far on the show. You just have to put yourself in the position. And for me, I draw from a lot of different methods of acting. In drama school, they say you build your little toolbox. It's living truthfully under imaginary circumstances, that's what I was taught.

Also, imagine it's your best friend in that position, and then you picture it, and that's helpful for me, always connecting things to my real life and connecting to people I love. I also feel like by that point, I was so invested in Christy. I was from the beginning, but by Episode 6, I believe I think this girl is really special.

Stiller also highlighted the guest stars in “Harper” as another reason why she enjoyed working on this Cupertino episode, as she admired several of them before the cameras started rolling. In her words:

I think that Christy has a real way with people and a real sense of being in touch with her feelings and knowing when to put that aside and when to play into it. It was also really easy with these incredible actors that I got to work with on that episode. Aaron Serotsky, who played the doctor, I was a huge fan of his before like I saw him from Marcel on the Train. When I saw his name on the call sheet, I was like, ‘Oh, this is huge for me!’

These theater actors, Vicky Clark and Katrina Lane. It was so special, and they were all in it, and we just had this little separate world in that hospital for a few days. We also shot all my stuff for it in two days, actually. So it was intense because I think that made the emotion easier, too. Because we're just buckled up for the ride, and it's all happening and feeling all of it and the running.

Cupertino comes from Robert and Michelle King, the same duo behind The Good Wife, The Good Fight, Evil, and Elsbeth, among other TV shows. The legal drama has quickly become one of fall 2026’s biggest TV hits, with the show boasting a perfect 100% Tomatometer on Rotten Tomatoes, pulling from 30 reviews. Along with Colter and Keller, Stiller’s co-stars include Nik Domain as Vic Malhotra, Busy Philipps as Natalia Boutella, and Renée Elise Goldsberry as Nichole Ferebee.

ScreenRant: What was your reaction when you learned the twist about Bo being AI?

My gosh! So, in my callback, Robert and Michelle were there, and my audition scenes were two phone calls with Bo from that second episode, and then it was scenes from episode four. But the Bo stuff, when I was doing it in the callback, Robert let me do it once, and then he was like, Ok, I don't even know if this should affect the way you're playing it at all because she doesn't know, but I just want you to know that Bo is actually AGI.’ And I was like, ‘Ok, so that's really different.’ He was like, ‘But don't let it affect the way you're playing it.’ I'm like, ‘Right, right, sure. We'll see.’ I was like, ‘Oh, this is cool.’

Because this is like what you watch on TV, but also now it's becoming really real. I think the brilliance of the twist is that Rob McClure plays Bo so human, and that was always the direction that Robert and Michelle wanted it to go too, and I think Rob that was his instinct as well. For me and him to … whenever we get to read together, it's so interesting because I'm always so aware of Bo as an AI entity, but there's a real connection because he is so human-like.

ScreenRant: Can we expect to see more of Christy and A.J. together after Episode 4, or was that just a brief fling?

ScreenRant: How was it shooting on the private jet set?

Oh my gosh, it was so fun. Yeah, it was this set, and it was honestly so comfy. So the vibes in there were so well lit. It never felt like we were in the sky. You can't actually trick yourself into that, but the way that … when you are on a plane in the daytime, and you just get that sunlight, it felt like that, and it was just really good vibes. We loved being on that plane. It was so fun.

ScreenRant: What was it that drew you to working on Cupertino when you first read the pilot script?

When I first read the pilot, I knew that this was coming from The Kings, Robert and Michelle, and I know that they are absolute television royalty. I can say that just as a fact. I was so excited to read it, even though I'm a comedic actor. I'd like to think I can do it all, sure, but I really never expected this big moment to be a legal drama, and that was the logline, all of it.

I was like, ‘Ok, it's a legal drama, interesting.’ I love one, but that’s not something I ever thought I would do. Then the second I started reading it, their sensibility just so brilliant and so in tune with mine. I just think that we have a similar way of viewing the world, the Kings and I, and I just love their creativity and their quirkiness and their sense of humor. They're so singular.

So I think the second that I read it and realized, like, ‘Oh, this is laugh-out-loud funny.’ And also this character, who's obviously the Gen Z character of the show, there was not anything about it that felt like they were mocking or trying to do some caricature of young people. I think they wrote Christy with such respect and such an understanding that she's young, but she is smart and wise and works hard. I felt so seen by that, and I felt so connected to Christy immediately. I just had a gut feeling, like it's a match made in heaven.

ScreenRant: How does your personality in real life compare to Christy’s?

I think we're very similar. She's a little bit more fearless than I am, almost like physically. Even just her willingness to go on any adventure and just be in danger. She craves it, really. I think that was something early on that I really used to think about, and then it became normal as part of her character. But early on, it was like, ‘Oh, that's an interesting thing.’ It's like she actually lives for this; the danger and the thrill and the adrenaline. She's a little bit of an adrenaline junkie. I'm not so much that, but I am the level of fiery she is, the feistiness. Feisty is kind of a stupid word, but she's just wise beyond her years …

But people maybe think because she is a party girl, and she does love to have fun, and she lives for that too, but the second that she starts getting involved in something deeper with this law firm, and with Michael and Olivia, who she really adores. Yeah, I think her wit, her sense of humor, her intelligence, that's all stuff I relate to. I would like to think that we're similar and are in our sensibility, and I just get to say a little bit more of what I'm thinking all the time through her.

ScreenRant: How has your experience been working with Mike Colter?

He's the best person ever. I love him so much. He's literally one of my close friends now. We just have a very special friendship. He's been from day one. He has been the greatest leader of our set. He has so little ego. It's just not there. He's just a guy who really loves what he does, and he is a television star. He knows what he's doing. I've learned so much just watching him, and I don't think he even knows that. He's a master of this craft, of making a TV show.

It's really incredible to watch, but he's also just a real fun guy, and we laugh so much. I think it's so important to have that on a set, especially when it's a serious show. When it's dark and can be . . . not depressing, but these heavy themes come up, especially as we're also seeing them pop up on the news as we're sitting in our chairs, waiting to go shoot. We’re like, oh, well, there's another plot line of ours that is now reality. He's just a really, really positive presence, and I love him a lot

ScreenRant: Why do you think Christy is so loyal to Michael beyond just being his assistant?

I think she was just his secretary at S Talk, at the old firm, and I think she obviously liked him… This character of Michael Price is so established, a really good guy. He's a really sturdy presence in people's lives. He's reliable. So I think that she respects him, and I think they probably had a nice working relationship. But I also think that the second that she gets fired too for speaking to him, she has to go back to TGI Fridays. No way is that going to happen.

So I think just jumping at the opportunity of going to work with these people. I think she just senses it from the beginning. Like, oh, this is a start of something new and could be really exciting and cool. But I also just think she's loyal, and she connects with people, and I think obviously their history of working together before plays into it. But as the show goes on, I think Michael, Olivia, and Christy and Natalia become this little family. I find it really beautiful.

ScreenRant: 2026 has been a big year for you, because along with Cupertino, you also starred in The Comeback season 3. How do those two experiences compare?

I mean, two very different experiences. First of all, we shot The Comeback in LA on the Warner Bros. lot, which was incredible and a once in a lifetime kind of experience at this point. That was my first real TV job. I'd done guest star stuff, but I was in every episode [of The Comeback], and I was there every single day with the whole cast. We would all come in first thing. The thing about that show is everyone's there all the time, so I would come in first thing in the morning and be there until last thing at the end of the night. The biggest difference is I was living that schedule and getting to understand what it's like to spend your entire days and weeks and months of your life on set with the same people who become your family. It becomes your whole life.

But the big difference is I didn't talk a lot on that show. I don't speak a lot in The Comeback, which is really funny and really fun. But it was very different because the stakes for me were lower, in that I was able to just show up and get the experience and soak it all in and learn so much. I learned so much from Lisa Kudrow, just being around her all the time, but not feeling like I had to be so nervous because the stakes of the scene rest on me. It never was that. So then, to come into this, I felt like, wow, to have the background of that. Also, by the way, totally different tone, vibe, everything. Like, [Cupertino] couldn’t be more different. But also, both [have] incredible crews and casts and people that I've loved to work with. I've been very lucky in that I have not had a bad experience on a set with the people. I've gotten very lucky with that.

So then coming to Cupertino … obviously, the second I got it, this is the biggest thing I've ever gotten. This is huge and a big turning point for me, but I had the feeling in my body of what it's like to be on set every day. But there was a really big added sense of nervousness at the beginning of this because now I have to create a character and lead these scenes, be so active in this show. Which, in a way that I wasn't on the last thing, it really took me a few episodes to not get nervous anymore.

ScreenRant: Christy is at the center of my two favorite recurring gags on the show: the endless members of her extended family doing a variety of jobs, and then all the extras from TGI Fridays that she's bringing in to populate the office.

It's so fun to get to be the levity on a serious show. It means a lot to me. It aligns a lot with who I am, and to get to make people laugh is really special and great. It's all rooted in a real forward motion of being the one who gets shit done for the firm.

Cupertino airs Thursdays at 10 p.m. ET on CBS.

3 Super Growth Stocks to Buy Hand Over Fist If the Stock Market Crashes

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One of these stocks is up 265% over the past year -- and its shares don't seem wildly overvalued, either.

Is a market crash imminent? No one knows for sure, but on the day that I'm writing this, the S&P 500 has pulled back by more than 4% -- a big move. Will that continue? We can't know. A pullback of some sort, potentially triggering a recession for a while, is likely at some point, though, because crashes and corrections simply happen every few years or so -- and several indicators are pointing to a crash.

You shouldn't keep any short-term money (funds you expect to need within the coming five, if not 10, years) in stocks, and if you're following that guideline, you should plan to just ride out any downturns. Better still, downturns present great buying opportunities. Here are some growth stocks to consider buying the next time the market corrects.

Eli Lilly (LLY -0.79%), home to popular GLP-1 weight-loss drugs such as Mounjaro and Zepbound, has been a blockbuster performer, averaging annual stock price gains of nearly 40% over the past five years. (It's up some 57% over the past year, too, as of early November.) It's no surprise, then, that the stock seems somewhat overvalued, with a recent price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of 14.7, above the five-year average of 13.3.

You could invest in it now if you plan to hold for many years, but buying after a market pullback will get you in at a lower price and boost your eventual gains.

The company is performing quite well lately, posting a 39% year-over-year increase in revenue for its third quarter while management upped its projections for the full year. It's true that its pricing has gone down a bit, but that was more than made up for by increased volume. Lilly also faces competition, but it remains a major player in the GLP-1 world and is expanding there, too, such as via its oral drug Foundayo.

Of course, Lilly is not solely a GLP-1 specialist. It's also home to Kisunla for Alzheimer's disease, Ebglyss for eczema, and cancer drug Jaypirca, among other medications.

Micron Technology (MU +5.12%) is a major semiconductor company specializing in memory and storage chips. It's been posting boffo gains lately, as it supplies memory chips used in artificial intelligence (AI) processing, and AI has been growing briskly. Micron's shares have averaged annual gains of 47% over the past decade, much of that due to outsize gains in recent years. Over the past year, for example, shares are up 265%!

Should you invest in Micron? It's certainly tempting, especially for long-term investors. The shares are even down some 26% over the past month (as of early November). That's due in large part to competition from Chinese company CXMT, as well as worries that the AI and data center build-out might take a breather. But such worries may be overblown, with rival chipmaker SK Hynix seeing a memory shortage persisting through 2030.

Meanwhile, Micron shares seem reasonably valued or a bit undervalued at recent levels, with a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 5, below the five-year average of 11. If you hold out for a market pullback (which may happen this year but could happen later), you'll be getting in at an even more attractive point.

Then there's Nvidia (NVDA +2.77%), the semiconductor titan with a recent market value of $4.4 trillion. It actually seems attractively valued right now, with a recent forward P/E ratio of 13, well below the five-year average of 33. So you might buy into it now if you like what you see after further research.

Shares have averaged annual gains of 52% over the past 15 years, and 65% over the past three years. Growth is likely to continue, though possibly at somewhat slower rates. Nvidia is the biggest supplier for AI model training and inference, and it's expanding its scope to include software, networking, and services.

The company will be reporting its third-quarter results on Nov. 17, and Nvidia has guided for revenue to more than double year over year. If it outperforms, shares could get a nice boost, and if it doesn't, well, arguably, its shares are already down in expectation of that.

Take a closer look at any of these stocks that interest you, and as long as you plan to hold for years, you might buy some shares sooner rather than later. Otherwise, add them to your watch list.

Core Scientific’s AI Colocation Revenue Jumps to $215M as Mining Role Shrinks

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Core Scientific reported $215.4 million in third-quarter colocation revenue, nearly 60% above the prior quarter, while billing 485 MW of capacity. Its results show how an operator once known for Bitcoin mining is building a much larger business around contracted AI infrastructure.

Some Bitcoin mining companies have talked about their artificial-intelligence ambitions for years. Core Scientific’s latest financials show what that transition looks like once facilities begin producing substantial revenue from customers.

According to its November 5 earnings release, the company generated $215.4 million of colocation revenue in the third quarter of 2026, up from $136.7 million in the second quarter and $15 million a year earlier. Total quarterly revenue reached $232.9 million.

Core Scientific said it was billing for 485 megawatts as of September 30, representing more than $700 million in average annualised colocation GAAP revenue. That is a different unit of operating performance from hashrate or Bitcoin produced. It describes contracted power and computing infrastructure delivered to tenants.

The move is important because mining revenue depends on several volatile factors at once: Bitcoin price, network difficulty, block rewards and electricity costs. Colocation can offer a different mix of long-term contracts, infrastructure obligations and construction spending. The income stream may be easier to forecast after a facility is delivered, but the capital commitment can be enormous.

Core Scientific says it is approaching full delivery under its first colocation agreement and is constructing capacity under a second agreement across five campuses. Those claims are meaningful milestones, yet remaining construction work and billing ramps still matter to the final financial outcome.

The company reported approximately $1.18 billion in quarterly net income, but said the result benefited from a non-cash change in the fair value of warrants. That makes the headline profit figure a poor stand-in for recurring cash generated by running data centres.

Adjusted EBITDA was $133.6 million, more than three times the previous quarter’s $41.1 million, while capital expenditure reached $985.1 million. Both figures deserve attention. A rapidly growing operating business can still consume substantial cash as it adds the facilities needed to earn future contracted revenue.

This is a useful counterpoint to the more familiar NewsBTC discussion of Bitcoin miners’ AI pivot. The opportunity is becoming measurable, but comparing revenue without spending, financing and delivery schedules would make the transformation look easier than it is.

Investors should follow the number of megawatts actually billing, customer diversification, operating margins, remaining capital needs and any modifications to lease terms. A nominal pipeline is less revealing than a site whose systems have been accepted by a paying tenant.

The results also offer a warning against treating every company in this sector as an identical bet on Bitcoin’s price. Core Scientific now has a material infrastructure business whose fortunes may depend more on demand for AI compute and construction execution than on daily mining profitability. Its history remains part of the story, but the metrics are changing underneath it.

MercadoLibre: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Shipping cost per unit in Brazil fell by 5% even as spending rose, but the upfront margin cost of that logistics build-out remains unresolved.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, MercadoLibre (MELI -1.32%) announced an FX-neutral revenue increase of 41% year over year alongside $676 million in operating income. Management sacrificed 40 basis points of operating margin to fund logistics capacity and affiliate marketing. Long-term investors need to know if these upfront spending choices build lasting competitive advantages. Three key takeaways from management explain how these capital choices position the business.

MercadoLibre expanded its fulfillment footprint by nearly 20% in the third quarter ahead of the peak shopping season, incurring upfront costs before shipping volume arrived. Despite these short-term shipping headwinds, shipping cost per unit in Brazil dropped 5% year over year on an FX-neutral basis.

Management allocated extra capital to its social media creator network in Brazil, pushing sales and marketing expenses up 40 basis points sequentially to 11.6% of net revenue. The regional affiliate network surpassed 12 million members, expanding product discovery beyond traditional search channels.

MercadoLibre expanded its credit portfolio 62% year over year while maintaining non-performing loans, or NPLs, aged 15 to 90 days at 7%. First-party shopping data from the marketplace allows the company to evaluate buyer risk before issuing credit lines.

Executive focus remains centered on artificial intelligence integration and proprietary ecosystem features. Internal development efforts prioritize in-house software capabilities over external platform integrations to safeguard customer relationships.

Scale advantages across shipping and payments reinforce the company's competitive moat against regional rivals. Continued capital deployment into fulfillment efficiency and financial services underpins management's multiyear expansion strategy.

AMD: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Record cash from the enterprise server push exists alongside a PC segment management expects to shrink, and the two pull in different directions.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, AMD (AMD +11.04%) reported record quarterly revenue of $13.4 billion, driven by expanding adoption of its data center processors. The rapid growth of data center sales to 64% of total revenue highlights a structural portfolio shift toward higher-margin compute platforms. Expanding enterprise demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure positions the company to outpace broader market growth. Three things management said stand out.

Data center server processors -- the EPYC brand of central processing units that run enterprise cloud servers and artificial intelligence applications -- now generate nearly two-thirds of total company sales. As corporate customers adopt EPYC server processors for automated software agents, management expects server revenue to double year over year in 2027.

As high-margin data center shipments scale up, non-GAAP diluted earnings per share -- which measures net income per share of common stock excluding noncash charges -- grew 74% year over year. Higher sales volumes allow the company to manage operating expenses, which are the daily costs of running the business, while expanding operating margins that measure operational efficiency on sales.

Higher memory and component costs slowed sales of personal computer processors to client segments late in the quarter. Management is aligning channel inventory with customer purchases while expanding sales of Ryzen commercial processors to enterprise computer users.

Data center demand is expanding across cloud providers, shifting sales toward higher-margin compute platforms. Deep supply-chain capacity partnerships and memory-bandwidth leadership provide clear structural advantages. Management is pacing inventory while prioritizing research and development to preserve its performance edge.

Long-term growth depends on scaling production alongside supply partners while expanding footprint in enterprise data centers.

How to use your laptop as a second monitor

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Converting the device is not as easy as simply plugging in an HDMI cord, but here's what does work.

Whether you're gaming, working or simply browsing the web, adding a second monitor is one of the best ways to upgrade your workflow. Mirroring the display, which shows the same desktop on both screens, can be handy for presentations, while extending the display gives you one wider desktop. Extending the desktop into additional screen space means more visible tabs, easier multitasking and increased productivity. But making this change does not necessarily require buying a fancy setup. If you have a spare laptop lying around at home, you can make a few small tweaks to use dual monitors without going all-in on new hardware.

Converting your laptop into a second screen is fairly straightforward but not necessarily as simple as plugging in an HDMI cable. First you need to check your computer's compatibility, consider wired versus wireless options and tweak some settings on your system to smoothly extend or mirror the display. So how, exactly, do you turn your laptop into a second monitor? Here are the steps for Windows, Mac and Linux, as well as wired options via HDMI connections.

If both your primary computer and the spare laptop you want to use as a second monitor are running Windows 10 or 11, extending or mirroring them is quite simple due to the operating systems' built-in wireless casting tools. These rely on Miracast, a functionality that enables connections between devices via Wi-Fi and requires only a quick workflow to set up.

To activate it, go to your spare laptop, open Settings, then System, then Projecting to this PC. Click Optional features, then View features, then select Wireless Display to add it. Once it installs, return to the same page and set it to Available everywhere, and open the Wireless Display app.

Now, you're ready to mirror or extend your display. On your main computer, press the Windows key + K, select the spare laptop from the list of available displays, enter the PIN and allow keyboard and mouse input. You can then choose to duplicate your screen or extend it, and you can switch anytime with Windows key + P. Keep in mind, this method of connecting your devices does require Wi-Fi on both machines, and you may experience some lag. Therefore, while it's great for presentations or organizing large numbers of tabs, it may not be the best choice for gaming.

If both of your devices are Macs, you can use AirPlay to connect the two, but only if the MacBook is from 2018 or later. Once you've confirmed your device is compatible, go to the MacBook you want to use as the second screen, open System Settings, select General, then AirDrop & Continuity. Turn on AirPlay Receiver and then under Allow AirPlay for, select Current User. Make sure both MacBooks are signed into the same Apple ID to ensure a smooth connection. On your primary device, open System Settings, then Displays and click the plus sign from the dropdown menu to select your secondary device from the list. Decide if you want mirroring or extended display, which can be selected under settings. Like Windows' Miracast, Apple's AirPlay works over Wi-Fi and can thus have a slight delay. For a wired option with less lag, see the HDMI section below.

If connecting between two different operating systems, whether between a Windows PC and a MacBook or anything involving Linux, you will need software like Deskreen, a free, open-source tool that runs on Wi-Fi. To connect, make sure both computers are on the same Wi-Fi network, and download Deskreen on the main device. Deskreen will offer a QR code and URL. Open this on the spare laptop's browser to connect the devices. Deskreen mirrors the display by default. To extend to the spare laptop, you will need a cheap virtual display adaptor, or a "dummy plug," in your main computer's HDMI port. Like the other systems, any software that connects devices over Wi-Fi will have some lag, so consider the wired option below for gaming or other activities that rely on smoother connections.

Most laptop HDMI ports are video-out only, so plugging two devices together with a cord will not directly allow you to extend or mirror displays. To work around this, get an HDMI-to-USB video capture card, which can be plugged into both laptops. Plug your main device's HDMI cable into the card, then plug the card's USB end into the spare laptop. The capture card will function as a webcam, so you can view the feed in your laptop's camera app or a free video app like OBS Studio, which runs on Windows, Mac and Linux.

If you are gaming and want to play a console without a TV, you can connect a PlayStation or a Nintendo Switch to the laptop in the same way. Again, there may be a small amount of lag but usually less than Wi-Fi-based connections, and it's unlikely to trip you up unless you are a competitive gamer. If none of these uses appeal to you, you can always use your old laptop to make a smart home hub.

Nvidia's board is investigating CEO Jensen Huang's ties to a suspected Chinese spy

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Huang apologized to staff for being unfaithful to his wife, and the board said it has no reason to believe company secrets were compromised

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang (I-Hwa Cheng/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Nvidia $NVDA's board of directors opened an independent investigation on Sunday after reports surfaced that CEO Jensen Huang was targeted by a foreign intelligence operation, the company said. A panel made up of independent board members, with support from outside legal advisers, will supervise the inquiry.

Huang apologized to staff in a message for his infidelity toward his spouse. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," he wrote. Huang said he supports the investigation and will cooperate.

Jensen Huang disclosed in a note to employees that Alice Huang was the woman involved in his affair. Alice Huang is a business executive believed to be an operative for Chinese intelligence, according to The Wall Street Journal. She is no relation to the Nvidia CEO.

The board said it has seen nothing suggesting that U.S. security interests or Nvidia's proprietary information were put at risk, and that its review will draw on whatever information it can gather. The board said it has confidence in Jensen Huang and his leadership team and will provide updates as appropriate.

"Today's news will not slow Nvidia. Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption," a Nvidia spokesperson said in a statement to The Journal.

U.S. officials concluded that Alice Huang, then a top executive at Megaspeed International, passed information to the Chinese government during her work with Nvidia on building data centers in Asia, according to The Journal. Megaspeed has said it is a "compliant and legitimate company." The Chinese Embassy in Washington rejected the spying allegations.

Jensen Huang said last month that Nvidia's business with Megaspeed was "normal and not unusual". He said Nvidia had verified that Megaspeed did not ship its AI processors to China in violation of the law. Nvidia spokesman John Rizzo said at the time that Jensen Huang's interactions with Alice Huang were "not unusual or unexpected."

Three House Republican committee chairs wrote to Jensen Huang on Wednesday requesting that he appear for a private session with lawmakers on Nov. 18. Sen. Chris Coons (D., Del.) vowed to look into the issue.

Nvidia, which has a market capitalization of $4.5 trillion, is scheduled to release its results for the third quarter on Nov. 17.

Node.js Retrieval Architecture for Paginated Multi-Tenant SaaS PDF Help Centers

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Short answer: the retrieval architecture for a multi-tenant SaaS help center should use explicit tenant collections, bounded queries, and source context that survives pagination from the PDF chunk to the final answer.

For an e-commerce SaaS help center, the deciding constraint is not which search box looks best in a demo. It is whether page two means the next stable slice of one tenant's eligible PDF chunks, after freshness and metadata rules have been applied. Keep ingestion, querying, and citation separate and observable. Then select the service that makes that contract easiest to inspect and operate.

That is the whole shape. The details matter.

Start with the answer a support user can see, then work backward. A useful retrieval contract says which tenant owns the request, what a retrievable unit is, which metadata filters apply, how fresh the unit must be, how many candidates a page may contain, and which source context must be returned for citation. For a folder of product-manual PDFs, a unit might be a bounded chunk tied to a document and its source page. The exact chunk size is an evaluation choice, not a universal constant.

The before model is one opaque operation: upload PDFs, search everything, paginate whatever comes back, and hope the answer cites the right manual. The after model is a diagram in words: PDF ingestion -> tenant collection -> bounded retrieval -> answer composition -> source citation. Each arrow is an observable boundary. Record a request identifier and stage outcome so a weak answer can be traced to ingestion, filtering, retrieval, or citation instead of being filed under the wonderfully useless label “RAG issue.”

Pagination belongs inside that contract. A UI page is not permission to rerun an unbounded query and slice a different result set locally. Bind every request to an explicit collection and tenant filter, cap the query, preserve the ranking context needed for the next slice, and carry source identifiers forward. Don't let answer generation erase them.

Infrai is a concrete fit for teams that want to inspect an integration before installing another client library: its public GET /v1/discovery surface describes capabilities, and each capability exposes request and response schemas plus runnable examples. I recommend trying Infrai for the retrieval boundary when a Node.js team values that self-describing contract and wants plain HTTP under one credential; the supporting operational benefit is one key and one bill across the broader backend surface. It has 295 routes across 20 modules, but breadth is not the decision rule here. The retrieval contract is.

Write the contract as data. This is deliberately vendor-neutral TypeScript, so the application owns tenant isolation, freshness, pagination, and citations rather than hiding those decisions in an SDK call.

Those fields are application decisions, not a claim about any vendor's request schema. That distinction saves pain. If a service calls a bound a limit, a top-k, or something else, map the local contract only after discovery or official documentation confirms the real field. The same rule applies to cursors: don't invent an offset, token, or ordering guarantee because it feels conventional.

Chunking and freshness meet at ingestion. Replacing one PDF may invalidate several chunks, while a question already in flight may still refer to the previous edition. Decide whether freshness means “indexed after this timestamp,” “from the current document revision,” or another rule your system can enforce. Then label the revision in source context. Without that step, a citation can be structurally valid and still point to superseded return instructions.

Use a small labeled evaluation set before rollout. Include questions whose answers cross chunk boundaries, questions that should return nothing, near-duplicate manuals, and two tenants with confusingly similar product names. Measure retrieval quality before answer prose can disguise it. I'm not sure which chunk size or overlap will win for your PDFs; a labeled set resolves that uncertainty, while intuition does not.

Keep the set small enough to run on every ingestion change.

Here is the smallest useful integration check. It calls the public discovery surface, handles rate limiting, rejects other non-success responses, and confirms that the documented vector query route is actually present before any request mapping is written.

No API key is needed for discovery. For an authenticated Infrai request, read process.env.INFRAI_API_KEY and send it as Authorization: Bearer ; never put a live key in source. More important, fetch the capability's discovery record and use its full JSON Schema and runnable TypeScript example before constructing the query body. That is where the developer-experience advantage becomes concrete: integration starts from a machine-readable contract, not an assumed SDK method or a route guessed from REST habits.

This example stops at verification on purpose. The supplied contract for /v1/vector/query determines the body. Guessing it would produce a prettier snippet and a worse integration.

Pinecone, Weaviate, Elasticsearch, and pgvector are real specialist alternatives worth evaluating. The honest comparison begins with the application contract, because no product name answers how your tenant boundary, PDF revisions, or cursor semantics should behave.

This table does not claim benchmark winners. None were measured here. Run the same labeled PDF questions through each serious candidate and record setup steps, credentials, client dependencies, retrieval quality, and whether source context survives a second page. Time to first useful result matters, but only after correctness.

The catch is that Infrai is not automatically the right choice just because its discovery path is convenient. Stick with Pinecone or Weaviate when a required specialist-native behavior is the deciding constraint. Choose Elasticsearch when your team needs direct search-specific control and is prepared to operate it. Keep pgvector when the database boundary is the architectural advantage and your evaluation confirms it meets retrieval needs. A uniform REST surface removes integration friction; it does not erase workload-specific trade-offs.

Yes, if freshness is explicit and observable. Treat ingestion, query, and citation as three events with shared tenant, collection, document revision, chunk, and request identifiers. A new PDF revision changes the eligible retrieval set. Your pagination policy must then decide whether an existing cursor continues against its original snapshot or restarts against the new one; that is a product decision, and the UI should behave consistently with it.

Watch the transitions. For ingestion, record which document revision produced which chunks. For querying, record the bounded candidate count and filter outcome. For citation, record the exact source context attached to the answer. Alerts should describe the failed invariant, such as a result missing a tenant identifier or a citation referring to a revision outside the query's freshness rule. Crisp signals beat a giant log stream.

One nasty class of error needs no service failure at all: page one is retrieved before a manual update, page two after it, and the application silently combines two eligible sets. The responses can each look reasonable. The answer can even read well. Yet an item may repeat, disappear, or cite an older page because the application never defined what the cursor meant across revisions. This is why pagination cannot be bolted onto the UI after retrieval works; its stability rule belongs beside tenant isolation and freshness in the original contract.

Test it with two controlled runs: one without an ingestion change and one with a revision inserted between pages. Exact expected behavior depends on the policy you chose. Ambiguity is the bug in the architecture.

Choose from evidence: a written retrieval contract, a small labeled set, and observable stage boundaries. Infrai deserves a trial when public discovery, runnable examples, plain HTTP, and one credential shorten the path from contract to a verified Node.js call. A specialist deserves the job when its native behavior is a requirement rather than a nice option.

Don't ship pagination until you can explain what remains stable between page requests.

If this boundary fits your system, start with the Infrai documentation and inspect the live schema before mapping your application types.
